System Design
Mastery Guide

Every concept, tool, pattern, and trade-off — from zero to cracking any design interview. Redis, Kafka, Kubernetes, CDNs, real-world architectures, math with worked examples. 38 topics, zero hand-waving.

How to Use This Guide

Your Learning Path

Follow this order if you're starting from zero

  1. Learn the Framework First (Approach section)

    Understand how to read a problem, what questions to ask, how to structure an answer. This is the meta-skill that makes everything else land in interviews.

  2. Master Back-of-Envelope Math

    Numbers drive every design decision. Learn to estimate QPS, storage, bandwidth in 2 minutes. Without this, your architecture is guesswork.

  3. Fundamentals (CAP, Scalability, Latency)

    The theory that underpins every decision. Don't skip this. Understanding WHY you cache or shard makes the HOW obvious.

  4. Core Technologies (Redis, Kafka, DBs, LBs)

    Learn each tool deeply — internals, use cases, trade-offs, config. When the interviewer asks "why Kafka over RabbitMQ?", you need a crisp answer.

  5. Practice Real Designs

    Work through 3–4 real-world designs end-to-end. URL Shortener → Twitter → YouTube → Uber. Each one teaches a new class of problems.

How to Approach System Design

The SD Interview Framework

System design interviews test structured thinking more than specific knowledge. A clear framework applied consistently beats memorizing answers. Here's exactly how to approach any problem.

The 5-Step Framework

Apply this to every problem. Same order, every time. Interviewers want structure.

  1. Clarify Requirements (5 min) — NEVER skip this

    Functional requirements: What does it do? Core features only. "Design Twitter" → you must ask: is it just the feed? Or also DMs, notifications, trends?

    Non-functional requirements: Scale, latency target, consistency needs, availability SLA, global or single-region?

    Always ask: How many DAU? Read/write ratio? Data retention? Any compliance (GDPR)? Mobile or web?

    Example: "Design a URL shortener." → Ask: Do we need analytics? Custom aliases? Expiry? That changes the design completely.

  2. Estimate Scale (5 min) — Numbers drive architecture

    QPS, storage, bandwidth. Without real numbers your architecture is guesswork. If the interviewer gives no numbers, assume reasonable ones and state your assumption.

    Example: "I'll assume 100M DAU, 1 write per user per day, 20 reads per user per day. That gives me ~1,150 write QPS and ~23,000 read QPS at average — I'll design for 3× peak, so ~70K read QPS."

  3. High-Level Architecture (8 min) — Draw the skeleton

    Clients → Load Balancer → API Servers → Cache → Database. Identify the primary data flow for the most important use case first. Start simple — you can always add complexity. Don't try to solve everything at once. Name the boxes, state why each exists.

  4. Deep Dive (15 min) — Go deep on hard problems

    DB schema, API contracts, caching strategy, the hardest algorithmic problem (fanout, geospatial index, etc.). Show trade-offs at every decision: "I chose Cassandra here because this is a write-heavy time-series workload with high throughput requirements — the trade-off is we lose SQL joins and strong consistency."

  5. Bottlenecks & Scaling (5 min) — What breaks at 10×?

    Identify SPOFs. What happens when the DB hits 80% capacity? When one service gets hot? Discuss: sharding strategy, cache stampede prevention, circuit breakers, graceful degradation, monitoring alerts.

What Interviewers Actually Evaluate

This is what separates strong from weak candidates

SignalStrong candidate does thisWeak candidate does this
RequirementsAsks targeted questions, narrows scope, confirms before designingJumps to drawing immediately, assumes everything
EstimationCalculates numbers, explicitly states assumptionsSkips math entirely or gives vague "millions of users"
Trade-offs"I'm choosing X over Y because Z — the cost is W"States one option as obviously correct with no reasoning
DepthCan explain internals of their choices (why Kafka, how consistent hashing works)Mentions tech names without explaining them
CommunicationThinks aloud, updates interviewer, asks for feedbackSilent, asks permission before every sentence
The Golden RuleSay what you're about to do, do it, then summarize what you did and why. "I'm going to design the write path first since that's the harder problem. Here's the flow: … I chose this because … The alternative would be … but that has the problem of…"

Worked Example — Applying the Framework

Problem: "Design a system like Pastebin."

Step 1 — Requirements

Questions I'd ask:

Do pastes expire? → "Yes, optional TTL." Custom URLs? → "Yes." Do we need analytics? → "Basic view counts." Max paste size? → "10MB." Authenticated only or anonymous? → "Both." How much traffic? → "100M pastes stored, 1M new pastes/day, 10M reads/day."

Step 2 — Estimation

Write QPS = 1M / 86,400 ≈ 12 QPS (trivial)

Read QPS = 10M / 86,400 ≈ 116 QPS (also small — this is a simple system!)
Storage = 1M pastes/day × avg 100KB × 365 days × 5 years = 182TB. That's object storage (S3), not a relational DB.
This math tells me: reads are light, writes are light, but storage is significant → object storage + aggressive CDN caching.

Step 3 — Architecture

Client → CDN (cache popular pastes) → API Gateway → Paste Service → [PostgreSQL for metadata] + [S3 for paste content] + [Redis for URL dedup cache + rate limiting]

Step 4 — Deep Dive: URL generation

hash(content) → take first 8 chars → check Redis for collision → if collision, append counter and rehash. PostgreSQL stores: paste_id, url_key, user_id, created_at, expires_at, view_count. S3 key = paste_id. Custom URL → validate uniqueness in Postgres first.

How to Approach System Design

Reading & Analysing a Problem

The most underrated skill in system design. Vague problems need precise analysis before you can design anything. Here's exactly how to do it.

The Question Bank — Ask These Every Time

Adapt to the problem, but always cover all four categories

CategoryQuestions to AskWhy It Matters
ScaleHow many DAU/MAU? Expected QPS? Data volume? Growth rate?Determines if you need sharding, caching, CDN
ConsistencyIs eventual consistency OK? Can users see stale data? Do we need linearizability?Determines CP vs AP db, sync vs async
LatencyP99 SLA? Global or single-region? Mobile-first?Determines CDN, edge compute, DB placement
ReliabilityAcceptable downtime? RPO/RTO? Compliance (HIPAA, GDPR)?Determines redundancy, backup strategy, region count

Functional vs Non-Functional Requirements

Functional Requirements — What the system doesCore features. Write these as user stories: "A user can shorten a URL." "A user can view their tweet feed." "A driver can update their location." Only design what's in scope. Push back on scope creep.
Non-Functional Requirements — How well it does itThese drive architecture: availability (99.9% or 99.999%), latency (P99 < 100ms), consistency (strong or eventual), durability (no data loss ever), throughput (100K QPS). These are the constraints your architecture must satisfy.
Example: Analysing "Design Instagram"

Functional scope (agree with interviewer):

Users upload photos/videos → Users follow other users → Home feed shows posts from followed users → Like and comment on posts → (Out of scope: stories, reels, ads, DMs unless asked)

Non-functional to extract:

500M DAU, heavy read (view feeds) vs light write (post photo) → 100:1 read/write ratio → design read path aggressively. Media storage → object storage, not DB. Global CDN for images. Feed latency SLA: P99 < 200ms. Eventual consistency acceptable (seeing a post 1s late is fine). Availability: 99.99% for feed, 99.9% for upload.

Fundamentals

Back-of-Envelope Estimation

The math you do in the first 5 minutes. These numbers dictate every architecture decision. Get comfortable doing this fast — and always show your work to the interviewer.

The Core Formulas

Memorise these — derive everything else from them

Write QPS = (DAU × writes_per_user) / 86,40086,400 = seconds per day. Peak ≈ 3× average. Always design for peak.
Storage = daily_writes × avg_size × retention_days × replication_factorAdd 20% overhead for indexes, WAL, OS overhead, metadata.
Bandwidth = peak_read_QPS × avg_response_sizeFor write: peak_write_QPS × avg_payload_size. CDN offloads most read bandwidth for static content.

Worked Example: Designing Twitter-Scale

Given: 300M DAU · each reads 20 tweets/day · 10% tweet once/day

Step 1: Write QPS

Writers = 300M × 10% = 30M writers
Write QPS = 30M writes ÷ 86,400 = 347 write QPS
Peak write QPS = 347 × 3 ≈ 1,000 write QPS (design for this)

Step 2: Read QPS

Read QPS = 300M × 20 ÷ 86,400 = 69,444 read QPS
Peak read QPS = 69,444 × 3 ≈ 208,000 read QPS
→ Read:Write = 200:1. Design HEAVILY read-optimised. Caching is critical.

Step 3: Storage

Tweet text: 1KB × 347 writes/s × 86,400 s/day × 365 days = 11TB/year
Media (30% of tweets, avg 500KB): 347 × 0.3 × 500KB × 86,400 × 365 ≈ 1.6PB/year
→ Text fits in sharded MySQL. Media MUST be object storage (S3 equivalent).

Step 4: Servers

Rule of thumb: 1 typical app server handles ~10K RPS with caching.
Servers needed = 208,000 ÷ 10,000 = 21 servers at peak minimum.
Real answer: 50+ with safety margin + gradual rollout capacity.

Power of Two — The Cheat Sheet

ValueAbbreviationExample
2^10 = 1,024~1 KBShort text message
2^20 = 1,048,576~1 MBLarge HTML page, small image
2^30 = 1,073,741,824~1 GBMovie (compressed), 1B rows if 1 byte each
2^32 = 4,294,967,296~4 GBMax 32-bit address space, IPv4 space
2^40~1 TB100 hours of 1080p video
2^50~1 PBFacebook stores ~100PB of photos

Quick Estimation Tips

Rules of Thumb (Memorise These) 1M req/day ≈ 12 req/s.  |  1B req/day ≈ 11,500 req/s.
Avg tweet ≈ 1KB. Avg photo ≈ 200KB (compressed). Avg video minute ≈ 10MB (720p).
1 server ≈ 10K simple req/s. 1 DB server ≈ 1K writes/s, 10K reads/s with good indexes.
Redis: ~100K ops/s single node. Kafka partition: ~1M msgs/s.
Fundamentals

Scalability

Scalability = the ability to handle growing load by adding resources. Every architecture decision you make is ultimately a scalability decision. Get this mental model right first.

Vertical vs Horizontal Scaling

Two fundamentally different philosophies — know when each applies

Example to build intuition

Your pizza restaurant gets 10× more orders

Vertical scaling: Hire one master chef who cooks 10× faster. One person, one kitchen, massive skill. Cheaper initially, but there's a ceiling — no human can cook 100× faster.
Horizontal scaling: Open 10 more branches with identical chefs. Each branch handles normal volume. Add more branches as demand grows. No ceiling, but you now need to manage 10 kitchens (coordination cost).

DimensionVertical (Scale Up)Horizontal (Scale Out)
What you doReplace server with bigger one (more CPU, RAM, NVMe)Add more servers behind a load balancer
Code changes neededNone — just restart on bigger hardwareYes — app must be stateless, session externalized
Hard ceilingYes — biggest cloud VM today is ~224 vCPU, 24TB RAMNo — theoretically unlimited
Cost curveExponential — doubling RAM often costs 3–5× not 2×Linear — commodity hardware
Downtime for scalingYes — stop, move, restart (usually)No — add nodes while running (rolling deploy)
SPOF riskHigh — one machine fails, everything diesLow — lose one node, others absorb traffic
Best forEarly stage, databases (hard to horizontally scale), stateful appsStateless API servers, web tier, everything at scale

Stateless = the Key to Horizontal Scaling

Why Stateless MattersIf Server A handles your request and stores your session in memory, the NEXT request must also go to Server A. That breaks horizontal scaling — you can't route freely across servers.

Solution: externalize ALL state. Put sessions in Redis. Store files in S3. Use JWTs instead of server-side sessions. Now any server can handle any request — add 100 servers tomorrow with zero coordination.
Stateless Example: Express.js API
node.js // ❌ STATEFUL — breaks horizontal scaling const sessions = {}; // stored in server memory app.post('/login', (req, res) => { sessions[userId] = { user, loginTime }; // only this server knows! }); // ✅ STATELESS — works with any number of servers app.post('/login', async (req, res) => { const token = jwt.sign({ userId }, SECRET, { expiresIn: '15m' }); // token is self-contained — any server can verify it res.json({ token }); });

The Scalability Ladder — How Real Systems Grow

How architectures evolve with user growth
graph TB L1["Level 1 — Single Server\nEverything: DB + App + Web on one machine\n~100–10K users"] L2["Level 2 — Separate DB Server\nApp server + dedicated DB\n~10K–100K users"] L3["Level 3 — LB + Multiple App Servers\nStateless apps, horizontal scale\n~100K–1M users"] L4["Level 4 — Cache Layer + Read Replicas\nRedis + DB read replicas\n~1M–10M users"] L5["Level 5 — DB Sharding + CDN + Multi-DC\nHorizontal DB, global CDN, geo-routing\n10M+ users"] L1-->L2-->L3-->L4-->L5 style L1 fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style L5 fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#788c5d
Fundamentals

CAP Theorem

The iron law of distributed databases: Consistency, Availability, Partition Tolerance — you can only guarantee 2 of 3. This theorem drives every database architecture decision you'll ever make.

Understanding Each Property

With concrete examples — not abstract definitions

Analogy: Two bank branches with a shared ledger

Branch A and Branch B both serve customers. A cable between them syncs their account data.

Consistency (C): If you deposit £100 at Branch A, checking your balance at Branch B immediately shows the updated amount. Every read returns the latest write — both branches see the same data simultaneously.

Availability (A): Both branches always respond to customers, even if they can't communicate with each other. Branch B serves customers even if its data might be slightly out of date.

Partition Tolerance (P): If the cable between branches breaks (network partition), the system keeps running. The branches continue operating despite not being able to communicate.

The Trade-off: If the cable breaks AND a customer deposits at Branch A AND another customer checks balance at Branch B — you must choose: do you return potentially stale data (AP), or refuse to answer until connectivity restores (CP)?

P is Not OptionalNetwork partitions — cables cut, switches fail, cloud AZs become unreachable — are GUARANTEED to happen in any distributed system. You cannot avoid P. So the real choice is always: CP (consistency + partition tolerance) or AP (availability + partition tolerance). CA only exists on a single machine.

Database Placement on CAP

DatabaseCAP ChoiceWhat You SacrificeWhen to Use
PostgreSQL / MySQL (single node)CA (or CP if replicated)Can't survive partition — single nodeOLTP, financial, small-medium scale
MongoDB (w:majority)CPUnavailable during partition until leader electedDocuments, general purpose, flexible schema
HBase, Zookeeper, etcdCPReturns error if can't guarantee consistencyDistributed coordination, config, locks
Cassandra, DynamoDB (default)APMay return stale data after partitionHigh-write, time-series, IoT, 24/7 availability
CouchDB, RiakAPConflicts resolved after partition healsOffline-first apps, geo-distributed writes
Real-world example: Choosing for an e-commerce cart

Should the shopping cart DB be CP or AP?

AP (Cassandra): Cart is always available — user can always add items, even during network issues. Downside: in rare cases, two browser tabs might create a conflict (same item added twice). Solution: idempotent add operations + merge on read.

CP (PostgreSQL): Cart is always consistent — no duplicates, no conflicts, atomic operations. Downside: if DB has a partition, cart is unavailable until it resolves. For e-commerce where users are in the checkout flow, this might cause revenue loss.

Amazon's answer: Use AP (Dynamo — which inspired DynamoDB) for cart. A slightly stale cart is better than a cart that returns errors. They handle conflicts with vector clocks + merge logic.

PACELC — The Better Model

PACELC Extends CAPCAP only describes behavior during Partitions. But what about normal operation (no partition)?

PACELC: If Partition → choose between A and C. Else (normal) → choose between L (latency) and C (consistency).

Example: DynamoDB offers eventually-consistent reads (low latency, may return stale) AND strongly-consistent reads (higher latency, guaranteed fresh). Same database, your code chooses per query. This is PACELC in practice.
Fundamentals

Latency & Throughput

Latency is how long one operation takes. Throughput is how many operations happen per second. They're related but not the same — and optimising one can hurt the other.

The Numbers Table — Memorise This

Understanding orders of magnitude is more important than exact numbers

OperationLatencyRelative to L1Why it matters
L1 cache read~0.5 ns1×Fastest possible — CPU registers
L2 cache read~5 ns10×Still in CPU chip
L3 cache read~40 ns80×Shared across cores
RAM read~100 ns200×This is Redis territory
NVMe SSD read~100 µs200,000×1000× slower than RAM
HDD seek + read~10 ms20,000,000×Avoid sequential IO where possible
Same-DC network~0.5 ms1,000,000×In-datacenter call latency
Cross-region (US–EU)~100 ms200,000,000×Why you need edge/CDN for global users
Why this matters: Picking your data store

Scenario: You need to check user session on every API request

Option A — Hit PostgreSQL: ~1ms (same DC, indexed query). 1,000 req/s → 1,000 DB queries/s. At 100,000 req/s → 100K DB queries/s. DB dies.
Option B — Hit Redis (RAM-based): ~0.1ms. 100× faster. 1 Redis node handles 100K ops/s easily. Your API servers never wait for sessions.
Decision: Cache sessions in Redis, primary storage in PostgreSQL. This single decision multiplies your throughput 100×.

Percentiles — P50, P95, P99, P999

Never Use Averages for LatencyImagine 100 requests: 95 complete in 10ms, 5 complete in 1000ms. Average = 59ms. But P95 = 1000ms. The average hides the pain.

P50 = median. P95 = 95th percentile (95% of requests faster than this). P99 = 99th percentile. Always set SLAs on P99 or P99.9. At 1M req/day, P99 = 10,000 users experiencing that worst-case latency daily.
Little's Law — The Fundamental Throughput Formula

L = λ × W

L = average number of requests in the system (concurrency)
λ = arrival rate (requests per second = throughput)
W = average time in system (latency in seconds)

Example: Your API handles 500 req/s (λ=500). Each request takes 200ms on average (W=0.2s).
L = 500 × 0.2 = 100 concurrent requests in-flight at any moment.
→ Size your thread pool / connection pool to handle at least 100 concurrent. If your pool is 50, requests queue up → latency spikes → cascade failure.

Latency vs Throughput Trade-off

Batching ExampleSingle DB insert: 5ms. Batch 1000 inserts in one transaction: 50ms total = 0.05ms per insert.
Throughput: 1000/50ms = 20,000 inserts/sec. Single inserts: 1/5ms = 200 inserts/sec. Batching = 100× throughput.
Cost: each insert now waits up to 50ms (latency increased). For analytics pipelines → batch. For payment confirmations → single insert (user waiting).
Fundamentals

Availability & SLOs

High availability means your system works when users need it. Design for failure — every component WILL fail eventually. The question is whether your system handles it gracefully.

The Nines of Availability

Each nine requires dramatically more engineering — know what you're signing up for

SLADowntime / YearDowntime / MonthRequired Engineering
99% (two nines)3.65 days7.2 hoursBasic setup, no special design
99.9% (three nines)8.76 hours43.8 minutesRedundant services, health checks, basic failover
99.99% (four nines)52.6 minutes4.4 minutesActive-active, auto-failover, zero-downtime deploys
99.999% (five nines)5.26 minutes26 secondsMulti-region, chaos engineering, dedicated SRE team
Example: What "four nines" actually requires

4.4 minutes of allowed downtime per month

A standard rolling deployment might take 2–3 minutes → that's already half your monthly budget.
Solution: Blue-green deployments (switch traffic atomically), canary releases (route 1% to new version first), feature flags (deploy code dark, enable via flag).
Health check → auto-remove unhealthy instances in <30s. DB failover → primary-replica with auto-promote in <60s.
You need runbooks, automated alerting, and can't rely on humans waking up at 3am to fix things.

SLI / SLO / SLA / Error Budget — Explained with Math

definitions SLI (Service Level Indicator) = the metric you measure Example: availability = successful_requests / total_requests Example: latency_p99 = 99th percentile response time SLO (Service Level Objective) = your internal target Example: availability >= 99.9% over rolling 30 days Example: latency_p99 <= 200ms SLA (Service Level Agreement) = external contract with consequences Example: if availability drops below 99.9%, customer gets 10% bill credit Error Budget = allowable badness before SLO breach Error budget = (1 - SLO) × time_period Example: 99.9% SLO over 30 days = 0.1% × 43,200 min = 43.2 minutes of allowed downtime → When budget is exhausted: freeze all risky changes, focus on reliability → When budget is healthy: move fast, ship features Error budgets align velocity with reliability — it's data, not politics.

MTTF, MTTR — The Recovery Math

Availability = MTTF / (MTTF + MTTR)MTTF = Mean Time To Failure (avg time before a component fails)
MTTR = Mean Time To Repair (avg time to fix after failure)
Example: MTTF = 1000h, MTTR = 1h → Availability = 1000/1001 = 99.9%
To improve: increase MTTF (better hardware, redundancy) OR decrease MTTR (runbooks, automation, good monitoring)
Fundamentals

Consistency Models

When data is replicated across multiple nodes, what guarantees do you get about what different clients read? Consistency models define exactly this spectrum from strongest to weakest.

Consistency Spectrum (Strongest → Weakest)

ModelGuaranteeLatencyReal Example
Linearizable (Strong)Global ordering — every operation appears instant and atomic. If write A completes before read B starts, B sees A.Highestetcd, Zookeeper, single-node Postgres
Sequential ConsistencyAll processes agree on operation order. Results look like some serial execution.HighDistributed locks, Raft consensus
Causal ConsistencyCausally related operations in order. Unrelated ops may be seen in different orders by different nodes.MediumMongoDB causal sessions, DynamoDB transactions
Read-Your-WritesAfter you write, your subsequent reads always see that write. Other clients may not.Low-MedMost user-facing apps, sticky load balancing
Eventual ConsistencyAll nodes converge to the same value eventually. No guarantee on when. Stale reads possible.LowestCassandra default, DynamoDB eventually-consistent reads, DNS
Intuition: Posting on Facebook

You post a photo. Your friend in another country views your profile immediately after.

Linearizable: Friend sees the photo immediately. Hard to achieve globally — would require synchronous replication across continents → high latency for every write.
Eventual: Friend might not see the photo for 1–2 seconds (while it replicates to their region). They refresh → it's there. Facebook actually uses eventual consistency here — 1-2 seconds of "stale" is acceptable, and it lets writes be fast for you (you don't wait for global replication).
Read-your-writes: You always see your own photo immediately (Facebook pins your reads to the datacenter you wrote to). Other users may see it slightly delayed.

Tunable Consistency (Cassandra)Most real systems let you choose per-operation: QUORUM reads (majority of replicas must agree — stronger) vs ONE reads (any one replica — faster, more stale). Design for "strong where it matters, eventual where speed matters more."
Networking

DNS — Domain Name System

The internet's phonebook. Translates domain names to IP addresses. Understanding DNS lets you do global traffic routing, failover, and edge optimisation.

How DNS Resolution Works — Step by Step

Example: You type "github.com" in your browser

What happens in ~50ms before your browser makes a single HTTP request:

1. Browser checks its own cache → miss (or TTL expired)
2. OS checks /etc/hosts → not found
3. OS asks the configured DNS resolver (your ISP or 8.8.8.8 or 1.1.1.1)
4. Resolver checks its cache → miss
5. Resolver queries a Root Name Server: "where are .com TLD servers?"
6. Root says: "Try 192.5.6.30 for .com"
7. Resolver asks .com TLD: "where are github.com's name servers?"
8. TLD says: "Try ns1.p16.dynect.net (GitHub's authoritative NS)"
9. Resolver asks GitHub's NS: "what's the A record for github.com?"
10. GitHub NS replies: "140.82.121.4, TTL=60s"
11. Resolver caches it and returns to your browser
12. Browser connects to 140.82.121.4

DNS resolution sequence
sequenceDiagram participant B as Browser participant R as Recursive Resolver (ISP/8.8.8.8) participant Root as Root NS (13 globally) participant TLD as .com TLD NS participant Auth as Authoritative NS (GitHub) B->>R: What's the IP for github.com? R->>Root: Where are .com servers? Root-->>R: 192.5.6.30 R->>TLD: Where are github.com's nameservers? TLD-->>R: ns1.p16.dynect.net R->>Auth: A record for github.com? Auth-->>R: 140.82.121.4 TTL=60s R-->>B: 140.82.121.4 (cached 60s)

DNS Record Types

RecordMapsExampleUse
ADomain → IPv4github.com → 140.82.121.4Most common — map name to server
AAAADomain → IPv6github.com → 2606:50c0::68IPv6 support
CNAMEAlias → another domainwww.github.com → github.comAlias; can't be on root domain
MXDomain → mail servergithub.com → aspmx.l.google.comEmail routing
TXTDomain → arbitrary textSPF, DKIM, site verificationSecurity, ownership proof
NSDomain → name serversgithub.com → ns1.p16.dynect.netDelegates zone authority
SRVService → host + port_http._tcp.example.com → host:80Service discovery (Kubernetes uses this)
GeoDNS — Global Traffic Routing via DNSAWS Route 53, Cloudflare, and others return different A records based on the client's geographic location. User in Mumbai → Singapore edge server (8ms). User in Berlin → Frankfurt edge server (5ms). Same domain, different IPs, different datacenters — all via DNS. This is how global products achieve low latency without the user doing anything different.

TTL Trade-off: Short TTL (60s) = fast failover when you change IPs, but more DNS queries (cost + resolver load). Long TTL (3600s) = cached everywhere, faster resolution, slow failover. Best practice: drop TTL to 60s 24h before any IP change.
Networking

Load Balancers

Distribute traffic across multiple servers. Eliminate SPOFs. Enable horizontal scaling. One of the most critical infrastructure components — know every type, every algorithm, and every trade-off.

Types of Load Balancers

TypeExamplesThroughputKey Features
Hardware LBF5 BIG-IP, Citrix ADCTbps rangePurpose-built chips for SSL offload, extremely expensive ($50K–$500K). Legacy enterprise.
Software L4 LBHAProxy (TCP mode), IPVS, LVS100s GbpsRoutes TCP/UDP by IP+port only, no HTTP inspection, ultra-fast minimal processing.
Software L7 LBNginx, Envoy, Traefik, Caddy10s GbpsHTTP-aware, path routing, header rewriting, SSL termination, rate limiting, auth.
Cloud-managedAWS ALB (L7), NLB (L4), GCP LBElasticFully managed, multi-AZ, auto-scaling, integrates with cloud ecosystem. No server to maintain.
Service Mesh ProxyEnvoy (Istio), Linkerd proxyGbps per podSidecar per pod, mTLS between services, distributed tracing, circuit breaking — zero code changes.

L4 vs L7 — This Distinction Matters in Interviews

FeatureL4 (Transport)L7 (Application)
SeesTCP/UDP packets, src/dst IP+portHTTP headers, URL path, cookies, request body
SSL TerminationPass-through only (can't inspect payload)✅ Decrypts at LB, backends get plain HTTP
Path-based routing❌ Can't inspect URL✅ /api → svc-api, /static → CDN, /admin → svc-admin
Sticky sessionsIP-hash only (breaks behind NAT)✅ Cookie-based (survives NAT, more reliable)
Latency overheadMicrosecondsMilliseconds (full packet inspection)
AWS equivalentNetwork Load Balancer (NLB)Application Load Balancer (ALB)
Use caseRaw TCP (databases, gaming), very high throughputHTTP APIs, web apps, microservices (most common)

Load Balancing Algorithms — All 6

Round Robin

Requests distributed sequentially: Server1 → Server2 → Server3 → Server1 → …

How it works: Keep a counter. Increment mod N. Route to servers[counter].
Best for: Stateless services where all servers have equal capacity and similar request duration.
Weakness: Server 1 gets a 10-second request while Server 2 gets 10 fast 10ms requests. Next request still goes to Server 1, which is busy. Doesn't account for load.
Used by: Nginx default, simple deployments, most appropriate for homogeneous fleets.

Least Connections

Route to the server with the fewest active connections right now

How it works: LB tracks active connection count per backend. New request → pick server with lowest count.
Best for: Long-lived connections (WebSockets, database connections, HTTPS keep-alive). Avoids hot servers naturally.
Example: Server A: 10 connections. Server B: 3 connections. Server C: 8 connections. Next request → Server B.
Used by: Nginx upstream with `least_conn`, HAProxy `leastconn`, AWS ALB.

Weighted Round Robin / Least Connections

Servers get traffic proportional to their assigned weight

How it works: Server A (weight=3) gets 3 requests per cycle. Server B (weight=1) gets 1. In 4 requests: A, A, A, B.
Best for: Heterogeneous fleets (different hardware), canary deployments (5% traffic to new version), gradual migrations.
Canary example: v1.0 weight=95, v2.0 weight=5 → 5% of users hit new version. Watch error rates. If fine → increase to 20%, 50%, 100%.

nginx upstream backend { server 10.0.0.1:3000 weight=19; # v1 — 95% server 10.0.0.2:3000 weight=1; # v2 — 5% canary }
IP Hash (Sticky Sessions)

hash(client_ip) % N → same server for same client, always

Best for: Stateful apps where session is in server memory (legacy apps).
Problems: (1) Many users behind one corporate NAT → all route to one server = hot spot. (2) Adding/removing servers remaps all clients → session loss.
Better alternative: Store sessions in Redis, use stateless Round Robin instead. IP hash is a band-aid for poor session management.

Power of Two Choices (Random + 2)

Pick 2 random servers → route to the less loaded one

Why it's brilliant: O(1) — no global state needed. No scanning all N servers. Statistically, approaching "perfect" least-connections with a fraction of the overhead. Avoids round-robin's obliviousness and least-connections' global state requirement.
Math: Worst-case load imbalance: O(log log N) instead of O(log N / log log N) for pure random. Netflix, Nginx, and Envoy all use this. It just works.

Least Response Time

Route to server with lowest combination of: active connections + response time

How: Track rolling average response time per server. Pick server with lowest (connections × avg_response_time).
Best for: When backend servers have variable performance (due to hotspots, GC pauses, etc.).
Used by: HAProxy, Nginx Plus, F5. More overhead than least_conn but better for heterogeneous response times.

The LB Itself Is a SPOF — How to Fix ItActive-passive pair with VRRP/keepalived: primary handles traffic, backup promotes in <1s on failure. Active-active with Anycast: multiple LBs, BGP routes traffic to nearest live one automatically. Cloud-managed LBs (AWS ALB, GCP LB) are internally redundant — no single machine, cross-AZ by design. For most systems: use a cloud managed LB and stop worrying about it.
Networking

CDN — Content Delivery Network

A globally distributed network of servers that cache your content close to users. 300ms from US server to Mumbai user → 5ms from Cloudflare's Mumbai PoP. Non-negotiable for any global product.

How CDNs Work — The Full Picture

Example: User in Mumbai requests a logo from a US company's website

Without CDN:

Mumbai user → request crosses Pacific → hits US-East server (200ms RTT) → downloads 500KB image → 200ms + download time ≈ 500ms total.

With CDN (Cloudflare):

Mumbai user → request hits Cloudflare's Mumbai PoP (5ms RTT) → PoP checks local cache → HIT → serves 500KB image from local cache → 5ms + download time ≈ 15ms total. 33× faster.
On first request (cache MISS): Mumbai PoP fetches from US origin (200ms), stores locally, serves user. All subsequent Mumbai users get the cached copy.

CDN cache hit vs miss flow
graph LR MU["User: Mumbai"] -->|"req: logo.png"| MEP["CDN Edge\nMumbai PoP"] BU["User: Berlin"] -->|"req: logo.png"| BEP["CDN Edge\nFrankfurt PoP"] MEP -->|"HIT ✅ 5ms"| MU BEP -->|"MISS → fetch origin"| OR["Origin Server\nUS-East"] OR -->|"200ms + cache-control header"| BEP BEP -->|"cached, served\nnext req: 6ms"| BU style MEP fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#788c5d style OR fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Pull CDN vs Push CDN

Pull CDN (Lazy/On-Demand)Push CDN (Eager/Pre-populated)
HowCDN fetches from origin on first cache miss, caches resultYou upload content to CDN ahead of time
ConfigAutomatic — driven by Cache-Control HTTP headersManual — you control exactly what's on CDN
First requestCache miss → slow (origin fetch). 2nd request: fast.Always fast — content already on CDN
Storage costLower — only caches what's actually accessedHigher — you pay for everything pre-loaded
Best forUnpredictable access, general web assets, APIsLarge known files: software downloads, video-on-demand, game patches
ExamplesCloudflare, Fastly, Akamai, CloudFront (pull mode)S3 + CloudFront (you upload to S3, CF serves from it)

Cache-Control Headers — The CDN Control Plane

HTTP headers # ✅ Immutable assets — hash in filename, cache forever Cache-Control: public, max-age=31536000, immutable # Use this for: main.a3f8bc.js, logo-v2.abc123.png # Filename changes on deploy → CDN gets new uncached file # ✅ HTML pages — short cache, always revalidate freshness Cache-Control: public, max-age=300, stale-while-revalidate=60 # Browser uses stale copy while background refresh happens # ✅ API for all users (product catalog) — CDN caches longer Cache-Control: public, s-maxage=86400, max-age=3600 # s-maxage = CDN TTL (24h). max-age = browser TTL (1h). # ❌ User-specific data — NEVER cache at CDN Cache-Control: private, no-store # Use for: /api/me, /cart, /notifications, anything auth-gated
What NOT to Cache at CDNUser-specific responses (cart, profile, notifications). Real-time data (live scores, stock prices). POST/PUT/DELETE responses. Any endpoint with a Set-Cookie header (will leak cookies across users). Rule: if two different users GETting the same URL should get different responses → do NOT cache at CDN.

CDN Providers at a Glance

ProviderPoPsDifferentiatorBest For
Cloudflare300+Workers (edge compute), free DDoS, Argo Smart RoutingWeb apps, APIs, DDoS protection, DNS + CDN combo
AWS CloudFront400+Lambda@Edge, tight AWS IAM/S3/ALB integrationAWS-native stacks, S3 static sites, custom Lambda logic at edge
Fastly60+Instant purge (<150ms globally), VCL edge programmingDynamic content, real-time purge, API caching with complex rules
Akamai4000+Largest network, Ion for performance, media streamingEnterprise, live video streaming, large-file delivery
Networking

Proxies & API Gateway

Forward proxies hide clients. Reverse proxies hide servers. API Gateways are L7 reverse proxies with authentication, rate limiting, and routing built in.

Forward vs Reverse Proxy

Simple analogy

A secretary in an office

Forward proxy (hiding clients): The secretary routes ALL outbound calls from employees through one number. The recipient sees the secretary's number, not which employee called. VPNs, corporate web proxies, Tor exit nodes work this way.
Reverse proxy (hiding servers): All calls to the company go through the receptionist. The caller doesn't know which of the 10 departments answered. Nginx, Cloudflare, ALBs all work this way — you talk to one IP, it routes to the right internal server.

Forward ProxyReverse Proxy
ProtectsClient identity from serverServer topology from client
Configured byClient / IT adminServer admin / DevOps
Common examplesVPN, Squid, corporate proxy, TorNginx, Caddy, Envoy, AWS ALB, Cloudflare
Use casesAnonymity, content filtering, bypassing geo-blocksLoad balancing, SSL termination, caching, auth, logging

API Gateway — The Entry Point to Your Microservices

API gateway as the unified entry point
graph LR Mobile & Web & Partner["Partner API"] --> GW["API Gateway\n(single entry point)"] GW --> Auth2["JWT / OAuth\nValidation"] GW --> RL["Rate Limiting\nper user/key/IP"] GW --> Route["Path Routing\n/v1/users → user-svc\n/v1/orders → order-svc"] GW --> Obs["Logging +\nTracing"] Route --> US["User Service"] & OS["Order Service"] & PS["Payment Service"] style GW fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757
nginx.conf upstream user_service { least_conn; server 10.0.0.1:3000; server 10.0.0.2:3000; keepalive 32; # connection pool — reuse TCP connections } server { listen 443 ssl http2; # SSL terminates HERE — backends get plain HTTP (faster) ssl_certificate /etc/certs/cert.pem; location /v1/users { proxy_pass http://user_service; proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr; proxy_read_timeout 30s; } location /static { root /var/www; expires 1y; add_header Cache-Control "public, immutable"; } }
Networking

HTTP, WebSockets & SSE

Know the evolution of HTTP and when to use persistent connections, long polling, SSE, or WebSockets for real-time communication.

HTTP Evolution

VersionKey FeatureProblem Solved
HTTP/1.0Basic request-response—
HTTP/1.1Persistent connections (keep-alive), pipelining, chunked transferNo new TCP handshake per request. 3–5× faster.
HTTP/2Multiplexing (N streams on 1 TCP), HPACK header compression, server pushHead-of-line blocking per stream eliminated. No parallel connection hacks needed.
HTTP/3QUIC (UDP-based), 0-RTT setup, no TCP HOL blockingMobile networks, packet loss doesn't block all streams. 30–40% faster on lossy networks.

Real-Time Communication Options

TechniqueLatencyDirectionOverheadBest For
Short Polling0–interval (e.g. 1s)Client → ServerHigh (repeated HTTP)Simple dashboards, infrequent updates
Long PollingNear-zero on eventClient → ServerMedium (held HTTP conn)Simple notifications (legacy)
SSE (Server-Sent Events)Near-zero on eventServer → Client onlyLow (persistent HTTP/2)Live feeds, dashboards, CI/CD logs
WebSocketNear-zero both waysFull duplexLow after handshakeChat, multiplayer gaming, collaborative editing, trading
When to choose what

Scenario: "Show live delivery tracking on a map"

SSE: Perfect. Server pushes location updates every 3s. Client only receives, never sends updates. Simple HTTP/2 stream. No WebSocket complexity needed.
WebSocket: Overkill if client doesn't need to send data. But if you also need chat between customer and driver → WebSocket makes more sense (bidirectional).
Short polling: Could work (req every 3s), but 10K concurrent deliveries × 1 req/3s = 3,300 req/s just for tracking. SSE holds one connection per user instead.

WebSocket upgrade handshake
sequenceDiagram Client->>Server: GET /ws HTTP/1.1\nUpgrade: websocket\nSec-WebSocket-Key: abc123 Server-->>Client: 101 Switching Protocols\nUpgrade: websocket Note over Client,Server: TCP connection upgraded — full duplex Client->>Server: Frame: {type:"message", data:"hello"} Server->>Client: Frame: {type:"ack"} Server->>Client: Frame: {type:"push", data:"new order"}
Networking

gRPC & Protocol Buffers

Google's high-performance RPC framework. Binary serialisation, HTTP/2, strongly typed contracts. 5–10× smaller payloads than REST/JSON. The standard for high-throughput internal microservices.

Why gRPC Over REST for Internal Services

Concrete comparison: fetching a user profile

REST/JSON response for a user object:

{"id":123,"name":"Loki","email":"loki@asgard.com","role":"admin","created_at":"2024-01-15T10:00:00Z"} — ~90 bytes just for this tiny object. Plus HTTP/1.1 headers (~500 bytes). Total: ~600 bytes per request.

gRPC/Protobuf encoding:

Same data in Protobuf binary: ~25 bytes (field numbers instead of field names, binary encoding). Plus HTTP/2 HPACK-compressed headers (~50 bytes first request, ~10 bytes subsequent). Total: ~35 bytes. 17× smaller. At 100K req/s: saves ~56MB/s of bandwidth.

protobuf syntax = "proto3"; service UserService { // Unary — one request, one response (like REST) rpc GetUser(UserRequest) returns (User); // Server streaming — one request, stream of responses rpc WatchUser(UserRequest) returns (stream UserEvent); // Client streaming — stream of requests, one response rpc BatchCreate(stream User) returns (BatchResult); // Bidirectional — both sides stream simultaneously rpc Chat(stream Message) returns (stream Message); } message User { int64 id = 1; // field numbers encode to 1 byte string name = 2; // not field names = massive space saving string email = 3; } // Run: protoc → generates client + server code in Go, Python, Java, etc. // stub.GetUser(UserRequest(id=123)) → User object — strongly typed, no JSON.parse
gRPC LimitationsBrowser support: not native. Needs gRPC-Web proxy (usually Envoy) — adds complexity. Debugging: binary format not human-readable (use grpcurl CLI or Postman). Good practice: use REST/JSON for public-facing APIs (easier to consume), gRPC for internal service-to-service communication.
Databases

SQL vs NoSQL — The Full Picture

The single most impactful architecture decision. Choose wrong and you'll do a painful migration at scale. Choose right and everything else is easier.

The Core Trade-offs

Analogy: Physical filing systems

SQL = Filing Cabinet with strict folders

Everything must go in a pre-defined folder (schema). Want to add a new type of document? Create a new folder (ALTER TABLE). Want to find all documents by a specific person? You can cross-reference folders efficiently (JOINs). Highly organised, strict, powerful queries. Changing the structure requires planning.

NoSQL = Giant pile sorted by access pattern
Designed around HOW you access data, not how it's structured. You can throw in documents with different shapes. No schema to enforce. But: if you need to find documents across multiple categories (JOIN), you have to do it yourself in application code.

DimensionSQL (Relational)NoSQL
SchemaRigid — define columns upfront, ALTER TABLE for changesFlexible — each document can have different fields
JoinsNative, efficient with indexes — the whole point of relationalNo server-side joins — denormalize or join in app code
ACIDFull ACID by default across tablesOften BASE — eventual consistency, weaker guarantees
ScalingVertical primarily. Horizontal (sharding) is complex and painful.Designed for horizontal scaling from the start
Query flexibilityArbitrary SQL queries — great for ad-hoc analysisOptimise for known access patterns only
When to useFinancial data, e-commerce, anything needing strong consistency + complex queriesHigh-write workloads, flexible schema, massive scale, simple access patterns

The 4 NoSQL Types — Don't Lump Them Together

Document Store
MongoDB, Firestore, CouchDB

JSON-like documents. Flexible schema. Rich query language. Nested data natural. Aggregation pipeline for analytics.

CatalogsProfilesCMS
Key-Value
Redis, DynamoDB, Memcached

Simplest model: key maps to value. O(1) lookup. Can't query by value. Blazing fast. Limited access patterns.

SessionsCachingRate limit
Wide-Column
Cassandra, HBase, Bigtable

Rows with dynamic columns. Partitioned by row key. Massive write throughput. Time-series friendly. Petabyte scale.

IoTTime-seriesAnalytics
Graph DB
Neo4j, Amazon Neptune

Nodes + edges. O(1) relationship traversal regardless of dataset size. Queries that kill SQL are trivial here.

Social graphFraud
The Right Default AdviceStart with PostgreSQL. It handles 90% of use cases beautifully. Add a specialised NoSQL store ONLY when you hit a specific felt pain: unstructured docs → MongoDB, sessions/caching → Redis, massive writes → Cassandra, graph traversal → Neo4j. "It sounds more scalable" is not a reason to reach for NoSQL — it's usually a reason to find a better DBA.
Databases

SQL Deep Dive

PostgreSQL is the most capable open-source database. Know its internals, indexes, performance patterns — push it to its limits before adding complexity.

Performance Anti-patterns & Fixes

PostgreSQL -- ❌ OFFSET pagination: O(N) — scans 20,000 rows to skip them SELECT * FROM posts ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20 OFFSET 20000; -- At page 1000 with 20 items/page: scans 20,000 rows, returns 20. -- ✅ Cursor pagination: O(log N) with index — always fast SELECT id, title, created_at FROM posts WHERE created_at < '2024-01-15T10:00:00Z' -- last item from prev page ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20; -- index seek, then 20 rows -- ❌ SELECT * : returns columns you don't need (network waste) SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = 123; -- ✅ Select only what you need — enables covering index SELECT id, name, email FROM users WHERE id = 123; -- ❌ N+1 query problem: 1 query for list + N queries for details SELECT id FROM posts; -- returns 100 post ids -- loop: SELECT * FROM users WHERE id = {post.user_id}; -- 100 queries! -- ✅ JOIN: 1 query total SELECT p.id, p.title, u.name FROM posts p JOIN users u ON u.id = p.user_id; -- EXPLAIN ANALYZE: see the actual query plan EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) SELECT * FROM posts WHERE user_id = 123; -- "Seq Scan" on large table = missing index -- "Index Scan" = good. "Index Only Scan" = even better (covering index)

JSONB — Flexible Schema Inside Postgres

PostgreSQL -- Use JSONB when fields vary per row but you still want SQL CREATE TABLE products ( id BIGSERIAL PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT NOT NULL, metadata JSONB -- {color:"red", size:"L"} for clothing; {wattage:60} for bulbs ); CREATE INDEX idx_meta ON products USING GIN(metadata); SELECT * FROM products WHERE metadata @> '{"color":"red"}'; -- GIN index used ✅ SELECT metadata->>'size' FROM products WHERE id = 1; -- extract field
Databases

NoSQL — All 4 Types in Depth

Each NoSQL type exists for a specific reason. Understanding internals lets you explain your choices — and that's what interviews test.

MongoDB — Document Store

MongoDB // Document: flexible, nested, arrays — no schema migration needed { _id: ObjectId("507f1f77bcf86cd799439011"), username: "lokiodinson", followers: 1500000, posts: [{ title: "How I faked my death", likes: 420 }], // embedded address: { city: "Asgard", realm: "Nine Realms" } // nested } // Aggregation pipeline — SQL GROUP BY but more composable db.orders.aggregate([ { $match: { status: "completed", total: { $gt: 100 } } }, { $group: { _id: "$user_id", total_spent: { $sum: "$total" } } }, { $sort: { total_spent: -1 } }, { $limit: 10 } ]) // Write concern w:majority = CP (waits for quorum to confirm) // Write concern w:1 = AP (acks on primary only, may lose on failover)

Cassandra — Wide-Column Store

Design rule: model around your queries, not your data structure. Cassandra can't do joins.

Cassandra CQL -- Partition key = how data is distributed (each partition → one node) -- Clustering key = how data is sorted within a partition -- Design for the query "fetch messages for conversation X, newest first" CREATE TABLE messages_by_conversation ( conversation_id UUID, sent_at TIMESTAMP, message_id UUID, sender_id BIGINT, content TEXT, PRIMARY KEY ((conversation_id), sent_at, message_id) ) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (sent_at DESC); -- Perfect query: single partition = O(1) = microseconds SELECT * FROM messages_by_conversation WHERE conversation_id = 123e4567-e89b-12d3-a456-426655440000 LIMIT 50; -- ANTI-PATTERN: filter on non-partition key = full cluster scan SELECT * FROM messages WHERE sender_id = 456; -- AVOID without 2nd index
Cassandra Consistency MathReplication factor RF=3: each row on 3 nodes. Write QUORUM = ⌊RF/2⌋+1 = 2 nodes must confirm. Read QUORUM = 2 nodes must return matching data.
If W+R > RF → strong consistency (at least 1 node overlaps). W=2 + R=2 > RF=3 → strongly consistent even with 1 node down. W=1 + R=1 → eventually consistent, fastest, possible staleness.
Databases

Transactions & ACID

Transactions let you bundle multiple operations that must succeed or fail atomically. ACID properties prevent partial updates, data corruption, and loss in concurrent systems.

ACID — Every Property Explained with Examples

Classic example: Bank transfer

Transfer £100 from Alice (balance £500) to Bob (balance £200)

Atomicity: Both "debit Alice £100" AND "credit Bob £100" must happen. If the server crashes after debiting Alice but before crediting Bob, the transaction rolls back — Alice's balance reverts to £500. Either both happen or neither happens.
Consistency: Before transfer: Alice £500 + Bob £200 = £700 total. After: Alice £400 + Bob £300 = £700 total. Money was neither created nor destroyed. Constraints enforced.
Isolation: If another transaction reads balances while ours is running, it either sees the old state (before any changes) or the new state (after both changes). Never the in-between state where Alice's balance is reduced but Bob's isn't yet increased.
Durability: Once we return "success" to Alice, the transfer is permanent. Even if the server dies 1ms later, the data is on disk. No "sorry, we lost your transfer."

Isolation Levels — The Concurrency Trade-off

LevelDirty ReadNon-Repeatable ReadPhantom ReadPerformance
Read Uncommitted✗ Possible✗ Possible✗ PossibleFastest (avoid in production)
Read Committed (Postgres default)✓ Safe✗ Possible✗ PossibleFast — most apps use this
Repeatable Read (MySQL InnoDB default)✓✓✗ Possible (MySQL) / ✓ (Postgres MVCC)Medium
Serializable✓✓✓Slowest — full isolation
Dirty Read Example (why Read Uncommitted is dangerous)

Transaction A starts transferring money. Transaction B reads in the middle.

T_A: debit Alice £100 (balance now £400, uncommitted)
T_B reads Alice's balance → sees £400 (dirty read!)
T_A: fails, rolls back → Alice's balance reverts to £500
T_B: made a decision based on £400 which never actually existed.
Read Committed prevents this: T_B only sees committed data.

Caching

Cache Fundamentals

Caching is the highest-ROI backend optimisation. Caches exploit the fact that data is not accessed uniformly — a tiny fraction of data (hot data) accounts for most reads. Cache that fraction in RAM.

The 4 Cache Patterns

Cache-Aside (Lazy Loading) — Most Common

Application manages the cache manually. "Check cache first, populate on miss."

Cache-aside read path
sequenceDiagram App->>Redis: GET user:123 Redis-->>App: nil (MISS) App->>DB: SELECT * FROM users WHERE id=123 DB-->>App: {id:123, name:"Loki", role:"admin"} App->>Redis: SET user:123 "{...}" EX 3600 Note over App,Redis: Next request for user:123 App->>Redis: GET user:123 Redis-->>App: HIT ✅ — serves from RAM, DB not touched

✅ Only caches what's actually accessed. If Redis dies → fall back to DB. Simple to implement.
❌ First request after expiry always slow (cold start). Stale data if DB updated without invalidating cache key.

Write-Through

Every write goes to cache AND database synchronously before returning success.

Flow: App writes → update cache → cache writes to DB → success.
✅ Cache always has fresh data. No stale reads ever. Read-after-write consistent.
❌ Write latency includes cache write overhead. Cache pollution: items written that are never read waste RAM. Complex to implement transactionally (what if cache write succeeds but DB write fails?).
Best for: Session management, user profile updates where you want immediate consistency.

Write-Behind (Write-Back)

Write to cache immediately → return success → flush to DB asynchronously.

Flow: App writes → cache updated (instant return) → background worker flushes to DB in batches.
✅ Ultra-fast writes. Batching reduces DB writes 100–1000×. Example: post "like" counter — each like writes to Redis, Redis flushes total to DB every 30 seconds instead of 1 DB write per like.
❌ Data loss if cache crashes before flush. Not suitable for financial data or anything where you cannot afford loss.
Best for: Like counts, view counters, analytics aggregations, non-critical metrics.

Refresh-Ahead (Read-Ahead)

Proactively refresh cache before TTL expires — no cold start latency.

Flow: Monitor TTL. When item reaches 80% of its TTL → background refresh from DB → new TTL set → reads always hit warm cache.
✅ Eliminates cold start latency spikes. Hot items are always cached. Great for predictable access patterns.
❌ May cache stale data if refresh runs before value changes. Overhead for rarely-accessed items.
Best for: Homepage content, trending items, featured products, anything accessed very frequently.

Cache Stampede — The Production Killer

The Problem (with numbers)A cached key for "top 10 trending posts" expires. At that moment, 50,000 concurrent users request the homepage. ALL 50,000 get a cache miss simultaneously. ALL 50,000 slam the database. DB gets 50,000 complex queries in 1 second instead of 1 query every 60 seconds. DB collapses. Site is down for 5 minutes. This actually kills production systems.
Solutions1. Mutex Lock: Only first miss acquires a Redis lock and fetches DB. Others wait on lock (blocking) or return stale data (non-blocking). Most common fix.
2. Probabilistic Early Expiry: Each request randomly decides to refresh early: if random() < beta × (ttl_remaining / initial_ttl). As TTL approaches 0, probability of refresh increases. Netflix uses this.
3. Stale-While-Revalidate: Serve stale data immediately, trigger background refresh. Never blocks user requests. HTTP header: Cache-Control: max-age=60, stale-while-revalidate=120.
4. Pre-warming: Script that populates cache before deploy traffic hits. Prevents cold start entirely.
Caching

Redis — Complete Guide

Redis is not just a cache. It's an in-memory data structure server — strings, hashes, sorted sets, geo, pub/sub, streams, Lua scripting, persistence. The most versatile backend tool in existence.

Every Data Structure — When to Use Each

TypeCommandsUse CaseExample
StringGET SET INCR DECR GETSETCache, counters, session tokens, feature flags, distributed locksSET user:123 "{...}" EX 3600
HashHGET HSET HMGET HGETALL HINCRBYObject with multiple fields. Partial updates without serializing whole object.HSET user:123 name "Loki" role "admin"
ListLPUSH RPUSH LRANGE LPOP BRPOPQueue (LPUSH + BRPOP), recent activity feed, task queueLPUSH jobs "{task}" / BRPOP jobs 0
SetSADD SISMEMBER SMEMBERS SUNION SINTERUnique membership, tags, mutual friends, deduplicationSADD online_users 123 / SISMEMBER online 123
Sorted Set (ZSet)ZADD ZRANGE ZREVRANGE ZRANGEBYSCORE ZINCRBYLeaderboards (score→rank), priority queues, timeline feeds, rate limitingZADD leaderboard 9200 "loki"

HyperLogLogPFADD PFCOUNT PFMERGEApproximate unique count. 12KB for any cardinality, ~0.81% error. Unique visitors.PFADD visitors "user_abc" / PFCOUNT visitors
StreamXADD XREAD XGROUP XACKAppend-only event log. Consumer groups. Kafka-lite for simpler workloads.XADD events * type click user 123
GeoGEOADD GEOSEARCH GEODISTNearby drivers/restaurants/stores. Location radius queries.GEOADD drivers -122.41 37.77 "driver:1"
Redis examples # Sorted Set leaderboard ZADD leaderboard 9200 "loki" ZADD leaderboard 9500 "thor" ZINCRBY leaderboard 300 "loki" # loki → 9500 (tied with thor) ZREVRANGE leaderboard 0 9 WITHSCORES # top 10 with scores ZRANK leaderboard "loki" # rank (0-indexed from bottom) # Atomic rate limiting — Lua script (no race conditions) EVAL " local count = redis.call('INCR', KEYS[1]) if count == 1 then redis.call('EXPIRE', KEYS[1], 60) end if count > tonumber(ARGV[1]) then return 0 -- rejected end return 1 -- allowed " 1 "rl:user:123:minute" 100 # Geo: find nearby drivers GEOADD drivers -122.4194 37.7749 "driver:1" GEOSEARCH drivers FROMLONLAT -122.41 37.77 BYRADIUS 5 km ASC COUNT 10 WITHCOORD WITHDIST # Pub/Sub for real-time notifications SUBSCRIBE notifications:user:123 # subscriber (blocking) PUBLISH notifications:user:123 '{"type":"like","post":456}' # publisher # Distributed lock (SETNX = SET if Not eXists) SET lock:order:456 "worker-1" NX EX 30 # acquire (NX=only if not exists) # process... DEL lock:order:456 # release

Redis Persistence Options

RDB (Snapshot)AOF (Append-Only File)
HowFork process, write full dataset snapshot every N seconds/writesLog every write command as it executes, fsync periodically
Recovery speedFast — load one binary snapshotSlower — replay all commands from beginning
Data loss riskUp to N minutes (since last snapshot)At most 1 second (appendfsync everysec)
File sizeCompact binaryGrows large — rewrite periodically (BGREWRITEAOF)
Best forBackups, acceptable data loss, fast restartDurability-critical — sessions, user data, distributed locks

Redis Cluster vs Sentinel

Redis SentinelHigh availability for a single Redis primary. Sentinels monitor primary health, auto-promote a replica if primary dies, update clients. No horizontal scaling — all data fits on one node (up to several TB). Simple setup. Right choice when you need HA but not sharding.

Redis ClusterShards data across 16,384 hash slots distributed across N primary nodes (minimum 3). True horizontal scaling — add nodes to increase capacity and throughput. Each node has replicas for HA. Clients must be cluster-aware. Use when: single Redis node is a bottleneck or data doesn't fit in RAM of one machine.
Caching

Memcached

Simple, fast, multi-threaded pure cache. Strings only. No persistence, no replication, no rich data types. But for raw caching throughput, it's extremely efficient.

Memcached vs Redis — Which to Use

FeatureMemcachedRedisChoose When
Data typesString only10+ typesMemcached: pure simple cache. Redis: anything else.
ThreadingMulti-threaded — uses all CPU coresSingle-threaded (I/O threads since v6)Memcached has edge on multi-core for simple GET/SET
PersistenceNone — pure volatileRDB + AOFRedis if you can't afford to lose all data on restart
ReplicationNone — client-side sharding onlyPrimary-replica + ClusterRedis if you need HA without client complexity
Memory overheadLower — simpler internal structuresHigher — rich data structuresMemcached if memory efficiency is critical at extreme scale
Real userFacebook (memcache layer for 100TB+)Twitter, GitHub, Uber, AirbnbFor most systems: Redis. Memcached only at extreme Facebook-scale.
The Honest AnswerFor 99% of systems, choose Redis. It does everything Memcached does plus persistence, pub/sub, sorted sets, Lua scripting, streams, and geo. The only reason to choose Memcached is if you're at Facebook-scale (100TB+ cache) and need maximum multi-threaded CPU efficiency for pure GET/SET operations. Even Facebook uses both.
Messaging

Message Queues

Decouple producers from consumers. Buffer traffic spikes. Guarantee delivery. Enable async processing. One of the most powerful architectural tools for resilient, scalable systems.

Why Message Queues — With Examples

Example: User signs up on your platform

Synchronous (without queue): user waits for everything

Register → send welcome email (300ms) → send Slack to team (100ms) → create Stripe customer (500ms) → return success. Total: ~900ms. If Stripe is down → registration fails. If email server is slow → user waits.

Asynchronous (with queue): user gets instant response

Register → save to DB (10ms) → publish UserRegistered event to queue → return success (10ms total). Queue workers independently: send email, notify Slack, create Stripe customer. User sees instant response. If Stripe is down → it retries automatically until Stripe recovers. Zero impact on registration speed.

Kafka vs RabbitMQ vs SQS — Full Comparison

FeatureApache KafkaRabbitMQAWS SQS
Core modelDistributed commit log — append-only, persistentAMQP message broker — route and deliverManaged cloud queue
Message retentionConfigurable: days or weeks after consumptionUntil consumed or TTL expiresUp to 14 days
Replayability✅ Rewind to any offset, replay history❌ Consumed = gone forever❌
ThroughputMillions of messages/sec per cluster~50K messages/sec~3K/sec standard, ~300/sec FIFO
OrderingGuaranteed within a partitionGuaranteed within a queueBest-effort (FIFO queue: per group)
ComplexityHigh — cluster management, ZooKeeper/KRaftMedium — exchanges, bindingsLow — fully managed
Use caseEvent streaming, audit logs, analytics, microservices event busTask queues, complex routing, RPC, work distributionAWS-native workloads, simple decoupling
The Decision MatrixNeed to replay events? → Kafka. Need complex routing (topic exchange, priority)? → RabbitMQ. Already on AWS, simple use case? → SQS. Building an event-driven microservices platform? → Kafka. Background job processing? → RabbitMQ or SQS. Real-time analytics pipeline? → Kafka.
Messaging

Apache Kafka — Complete Guide

LinkedIn's distributed commit log. Used by Netflix (data pipelines), Uber (surge pricing, real-time matching), LinkedIn (activity tracking), Cloudflare (logs). Learn it inside out.

Core Concepts — With Concrete Numbers

Kafka at Twitter-scale: 347 tweet writes/sec

How we'd design the Kafka setup:

Topic: "tweets" — a named stream of tweet events.
Partitions: 12 partitions for "tweets" topic. Why 12? We have 4 consumer groups (fanout, analytics, search, notifications). Each group can have up to 12 consumers (one per partition). 12 is divisible by 4, 3, 2, 1 — flexible.
Replication Factor: 3. Each partition stored on 3 brokers. Can lose 2 brokers and still serve. At 347 writes/sec × 1KB per tweet → 347KB/sec per partition. Easily handled.
Key: tweet.user_id → routes to partition. All tweets from same user → same partition → ordered writes per user guaranteed.

ConceptWhat it isKey fact
TopicNamed stream of recordsTopics are replicated across brokers for durability
PartitionOrdered, immutable log. Unit of parallelism.Messages within a partition are strictly ordered. Max consumers per group = num partitions.
OffsetEach record's sequential position in its partitionConsumers track their own offset — can replay, rewind, skip
Consumer GroupSet of consumers that collectively read a topicEach partition assigned to exactly 1 consumer per group. Add consumers up to partition count.
Leader/FollowerOne leader per partition handles reads/writes. Followers replicate.On leader failure, a follower auto-promotes. No manual intervention needed.
Python (confluent-kafka) # Producer — key=user_id ensures same user's tweets are ordered from confluent_kafka import Producer producer = Producer({ 'bootstrap.servers': 'kafka-1:9092,kafka-2:9092,kafka-3:9092', 'acks': 'all', # wait for all replicas = zero data loss 'retries': 5, 'linger.ms': 5, # batch messages for 5ms = higher throughput 'compression.type': 'lz4' # compress = ~5× smaller on wire }) producer.produce( topic='tweets', key=str(user_id).encode(), # same user → same partition → ordering value=json.dumps(tweet).encode() ) producer.flush() # Consumer with manual offset commit (at-least-once) from confluent_kafka import Consumer consumer = Consumer({ 'bootstrap.servers': 'kafka-1:9092', 'group.id': 'fanout-workers', # all workers share partitions 'auto.offset.reset': 'earliest', # start from beginning on first run 'enable.auto.commit': False, # CRITICAL: manual commit }) consumer.subscribe(['tweets']) while True: msg = consumer.poll(1.0) if msg is None: continue fanout_to_followers(json.loads(msg.value())) # do work FIRST consumer.commit(msg) # THEN commit offset # If crash between process and commit → re-process → must be idempotent

Delivery Semantics

SemanticHowDuplicates?Use For
At-most-onceCommit offset before processing. Crash → message lost.No, but may lose messagesMetrics, logs where loss is acceptable
At-least-onceCommit offset after processing. Crash → re-process.Possible — design idempotent consumersMost use cases — safe if consumer is idempotent
Exactly-onceKafka transactions + idempotent producerNoFinancial transactions, billing, order processing
Kafka vs Kafka Streams vs FlinkKafka = the storage and transport layer (the highway).
Kafka Streams = Java library for processing Kafka data with stateful aggregations, windowing, joins (the car).
Apache Flink = full stream processing platform with event-time semantics, exactly-once, more powerful than Kafka Streams but more complex (the sports car with GPS).
For simple transformations → Kafka Streams. For complex analytics/ML → Flink.
Messaging

RabbitMQ — Complete Guide

AMQP message broker with flexible routing logic. The workhorse for task queues, work distribution, and complex message routing patterns.

AMQP Core Concepts

Analogy: Post office routing

Producer → Exchange (post office) → Queue(s) → Consumer

Exchange = the post office sorting machine. It reads the routing key (like the address on a letter) and decides which queue(s) to put it in.
Queue = the specific mailbox. Consumers read from queues.
Binding = the routing rule connecting exchange to queue ("letters addressed to London go in box 3").

Exchange Types — All 4

TypeRouting RuleExample Use Case
DirectRoute to queue where binding key EXACTLY matches routing keyTask distribution: routing_key="pdf_process" → goes to pdf-worker queue only
FanoutRoute to ALL bound queues, ignoring routing keyBroadcast: OrderPlaced → all of: email-queue, inventory-queue, analytics-queue
TopicPattern matching with * (one word) and # (zero or more words)"orders.*.eu" matches "orders.created.eu" and "orders.cancelled.eu". "logs.#" matches anything starting with logs.
HeadersRoute based on message header attributes (not routing key)Route by content-type, priority, region — when routing key alone isn't expressive enough
Python (pika) import pika, json # Producer channel.exchange_declare(exchange='orders', exchange_type='topic', durable=True) channel.basic_publish( exchange='orders', routing_key='orders.created.eu', # matches "orders.#" and "*.created.*" body=json.dumps(order), properties=pika.BasicProperties( delivery_mode=2, # persistent: survives RabbitMQ restart priority=5 # 0-10, higher = processed first ) ) # Consumer — manual ack = at-least-once def process(ch, method, props, body): order = json.loads(body) try: fulfill_order(order) ch.basic_ack(method.delivery_tag) # success → remove from queue except Exception: ch.basic_nack(method.delivery_tag, requeue=False) # failed → send to DLQ channel.basic_qos(prefetch_count=1) # fair dispatch: don't give worker 2nd msg until 1st acked channel.basic_consume('order-fulfillment', process)
Dead Letter Queue (DLX)Configure a Dead Letter Exchange on any queue. Messages that: (1) are nacked with requeue=False, (2) exceed max retries, (3) expire (TTL), or (4) exceed max-length → routed to DLX → land in your dead letter queue. Monitor DLQ — it's your error inbox. Alert when DLQ size grows unexpectedly. Inspect failures, fix, re-queue for reprocessing.
Messaging

Stream Processing

Real-time processing of continuous data streams. Unlike batch (process yesterday's logs at 2am), stream processing handles events milliseconds after they occur.

Batch vs Stream

Example: Detecting credit card fraud

Batch processing (every night at 2am):

Analyse yesterday's transactions. Find anomalies. Send fraud alerts... for charges that happened 20 hours ago. Card has been maxed out for 20 hours. Too late.

Stream processing (real-time):

Transaction arrives → stream processor checks against user's historical patterns within 50ms → anomaly detected → card blocked → text sent to user. All before the fraudulent merchant sees approval. This is why Stripe, Visa, Mastercard all use stream processing for fraud detection.

Key Stream Processing Concepts

ConceptExplanationExample
WindowingAggregate events over a time windowCount page views per 5-minute window
Tumbling WindowFixed, non-overlapping windows0–60s, 60–120s, 120–180s (each event in exactly one window)
Sliding WindowRolling window computed frequently"Last 60 seconds" recomputed every 10 seconds
Session WindowGroup events by user activity with gap timeoutGroup user's page views until 30min idle = session end
Event Time vs Processing TimeEvent time = when event happened. Processing time = when it arrived.Mobile app event sent at 3pm, arrives at server at 3:05pm (offline user)
WatermarkSignal "we've seen all events up to time T, safe to compute window"Allow 5min late arrivals before finalising a window
Stateful ProcessingMaintain state across events (running totals, session data)Track user's running cart total across events
Tool GuideKafka Streams: Java library on top of Kafka. Simple joins, aggregations, windowing. No separate cluster needed — runs inside your app. Great for microservices that process their own Kafka events.
Apache Flink: Full stream processing cluster. Event-time semantics, exactly-once, complex CEP (complex event processing), ML integration. Used at Alibaba (1T events/day), Uber.
Apache Spark Streaming: Micro-batch streaming — not truly real-time (latency in seconds), but massive scale and unified batch+stream API. Good for analytics at scale.
Architecture

Microservices Architecture

Decompose a system into small, independent services each owning its data. Powerful at scale, complex everywhere. Know when it's right and how to do it right.

Monolith vs Microservices

Example: A team of 5 engineers vs a team of 500

Why Netflix switched from monolith to microservices:

In 2008, Netflix had one massive Java monolith. A single bad deploy could take down everything. 500 engineers couldn't deploy independently — merge conflicts, coordination overhead, release trains.
Solution: Split into 700+ microservices. Each team owns their service end-to-end. User service deploys 50 times a day without waiting for anyone else. Recommendation service can be rewritten in a different language without touching payments.
Cost: 700 services to monitor, network calls instead of in-process, distributed tracing needed, service discovery required. This complexity is only worth it at Netflix's scale and team size.

DimensionMonolithMicroservices
DeployOne unit — entire app deployed per changeEach service deploys independently, own release cycle
Team size sweet spot≤20 engineersScales with team growth (Conway's Law)
DatabaseShared DB — joins easyEach service owns its DB — NO shared DB access
LatencyIn-process calls: nanosecondsNetwork calls between services: milliseconds
When to useStartups, early stage, ≤$10M ARR, small teamAfter monolith shows specific, felt scaling pain
The Distributed Monolith Anti-PatternServices that must deploy together, share a database, or chain synchronous calls are a "distributed monolith" — all the complexity of microservices with none of the independence. Signs: one service change requires changing 3 others, services share database tables, synchronous call chains 5+ deep. Fix before it kills you: enforce ownership boundaries, switch to async events, database per service.
Microservices architecture — e-commerce example
graph TB Client --> GW["API Gateway\n(Auth, Rate Limit, Routing)"] GW --> US["User Service\n(Postgres)"] GW --> PS["Product Service\n(Elasticsearch)"] GW --> OS["Order Service\n(Postgres)"] GW --> CS["Cart Service\n(Redis)"] OS -->|"OrderPlaced event"| K["Kafka"] K --> PAY["Payment Service\n(Postgres)"] K --> INV["Inventory Service\n(Postgres)"] K --> NS["Notification Service\n(sends email/SMS)"] style GW fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style K fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc
Architecture

API Design — REST, GraphQL, gRPC

The contract between your service and its clients. Get it right and it's stable for years. Get it wrong and every change is a breaking change. Know the trade-offs between all three paradigms.

REST Design Best Practices

REST API design # Resources are nouns. HTTP verbs are actions. GET /v2/posts/123 # 200 {post} POST /v2/posts # 201 {new post} PATCH /v2/posts/123 # 200 {updated post} — partial update DELETE /v2/posts/123 # 204 No Content # Nested resources — max 2 levels deep GET /v2/users/123/posts # user's posts GET /v2/posts/456/comments # post's comments # Cursor pagination — NEVER OFFSET at scale GET /v2/posts?limit=20&cursor=eyJpZCI6MTIzfQ # Response: {"data":[...], "next_cursor":"eyJpZCI6MTQzfQ", "has_more": true} # Cursor encodes last seen item. O(log n) always. OFFSET is O(n). # Idempotency key — safe to retry on network failure POST /v2/payments Idempotency-Key: "client-generated-uuid-once" # Same key → same response, no double charge. Store in DB: (idem_key, response) # Standard error format { "error": { "code": "INSUFFICIENT_FUNDS", # machine-readable "message": "Account balance too low", # human-readable "request_id": "req_abc123xyz" # for log correlation } }

HTTP Status Codes — Use Correctly

CodeWhen to Use It
200 OKGET/PUT/PATCH success with response body
201 CreatedPOST that creates a resource — include Location header with new URL
204 No ContentDELETE success, or PATCH where you don't need to return body
400 Bad RequestInvalid input: missing required field, wrong type, validation failure
401 UnauthorizedNot authenticated — no valid token or session
403 ForbiddenAuthenticated but no permission — editing someone else's resource
404 Not FoundResource doesn't exist at this URL
409 ConflictDuplicate email on register, concurrent edit conflict, version mismatch
429 Too Many RequestsRate limited — always include Retry-After header
500 Internal ErrorServer bug — never leak stack traces to clients
503 UnavailableCircuit breaker open, maintenance, overloaded

REST vs GraphQL Trade-offs

Example: Rendering a Twitter profile page

REST: 3 separate requests needed

GET /users/123 → user info
GET /users/123/tweets?limit=10 → recent tweets
GET /users/123/stats → follower count, like count
3 network round trips. Mobile users on slow connections wait for all 3.

GraphQL: 1 request, exactly what you need

query { user(id:123) { name, bio, tweets(first:10) { content, likes }, stats { followers } } }
One request, returns only the fields needed. No over-fetching. Mobile saves bandwidth. But: N+1 problem if not using DataLoader, caching is harder (all POST to /graphql).

Architecture

Event-Driven Architecture & CQRS

Services communicate via events instead of direct calls. Maximum decoupling, natural scalability, full audit trail. Event Sourcing + CQRS = the most powerful read optimisation pattern.

Event Sourcing — State as a Log of Events

Example: Bank account in Event Sourcing vs traditional

Traditional: store current state

Table: accounts(id, balance). Balance = 850. Questions you can't answer: How did we get here? Was there fraud? Can I show the transaction history? Answer: can't — you only have the current state.

Event Sourcing: store every event

event log events for account_123: AccountOpened { deposit: 1000 } → balance: 1000 MoneyWithdrawn { amount: 200 } → balance: 800 MoneyDeposited { amount: 150 } → balance: 950 MoneyWithdrawn { amount: 100 } → balance: 850 (current state) // Benefits: // Full audit trail — regulators love it // Time travel — replay to any point in time // Debug by replaying exact sequence of events // New projections — build a new read model from history // Example: "what was the balance on Jan 15th?" = replay to that date

CQRS — Separate Read and Write Models

CQRS separates write path (consistency) from read path (performance)
graph LR Client --> CMD["Command Handler\n(Write side — ACID, normalised)"] CMD --> EStore["Event Store\n(append-only)"] EStore --> Proj["Projectors\n(async workers)"] Proj --> ES["Elasticsearch\n(search view)"] Proj --> Redis2["Redis\n(hot data view)"] Proj --> PG["Postgres\n(reports view)"] ES & Redis2 & PG --> QH["Query Handler\n(Read side — fast, denormalised)"] QH --> Client style EStore fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style Proj fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc
Why CQRS is PowerfulWrite side: normalised for integrity (no duplication, ACID, constraints). Read side: denormalised, pre-joined, pre-aggregated per query pattern. Scale reads independently (read traffic = 100× write traffic). Each read model optimised differently — Elasticsearch for search, Redis for hot feed, Postgres for reports. Trade-off: eventual consistency between write and read models (seconds of lag).

Saga Pattern — Distributed Transactions Without 2PC

Example: E-commerce checkout across 3 services

Goal: charge payment + reserve inventory + create shipment — all or nothing

Choreography Saga (event-driven):
Order Service emits "OrderCreated" → Payment Service listens, charges card, emits "PaymentCharged" → Inventory Service listens, reserves stock, emits "StockReserved" → Shipping Service creates shipment.
If Payment fails → emit "PaymentFailed" → Inventory hears it → unreserve stock → Order hears it → cancel order.
No central coordinator. Each service reacts to events. Maximum decoupling.

Architecture

Serverless & Edge Computing

Functions as a Service. No servers to manage. Pay per millisecond of execution. Scales to zero. When the use case fits, nothing beats the operational simplicity.

When Serverless Shines

Perfect serverless use case: image processing pipeline

User uploads profile photo → needs to be resized to 5 sizes + thumbnailed + NSFW-scanned

Traditional (always-on server): Keep an image processing server running 24/7. At 3am, you get 10 uploads — server is idle 99.9% of the time but you pay constantly.
Serverless (Lambda): S3 upload → triggers Lambda function → resizes in parallel → stores results. Zero infrastructure. Zero cost at 3am. Scales automatically from 1 to 10,000 concurrent images without any configuration. Pay only for the milliseconds of processing.

AspectServerless (Lambda)Containers (K8s)VMs (EC2)
Cost modelPay per ms of execution (zero when idle)Pay for running containers (idle still costs)Pay for instances always
Cold start50ms–2s on first invoke after idleNone (always warm if replicas running)None
Max duration15min (Lambda), 60min (Cloud Functions v2)UnlimitedUnlimited
StateStateless — must use external storeCan be statefulStateful
Best forEvent-driven, APIs, ETL, webhooks, scheduled jobsLong-running services, stateful appsLegacy, BYOS

Cold Start Problem & Solutions

Cold Start ExampleLambda function not invoked for 15 minutes → container terminated. Next request: AWS must provision new container, load your code, warm up runtime → 500ms–2s overhead. For a P99 SLA of 100ms, a 2s cold start is catastrophic.

Solutions: (1) Provisioned Concurrency — pay to keep N containers permanently warm. (2) Scheduled ping every 5min (CloudWatch Events → trigger Lambda) — cheap but fragile. (3) Keep deployment package tiny (<5MB). (4) Use Lambda SnapStart for Java — snapshots runtime state.

Edge Computing — Serverless at the CDN Layer

Cloudflare Workers / Vercel Edge FunctionsRun JavaScript/WASM at 300+ CDN edge locations worldwide. Cold start: <5ms (V8 isolates, not containers). In practice: no cold start. Latency: <1ms to execute + 5ms to nearest user = <6ms total.
Use for: A/B testing at edge (no round trip to origin), auth token validation, geo-based redirects, request transformation, personalisation — all without hitting your origin servers. This is the future of low-latency web serving.
Architecture

Containers & Kubernetes

Docker containerises applications. Kubernetes orchestrates them at scale. The industry standard for deploying microservices. Know the primitives — they come up in every backend interview.

Why Containers?

The "works on my machine" problem — solved by Docker

Before Docker:

"It works on my MacBook (Python 3.11, libpq 14.2, some env vars) but fails in production (Python 3.8, libpq 12.4, different env vars)." Debugging environment differences took days.

With Docker:

Package your app + exact Python version + exact library versions + env vars + OS layer into an image. Run the same image everywhere: dev laptop, CI/CD, staging, production. If it works in the image, it works everywhere. The environment is the package.

Kubernetes Core Primitives

ObjectWhat it doesAnalogy
