PodRuns 1+ containers sharing network + storage. Ephemeral — can die any time.One instance of your app
DeploymentDeclares desired Pod count + template. Manages rolling updates, rollbacks.Manager ensuring N workers always running
StatefulSetLike Deployment but pods have stable names (pod-0, pod-1) + persistent storage.Named, persistent workers — for databases
ServiceStable IP + DNS name for a set of pods. Load-balances across pod IPs.Reception desk routing to any available worker
IngressHTTP/HTTPS routing rules → routes to Services. Nginx/Traefik typically.Front door with directory of internal offices
HPAHorizontal Pod Autoscaler — scales replicas based on CPU/memory/custom metrics.Manager who hires more workers when queue is long
ConfigMapNon-sensitive config stored in cluster. Injected as env vars or volumes.Company policy document
SecretBase64-encoded sensitive data. Use external-secrets or Vault in production.Company safe — for passwords/keys
kubernetes deployment.yaml apiVersion: apps/v1 kind: Deployment metadata: { name: api-service } spec: replicas: 3 strategy: type: RollingUpdate rollingUpdate: { maxSurge: 1, maxUnavailable: 0 } # zero-downtime deploy template: spec: containers: - name: api image: registry/api:v1.2.3 resources: requests: { cpu: "100m", memory: "128Mi" } # scheduling limits: { cpu: "500m", memory: "512Mi" } # cap — OOM if exceeded readinessProbe: httpGet: { path: /health, port: 3000 } # no traffic until ready initialDelaySeconds: 5
Reliability

Reliability Patterns

Circuit breakers, bulkheads, retries, timeouts, graceful degradation. These patterns prevent one failing service from cascading and killing your entire distributed system.

Circuit Breaker — With a Real Example

Example: Payment service calling a slow third-party fraud API

Without circuit breaker:

Fraud API goes down at 3pm. Every payment request waits 30s for timeout. 100 req/s × 30s timeout = 3,000 requests in-flight simultaneously, all holding threads. Thread pool exhausted. Payment service hangs. User-facing "place order" button returns error. Entire checkout flow broken for 30+ minutes.

With circuit breaker:

Fraud API down → 10 requests fail quickly → circuit opens. Next requests: circuit is OPEN → fail immediately (5ms) → fallback: "allow payment but flag for manual fraud review later." Service keeps working. Fraud API recovers → circuit probes → success → closes. Users barely notice.

Circuit breaker state machine
stateDiagram-v2 [*] --> Closed Closed --> Open: failure_rate > 50% in 10s window Open --> HalfOpen: sleep window expires (30s) HalfOpen --> Closed: probe succeeds ✅ HalfOpen --> Open: probe fails ❌ Closed: CLOSED — Normal\nTrack failure rate Open: OPEN — Fail Fast\nReturn error immediately\nSave threads & connections HalfOpen: HALF-OPEN — Probe Mode\nAllow 1 test request

Full Resilience Toolkit

PatternWhat it doesExample config
TimeoutFail fast instead of waiting forever. Frees threads for other requests.Cache: 50ms. Internal API: 200ms. External API: 2s.
Retry + BackoffRetry with increasing delay. Jitter prevents thundering herd on recovery.Retry 3×, wait 1s, 2s, 4s + random 0–500ms
Circuit BreakerOpen when failure rate exceeds threshold. Fail fast. Probe recovery.Open at 50% errors in 10s, probe after 30s
BulkheadIsolate thread pools per dependency. Slow upstream can't starve others.20 threads for fraud API, 50 threads for payment API
FallbackWhen upstream fails, return degraded response instead of error.Recommendations fail → return popular items list
Load SheddingReject low-priority requests when overloaded. Protect core functionality.Drop analytics events, serve profile pages, keep checkout working
Python (tenacity) from tenacity import retry, stop_after_attempt, wait_exponential, wait_random @retry( stop=stop_after_attempt(3), wait=wait_exponential(min=1, max=8) + wait_random(0, 0.5), # jitter reraise=True ) def call_payment_api(order): return stripe.charge(order) # retries 3× on failure with backoff # Idempotency key: make retries safe def idempotent_charge(order_id, amount): key = f"charge:{order_id}" if not redis.set(key, "processing", nx=True, ex=300): return redis.get(f"result:{order_id}") # already processed result = stripe.charge(amount=amount, idempotency_key=order_id) redis.setex(f"result:{order_id}", 300, json.dumps(result)) return result
Reliability

Rate Limiting

Control how many requests a client can make in a time window. Essential for API security, cost control, fair resource allocation, and DDoS protection.

All 4 Algorithms — With Math

Token Bucket — Most Common

Tokens added at rate R per second. Max bucket size B. Each request costs 1 token.

Example: B=100 (burst), R=10 tokens/sec (sustained rate).
User does nothing for 10 seconds → bucket fills to 100 tokens.
User then sends 100 requests in 1 second → all allowed (burst!).
User sends request #101 → bucket empty → rejected.
Wait 0.1 seconds (100ms) → 1 new token added → next request allowed.
Sustained rate: 10 req/sec. Burst: up to 100 req. Used by: AWS API Gateway, Stripe, GitHub.

Redis Lua (atomic — no race conditions) EVAL " local bucket = KEYS[1] local max_tokens = tonumber(ARGV[1]) -- B local refill_rate = tonumber(ARGV[2]) -- R per second local now = tonumber(ARGV[3]) -- current unix ms local last = tonumber(redis.call('HGET',bucket,'last') or now) local tokens = tonumber(redis.call('HGET',bucket,'tokens') or max_tokens) local elapsed = (now - last) / 1000 -- seconds elapsed tokens = math.min(max_tokens, tokens + elapsed * refill_rate) if tokens < 1 then return 0 end -- reject redis.call('HMSET', bucket, 'tokens', tokens-1, 'last', now) redis.call('EXPIRE', bucket, 3600) return 1 -- allow " 1 "rl:user:123" 100 10 {unix_ms}
Leaky Bucket — Smooth Output Rate

Requests enter a queue. Processed at a fixed rate R. Queue max size B. Overflow = reject.

Example: B=100 (queue size), R=10 req/sec output.
100 requests arrive at once → all queue up.
Processed at 10/sec → takes 10 seconds to drain the queue.
During those 10 seconds, any new request sees queue full → rejected.
Key difference from Token Bucket: Leaky Bucket smooths output (constant 10/sec no matter what). Token Bucket allows bursts.
Best for: network traffic shaping, protecting a backend that can only handle steady load.

Fixed Window Counter — Simple but has a vulnerability

Count requests per fixed time window. Reset at window boundary.

Example: Limit = 100 req/minute. Windows: [0s–60s], [60s–120s]…
Implementation: key = f"rl:{user}:{int(time/60)}", INCR the key, EXPIRE after 60s.
Boundary burst attack: User sends 100 req at second :59 (end of window 1) → allowed. User sends 100 req at second :00 (start of window 2) → also allowed. Result: 200 requests in 2 seconds despite 100/min limit. Classic attack vector.

Sliding Window Log/Counter — Best Accuracy

Track requests in a rolling window. No boundary burst problem.

Log approach: Store timestamp of each request in a sorted set. Count entries in last 60s. O(requests) memory per user.
Counter approach (hybrid): Two fixed windows (current + previous). Weighted count = prev_count × (1 - elapsed_fraction) + curr_count.
Example: Limit 100/min. At 35s into current minute: elapsed = 35/60 = 58%. Count = prev_count × (1-0.58) + curr_count = 40×0.42 + 25 = 41.8 ≈ 42. Under 100 → allow.
~1% inaccuracy. Low memory. Used by Cloudflare, most production systems.

Rate Limiting Best PracticesAlways return: 429 Too Many Requests with Retry-After: 30, X-RateLimit-Limit: 100, X-RateLimit-Remaining: 0, X-RateLimit-Reset: 1700000060. Identify clients by: API key > user ID > IP (IP alone breaks behind NAT). Different limits per endpoint tier: auth endpoints 5/min, reads 1000/min, writes 100/min. Use Lua scripts in Redis for atomic operations — no race conditions possible.
Reliability

Security Patterns

Authentication, authorization, encryption, common attacks. Security must be designed in — cannot be bolted on. These patterns protect millions of users' data.

Authentication Patterns

JWT — Stateless Authentication

Structure: header.payload.signature — all base64url encoded

Header: {"alg":"HS256","typ":"JWT"}. Payload: {"sub":"user123","role":"admin","exp":1700001200}. Signature: HMAC-SHA256(header.payload, secret).

Key insight: the signature means no one can tamper with the payload without knowing the secret. Server validates signature — no database lookup needed. Perfect for stateless microservices.

Security rules: Store access token in JavaScript memory (not localStorage — XSS readable). Store refresh token in HttpOnly + Secure + SameSite=Strict cookie (JavaScript cannot read HttpOnly cookies). Short-lived access tokens (15 min). Long-lived refresh tokens (30 days, rotated).

JWT refresh token flow
sequenceDiagram Client->>Auth: POST /login {email, password} Auth->>Auth: bcrypt.compare(password, hash) Auth-->>Client: {access_token:JWT(15min), refresh_token:JWT(30d) in HttpOnly cookie} Client->>API: GET /me\nAuthorization: Bearer access_token API->>API: verify signature — no DB call! API-->>Client: user data ✅ Note over Client,API: 15min later — access token expires Client->>Auth: POST /refresh (cookie auto-sent by browser) Auth-->>Client: new access_token
OAuth 2.0 — "Login with Google" Flow

Authorization Code + PKCE (mandatory for SPAs and mobile)

1. "Login with Google" clicked → redirect to Google with: client_id, redirect_uri, scope=email profile, state=random_csrf_token, code_challenge=sha256(random_verifier)
2. User approves → Google redirects back: ?code=auth_code&state=csrf_token
3. Verify state matches → backend calls Google: POST /token {code, code_verifier, client_secret} — server-to-server, secret never exposed to browser
4. Google returns access_token + id_token → extract user info
PKCE prevents malicious apps from stealing the authorization code.

Server-side Sessions — Traditional, Instant Revocation
Python # On login session_id = secrets.token_urlsafe(32) # cryptographically random redis.setex(f"session:{session_id}", 86400, json.dumps(user)) response.set_cookie("sid", session_id, httponly=True, secure=True, samesite="Strict") # On each request user = redis.get(f"session:{request.cookies['sid']}") # Instant revocation — just delete the Redis key redis.delete(f"session:{session_id}") # JWT can't be revoked (until expiry). Sessions can. Use sessions for security-critical apps.
API Keys — Server-to-Server Authentication

Format: sk_live_[32 random bytes in base64url]

Prefix (sk_live_, pk_test_) enables instant identification in logs — you know what kind of key it is without checking the DB.
Storage: hash with SHA-256 before storing. User sees key ONCE at creation. If DB is leaked, attackers can't reverse the hash to get actual keys.
Features: per-key scopes (read-only, write), per-key rate limits, last-used-at tracking, audit log of all requests, instant revocation (delete the row).

Top Vulnerabilities — Every Engineer Must Know

AttackHow it worksDefence
SQL InjectionUser input includes SQL code: '; DROP TABLE users; --Parameterised queries always. ORMs handle this. Never string-concat SQL.
XSS (Cross-Site Scripting)Inject <script> into page that other users see. Steals cookies/tokens.Content-Security-Policy header, sanitise output, HttpOnly cookies (JS can't read)
CSRFTrick browser into making authenticated request to your site from malicious siteSameSite=Strict cookie, CSRF token in form, check Origin header
IDORGET /orders/1234 shows other user's order by changing IDAlways filter by ownership: WHERE id=? AND user_id=current_user
Secrets in CodeAPI keys committed to git repo — scraped by bots within secondsEnvironment variables, AWS Secrets Manager, git-secrets pre-commit hook
Weak PasswordsBrute force or credential stuffing with leaked password listsbcrypt/argon2 (not MD5/SHA1), rate limit auth, MFA, check against HaveIBeenPwned
Reliability

Monitoring & Observability

You can't fix what you can't see. Three pillars give complete visibility. The difference between a 5-minute and a 5-hour production incident is how fast you can find the root cause.

The Three Pillars

Example: "Checkout is slow" — how each pillar helps

Metrics (Prometheus + Grafana): "What is broken?"

Dashboard shows: checkout API P99 latency jumped from 80ms to 2000ms at 3:04pm. Error rate 0.1% → 15%. QPS unchanged → not less traffic. Payment service error rate also up → downstream issue.

Traces (Jaeger/OpenTelemetry): "Where is the time being spent?"

Click on a slow trace: checkout API (50ms) → payment service (1800ms) → fraud API (1750ms). Fraud API is the bottleneck. 99% of the 2000ms is spent waiting for fraud API.

Logs (ELK/Loki): "What exactly happened?"

Filter logs for fraud API. See: "connection timeout after 1500ms to fraud-api.external.com". External fraud vendor is having an outage. Root cause found in 3 minutes.

PillarWhat it answersTools
Metrics (numbers over time)What is happening? Is it abnormal?Prometheus, Grafana, Datadog, CloudWatch
Logs (timestamped events)What exactly happened for this request?ELK Stack, Loki + Grafana, Splunk
Traces (request journey)Why is this specific request slow? Which service?Jaeger, Zipkin, AWS X-Ray, OpenTelemetry
RED Method — Monitor Every ServiceR = Rate (requests per second)
E = Errors (error rate as %)
D = Duration (latency P50, P95, P99)

Track these 3 for every service. Alert on: P99 > SLA. Error rate > 1%. QPS drop >20% (broken clients stop sending — silent failure). This catches 90% of production incidents.

SLI / SLO / Error Budget

example SLO definition ## checkout API SLOs SLI: availability = successful_requests / total_requests SLO: availability >= 99.9% (rolling 30 days) Error budget: (1 - 0.999) × 43200 minutes = 43.2 minutes/month allowed downtime SLI: checkout_p99_latency SLO: p99_latency <= 500ms (rolling 7 days) ## Policy: ## When error budget < 20% remaining: freeze risky deploys ## When error budget > 80% remaining: can move fast with experiments ## Error budgets replace "dev wants features" vs "ops wants stability" arguments
OpenTelemetry (OTel) — Instrument Once, Export AnywhereVendor-neutral instrumentation framework. Instrument your code once with OTel SDK. Export to: Jaeger, Grafana Tempo, Datadog, Honeycomb, AWS X-Ray — your choice, switchable without code changes. Auto-instrumentation for Flask, Django, Express, Spring Boot — zero code changes, traces just appear. Industry standard. Use it.
Real-World Design

Design a URL Shortener

The classic system design starter. Deceptively simple on the surface — deeply interesting under the hood. Teaches ID generation, redirection mechanics, and read-heavy caching.

Requirements & Estimation

Clarified requirements

Functional: Shorten URL, redirect to original, custom aliases, expiry, click analytics

Scale assumptions: 100M new URLs/day, 10B redirects/day (100:1 read/write ratio)
Write QPS = 100M ÷ 86,400 ≈ 1,157 writes/sec (design for 3K peak)
Read QPS = 10B ÷ 86,400 ≈ 115,740 redirects/sec (design for 350K peak)
Storage = 100M × 500 bytes (URL + metadata) × 365 days × 5 years = 91 TB

URL ID Generation

Base62 — The Standard Approach

Characters: [0-9][a-z][A-Z] = 62 chars. 7-char code = 62^7 = 3.5 trillion unique URLs

Process: Generate unique integer ID (from DB auto-increment or distributed counter) → convert to Base62.
Math: ID = 125,000,000 → divide repeatedly by 62, collect remainders.
125,000,000 ÷ 62 = 2,016,129 rem 2 → 125,000,000 in Base62 = "BL5vB"
Pros: URL-safe, short, easy to decode back to ID, globally unique if source ID is unique.
Cons: Sequential IDs = predictable (abc001, abc002) — attackers enumerate. Fix: shuffle the ID before encoding.

Python CHARS = '0123456789abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyzABCDEFGHIJKLMNOPQRSTUVWXYZ' def to_base62(n): if n == 0: return '0' result = '' while n: result = CHARS[n % 62] + result n //= 62 return result to_base62(125000000) # → "BL5vB"
Hash-based

hash(long_url) → take first 7 chars → check collision → store

Process: MD5(url) = 128-bit hash → take first 43 bits → Base62 → 7 chars.
Pros: Same URL always gives same short code (idempotent) — no duplication if user shortens same URL twice.
Cons: Hash collision risk (two different URLs → same 7 chars). Must check DB and handle collision (append random suffix, retry). At 100M URLs: collision probability with 7 chars ≈ 0.001% — rare but must handle.

Snowflake ID (Twitter's approach)

64-bit ID = 41 bits timestamp + 10 bits machine ID + 12 bits sequence

Pros: Globally unique, sortable by time, no coordination between nodes, 4096 IDs/ms/node.
Math: 41 bits of milliseconds = 69 years before overflow. 10 bits machine ID = 1024 machines. 12 bits sequence = 4096 per ms per machine. Total capacity: 4096 × 1024 × 86,400,000ms/day = 363 billion IDs/day. More than enough.
Convert Snowflake ID to Base62 → 7–11 char short code.

301 vs 302 Redirect — This Matters for Caching

301 Permanent302 Temporary
Browser caches?Yes — browser caches forever, won't hit your servers againNo — every redirect goes through your servers
Analytics❌ Can't track clicks after browser caches it✅ Every click goes through your servers → can count
Server loadLow — cached requests skip your serversHigh — every redirect hits your servers
Use whenNo analytics needed, maximise performanceAnalytics required (bit.ly uses this)

High-Level Architecture

URL shortener architecture
graph TB U["User browser"] --> CDN["CDN\n(cache popular short URLs)"] CDN -->|"MISS"| LB["Load Balancer"] LB --> API["URL Service\n(stateless)"] API -->|"Read: GET short→long"| Cache["Redis Cache\n(hot URLs, 99% hit rate)"] API -->|"Cache miss"| DB["PostgreSQL\n(all URL mappings)"] API -->|"Write: create URL"| DB API -->|"click event"| MQ["Kafka\n(analytics events)"] MQ --> ANS["Analytics Service\n(click counts, geo)"] style Cache fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style DB fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Database Schema

SQL CREATE TABLE urls ( id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY, -- Snowflake ID short_code VARCHAR(11) UNIQUE NOT NULL, -- Base62 of id long_url TEXT NOT NULL, user_id BIGINT, -- NULL = anonymous created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), expires_at TIMESTAMPTZ, -- NULL = never is_active BOOLEAN DEFAULT TRUE ); CREATE INDEX idx_short_code ON urls (short_code); -- redirect lookup CREATE INDEX idx_user_created ON urls (user_id, created_at); -- user's URLs -- Redis cache structure -- Key: "url:abc1234" Value: "https://long.original.url.com/path?q=1" TTL: 86400s -- 99% of redirects served from Redis — DB barely touched
Real-World Design

Design Twitter / X Feed

The hardest feed design problem. 300M DAU, each following hundreds of accounts. The core challenge: how do you deliver personalised timeline at low latency? The answer is fanout.

The Core Problem: Fanout

Fanout at Twitter-scale

Cristiano Ronaldo (600M followers) tweets. What happens next?

The naive approach (pull model): Each user's timeline is computed on-demand: "get all tweets from people I follow, sorted by time." For a user following 500 people → 500 DB reads per timeline load. At 300M DAU each loading timeline 10 times/day = 1.5 trillion DB reads/day. Impossible.
The scalable approach (push/fanout model): When Ronaldo tweets → pre-compute which timelines to update → write tweet ID to all 600M followers' timeline cache. Timeline load = 1 Redis read. 300× cheaper per read, but 600M writes per tweet.

Fanout Strategies

Push Model — Pre-compute timelines

On write: iterate over all followers, write tweet_id to each follower's timeline cache

✅ Timeline read = single Redis list read. Milliseconds, constant cost regardless of following count.
❌ Writing takes time proportional to follower count. Ronaldo's tweet → 600M Redis writes → takes minutes even at 1M writes/sec. Celebrity tweets cause massive write spikes.
❌ Wastes space for inactive users (wrote to their timeline, they never looked).

Pull Model — Compute on read

On read: get all accounts user follows, get their recent tweets, merge and sort

✅ No fanout write cost. Celebrity tweets are trivially cheap on write — just store the tweet once.
❌ Read is expensive: N+1 queries (N = number of accounts followed), sorting N×recent_tweets.
❌ User follows 5,000 accounts → 5,000 DB reads per timeline load. At P99, this is hundreds of milliseconds.

Hybrid Model — Twitter's actual solution

Push for regular users (≤10K followers). Pull for celebrities (>10K followers).

Regular user tweets: Fanout to all followers' timeline caches immediately. Fast because few followers.
Celebrity tweets: Store tweet once. Don't fanout. On timeline load: merge pre-computed timeline (from regular follows) + real-time fetch of celebrity tweets the user follows → sort and merge.
Math: 99.9% of users have fewer than 10K followers → push covers almost everyone. The 0.1% with >10K followers → pull prevents write amplification catastrophe.
Timeline cache: Redis Sorted Set per user. Score = tweet timestamp. Value = tweet_id. ZREVRANGE → latest tweets in order.

Full Architecture

Twitter high-level architecture
graph TB Client --> LB["Load Balancer"] LB --> TS["Tweet Service"] LB --> TL["Timeline Service"] TS -->|"store tweet"| TDB["Tweet DB\n(MySQL sharded by tweet_id)"] TS -->|"tweet event"| K["Kafka\n('tweet' topic)"] K --> FW["Fanout Workers\n(push to follower timelines)"] FW -->|"ZADD timeline:{user_id}"| TC["Timeline Cache\n(Redis Cluster)"] TL -->|"ZREVRANGE"| TC TL -->|"cache miss / celebrity"| TDB style TC fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style K fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Trending Topics — Count-Min Sketch

How trending works with probabilistic countingExact counting of every hashtag mention at Twitter scale requires too much memory. Count-Min Sketch: a 2D array of counters + multiple hash functions. For each tweet, hash the hashtag with K different hash functions → increment K positions in the array. To read count: hash with same K functions → take minimum of K positions (overcounts cancel out).
Uses ~1KB for millions of distinct items with ~1% error. Way more efficient than exact counting with a HashMap. Top-N trending hashtags: apply Count-Min Sketch → sort by count → take top N.
Real-World Design

Design YouTube

Video streaming at scale. 500 hours of video uploaded per minute. 2B users watching. The key challenges: upload pipeline, video transcoding, adaptive bitrate streaming, CDN delivery.

Requirements & Scale

Estimation

Upload: 500 hours/min. Watch: 1B hours/day.

Upload bandwidth: 500 hrs/min × 60min/hr × 1GB/hr (raw HD) ≈ 30 GB/s inbound
Storage (transcoded): 500 hrs/min × 60 × 24 × 365 = 262M hours/year × 3 bitrates × 1GB/hr = 786 PB/year
→ Must use lossy compression heavily. Real average storage: ~1GB/hr after transcoding.

Upload Pipeline

Video upload → processing → delivery pipeline
graph LR U["User uploads\nvideo file"] --> API["Upload API\n(chunked upload)"] API --> Raw["Raw Storage\n(S3)"] Raw -->|"trigger"| TQ["Transcoding\nQueue (Kafka)"] TQ --> TW["Transcoding Workers\n(FFmpeg clusters)"] TW -->|"360p, 720p, 1080p, 4K"| CDN2["CDN Storage\n(multiple qualities)"] TW -->|"metadata"| VDB["Video DB\n(PostgreSQL)"] TW -->|"thumbnail frames"| TS2["Thumbnail Service"] CDN2 --> CDN["CDN Edge\n(Akamai / CloudFront)"] CDN --> WU["Watching Users"] style TW fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style CDN fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Chunked Upload — Resumable

Why chunked upload mattersA 2GB raw video file upload over slow/unreliable connection. If the connection drops at 99% → user must restart the entire upload from scratch.

Chunked upload: split into 5MB chunks. Upload each chunk independently. If connection drops → resume from last successful chunk. Server reassembles after all chunks arrive. Google's resumable upload API (used by YouTube) works exactly this way.
chunked upload flow # Step 1: Initiate upload POST /uploads/initiate → upload_id = "abc123", expected_chunks = 400 # Step 2: Upload each chunk (can be parallel, can resume) PUT /uploads/abc123/chunk/1 Content-Range: bytes 0-5242879/2097152000 PUT /uploads/abc123/chunk/2 Content-Range: bytes 5242880-10485759/2097152000 ... # Step 3: Complete upload POST /uploads/abc123/complete → triggers transcoding pipeline

Adaptive Bitrate Streaming (ABR)

How YouTube adapts to your connectionVideo encoded at multiple bitrates: 360p (1 Mbps), 720p (5 Mbps), 1080p (8 Mbps), 4K (20 Mbps).
Player measures your actual download speed in real-time. Slow connection → switch to lower bitrate segment. Fast connection → switch to higher. Each bitrate stored as 2–10 second segments.
Format: HLS (.m3u8 manifest + .ts segment files) or DASH (.mpd + .m4s). The manifest file is tiny (1KB), lists all available bitrates, player chooses.
CDN caches each segment independently. Popular segments cached at edge — zero origin hits.
Real-World Design

Design WhatsApp Chat

2 billion users. Real-time messaging. Delivery guarantees. Presence detection. Group chats. End-to-end encryption. The hardest part: managing persistent connections at scale.

Core Challenge: Persistent WebSocket Connections

The numbers problem

2B users. How many simultaneous WebSocket connections?

Not all 2B are online simultaneously. WhatsApp DAU ≈ 1B. Peak concurrent ≈ 500M. Each WebSocket connection holds a TCP socket. A single server can hold ~100K concurrent connections (file descriptor limit, RAM). 500M ÷ 100K = 5,000 connection servers minimum. This is the minimum fleet size just for connections.

Message Delivery Flow

WhatsApp message delivery
sequenceDiagram participant A as Alice (online) participant CS1 as Connection Server 1 participant MS as Message Service participant DB as Cassandra participant CS2 as Connection Server 2 participant B as Bob (online) A->>CS1: Send message to Bob (via WebSocket) CS1->>MS: Route message MS->>DB: Persist message (status: sent) MS->>CS2: Deliver to Bob's connection server CS2->>B: Push message via WebSocket B-->>CS2: ACK received CS2-->>MS: Bob received MS->>DB: Update status: delivered MS-->>CS1: Delivered ✅ CS1-->>A: Double tick ✓✓

Offline Message Delivery

Bob is offline when Alice sends a message. Message Service stores it in Cassandra (persistent). When Bob reconnects: Bob's connection server queries Message Service for pending messages → delivered in order → ACKed → marked delivered.

Database Schema (Cassandra)

Cassandra CQL -- Messages partitioned by conversation, sorted by time CREATE TABLE messages ( conversation_id UUID, sent_at TIMESTAMP, message_id UUID, -- unique per message sender_id BIGINT, content TEXT, media_url TEXT, -- S3 URL for images/videos status INT, -- 0=sent, 1=delivered, 2=read PRIMARY KEY ((conversation_id), sent_at, message_id) ) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (sent_at DESC); -- Why Cassandra? Write-heavy. Append-only. Time-series friendly. -- Each conversation is one partition. Loading last 50 msgs = single partition read. -- At WhatsApp scale: ~70B messages/day. Cassandra handles this natively.

Presence Detection — Online/Offline Status

How to implement presence at scaleWhen user connects WebSocket → SET presence:{user_id} "online" EX 30 in Redis. Background heartbeat every 10s → EXPIRE presence:{user_id} 30 (resets TTL). When user disconnects → DEL presence:{user_id}. Check if user is online: GET presence:{user_id} → "online" or nil.

At 500M concurrent users: 500M keys in Redis, each ~30 bytes = ~15GB. Fits in one Redis cluster easily. TTL-based expiry means no cleanup job needed.
Real-World Design

Design Uber

Real-time location tracking, intelligent matching, surge pricing. The core problem: given a rider's location, find the nearest available driver within seconds, at global scale.

Geospatial Indexing — The Core Problem

Naive approach vs Geohash

Naive: "Give me all drivers within 5km of rider at (37.77, -122.41)"

SQL: WHERE sqrt((lat-37.77)^2 + (lon+122.41)^2) < 5/111 — full table scan on millions of drivers. Too slow (seconds).
Even with 2D spatial index (PostGIS): works at small scale, but at Uber's scale of 5M active drivers globally, still too slow.

Geohash: encode latitude+longitude as a single string prefix

Geohash works by recursively dividing the world into a grid. Each character of the geohash narrows to a smaller cell:
Precision 6: ~1.2km × 0.6km cell. Precision 7: ~150m × 150m cell.
San Francisco driver at (37.77, -122.41) → geohash "9q8yy" (precision 5, ~5km × 5km cell).
Rider at (37.771, -122.412) → geohash "9q8yy". Same prefix = same cell = nearby!
To find drivers near rider: search drivers in same geohash cell + 8 neighboring cells. O(1) lookup in Redis sorted set keyed by geohash.

Redis GEO Commands — Production Implementation

Redis GEO # Driver updates location (every 3 seconds) GEOADD drivers -122.4194 37.7749 "driver:1234" GEOADD drivers -122.4174 37.7789 "driver:5678" # Rider requests — find drivers within 5km, sorted by distance GEOSEARCH drivers FROMLONLAT -122.41 37.77 BYRADIUS 5 km ASC COUNT 10 WITHCOORD WITHDIST # Returns: driver:1234 (0.3km, -122.4194, 37.7749), driver:5678 (0.5km, ...) ... # Redis GEO internally uses geohash + sorted set # GEOADD = O(log N). GEOSEARCH radius = O(N+log M) # 5M drivers: GEOSEARCH returns top 10 in <5ms

Driver Matching Algorithm

Uber ride request flow
sequenceDiagram participant R as Rider App participant MS as Matching Service participant Redis as Driver Location (Redis) participant DS as Driver Service participant D as Nearest Driver R->>MS: Request ride at (37.77, -122.41) MS->>Redis: GEOSEARCH radius 5km → [driver:1234, driver:5678, ...] MS->>DS: Get availability & rating of top 10 drivers DS-->>MS: driver:1234 available, 4.9 rating, 0.3km MS->>D: Send ride request (push notification / WebSocket) D-->>MS: Accept within 10s MS-->>R: Driver coming! ETA 3 min Note over MS,Redis: If driver declines → try next in list

Surge Pricing — The Math

Surge Multiplier = max(1.0, demand / supply × base_factor)demand = rider requests in area in last 5 min
supply = available drivers in same area
base_factor = tuned per city (usually 0.5–0.8)
Example: 200 requests, 50 drivers → surge = max(1.0, 200/50 × 0.6) = max(1.0, 2.4) = 2.4×
Surge incentivises more drivers to go online → supply increases → surge drops → equilibrium
Real-World Design

Design a Search Engine

Web crawler, document processing, inverted index, TF-IDF ranking, PageRank, typeahead. One of the most complex system design problems — teaches indexing, ranking, and distributed crawling.

Component Overview

Search engine architecture
graph TB Seeds["Seed URLs"] --> Crawler["Web Crawler\n(distributed)"] Crawler --> RQ["Raw Queue\n(Kafka)"] RQ --> DP["Document Processor\n(parse, clean, extract links)"] DP --> II["Inverted Index\n(Elasticsearch cluster)"] DP --> LG["Link Graph\n(PageRank computation)"] LG -->|"scores"| Ranker["Ranking Service"] User --> QS["Query Service"] QS -->|"search"| II II -->|"candidate docs"| Ranker Ranker -->|"ranked results"| QS QS --> User style II fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style Ranker fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Inverted Index — How Search Works

Example: Building an inverted index

3 documents:

Doc1: "Loki is the god of mischief"
Doc2: "Thor is the god of thunder"
Doc3: "Loki and Thor are Asgardian gods"

Inverted index:
"loki" → [Doc1, Doc3]
"thor" → [Doc2, Doc3]
"god" → [Doc1, Doc2]
"mischief" → [Doc1]

Query "loki mischief" → AND([Doc1, Doc3], [Doc1]) = [Doc1]. Found in milliseconds regardless of total document count.

TF-IDF Ranking

TF-IDF(term, doc, corpus) = TF(term, doc) × IDF(term, corpus)TF (Term Frequency) = count(term in doc) / total_words(doc) — how often term appears in this doc
IDF (Inverse Document Frequency) = log(total_docs / docs_containing_term) — how rare/specific the term is globally
Example: "the" appears in all docs → IDF ≈ 0 (useless for ranking). "mischief" appears in 1/1M docs → IDF = log(1M) = 13.8 (very specific, high weight)
Higher TF-IDF = document is more relevant for this query term

Web Crawler — Challenges

Crawler design considerationsrobots.txt: Must respect Disallow rules. Crawl responsibly — don't hammer small sites.
Duplicate detection: SimHash or MinHash to detect near-duplicate pages (two URLs with 95% same content).
Politeness: Rate limit per domain (1 req/5s max to any site). Use crawl delay from robots.txt.
Priority queue: High-PageRank domains crawled more frequently. New links from high-PR sites get higher priority.
Scale: At Google scale, 15–20B pages. Crawl the entire web weekly. Requires thousands of crawler instances distributed across DCs.
Real-World Design

Design a Notification System

Push notifications, email, SMS. Priority queues, deduplication, retry logic, delivery tracking. Supporting 10M notifications per day across multiple channels.

Architecture

Notification system flow
graph LR SVC["Source Services\n(Order, User, etc.)"] --> NS["Notification Service\n(routing + validation)"] NS --> PQ["Priority Queue\n(Redis sorted set)\nHigh / Normal / Low"] PQ --> PW["Push Workers\n(APNs / FCM)"] PQ --> EW["Email Workers\n(SendGrid / SES)"] PQ --> SW["SMS Workers\n(Twilio)"] PW & EW & SW -->|"success"| DT["Delivery Tracker\n(Cassandra)"] PW & EW & SW -->|"failure"| RQ["Retry Queue\n(exponential backoff)"] DT --> DS["Dashboard &\nAnalytics"] style PQ fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style DT fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Key Design Decisions

Deduplication with Redis SETNX

Problem: retry logic causes duplicate notifications. User gets "Your order shipped" 3 times.

Python def send_notification_idempotent(notif_id, user_id, message, channel): dedup_key = f"notif:sent:{notif_id}:{channel}" # SETNX: set only if not exists. Returns True if we set it (first time). already_sent = not redis.set(dedup_key, "1", nx=True, ex=86400) if already_sent: logger.info(f"Duplicate notif {notif_id}, skipping") return # Actually send send_via_channel(user_id, message, channel)
APNs / FCM Retry StrategyAPNs (Apple) and FCM (Google) have their own retry logic. Don't hammer them on failure.
Exponential backoff: retry after 1s, 2s, 4s, 8s, 16s. Max 5 retries. After that → dead letter queue for manual inspection.
For transient failures (5xx from APNs/FCM) → retry. For permanent failures (invalid token, user unsubscribed) → remove token from DB, don't retry.
Real-World Design

Design S3 / Object Storage

Unlimited storage, 11 nines durability, any-size objects. S3 stores 200 trillion objects. The key challenges: consistent hashing for storage nodes, multipart upload, erasure coding for durability.

Architecture

Object storage architecture
graph TB Client --> API["API Gateway\n(Auth, routing)"] API --> MS["Metadata Service\n(PostgreSQL: bucket, key → node mapping)"] API --> DN["Data Nodes\n(consistent hashing ring)"] DN --> N1["Node 1\n(stores chunks)"] & N2["Node 2"] & N3["Node 3"] MS -->|"which node?"| API N1 <-->|"replication"| N2 <-->|"replication"| N3 style MS fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style DN fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Multipart Upload

Why multipart upload mattersProblem: uploading a 5GB file. Single request: if network drops at 99% → restart from zero.

Multipart upload:
1. Initiate: POST /multipart → upload_id
2. Upload parts: PUT /multipart/{upload_id}/part/1 (5MB each, can be parallel)
3. Complete: POST /multipart/{upload_id}/complete → assembles parts

Benefits: parallelise parts (faster for large files), resume from failed part, streaming upload while encoding (no need to wait for full file). AWS S3 requires multipart for objects >5GB, recommends it for >100MB.

Erasure Coding — 11 Nines Durability

Erasure Coding vs ReplicationSimple replication: 3 copies = 3× storage cost, survives 2 node failures.
Erasure coding (Reed-Solomon 6+3): split object into 6 data shards + 3 parity shards = 9 shards total across 9 different nodes. Can reconstruct original from ANY 6 of 9 shards. Survives 3 simultaneous node failures. Storage cost: only 1.5× (vs 3× for replication).
S3 uses erasure coding across multiple AZs. Single datacenter fire → still have 6/9 shards in other DCs → data recovered. 11 nines durability = losing a file probability of 0.000000001% per year.
Real-World Design

Design Pastebin / Dropbox

File storage and sharing. Pastebin: simple text storage. Dropbox: sync across devices, delta sync, deduplication. Teaching: content-addressed storage, chunking, dedup.

Content-Addressed Storage (Deduplication)

How deduplication saves massive storage

100 users upload the same popular PDF (50MB each)

Naive approach: Store 100 copies × 50MB = 5GB used.
Content-addressed storage: hash(file_content) → SHA-256 = "abc123..."
First upload: store once in S3 at key "content/abc123". Record in DB: {user1, "report.pdf", hash:"abc123"}.
Next 99 uploads: compute hash → "abc123" already in S3 → just record {user2, "report.pdf", hash:"abc123"} in DB.
Storage used: 50MB (1 copy) + DB metadata. 99× storage saving. Dropbox estimates 70% of stored data is duplicate.

Dropbox Delta Sync

How Dropbox syncs efficientlyProblem: user modifies last page of a 100MB PDF. Don't re-upload 100MB.

Solution: chunking. Split file into 4MB chunks. When file changes:
1. Compute hash of each chunk
2. Compare hashes to previous version
3. Only upload chunks where hash changed
4. Modified 1 page = typically 1-2 chunks = 4-8MB upload (not 100MB)

rsync algorithm: even more sophisticated — finds moved/reordered blocks across files.

Schema

SQL -- Metadata table — small, fast CREATE TABLE files ( id BIGSERIAL PRIMARY KEY, user_id BIGINT NOT NULL, name TEXT NOT NULL, content_hash CHAR(64), -- SHA-256 of content size_bytes BIGINT, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ ); -- Content table — maps hash to S3 location CREATE TABLE content ( hash CHAR(64) PRIMARY KEY, s3_key TEXT NOT NULL, size BIGINT, ref_count INT DEFAULT 1 -- how many files reference this content ); -- When ref_count → 0: garbage collect from S3
Real-World Design

Design Ticket Booking (Flash Sale)

The hardest inventory problem. 100K people try to buy the same 50 Coldplay tickets simultaneously. How do you prevent overselling? How do you maintain fairness? This is the most complex concurrency design problem.

The Core Problem: Race Condition

Why this is hard

Naive implementation fails catastrophically

SQL — BROKEN (race condition) -- Thread 1 and Thread 2 run simultaneously -- Both check availability at the same time: Thread1: SELECT available_seats FROM shows WHERE id=1; -- returns 1 Thread2: SELECT available_seats FROM shows WHERE id=1; -- also returns 1! -- Both see 1 seat available. Both proceed to book. Thread1: UPDATE shows SET available_seats = 0 WHERE id=1; Thread2: UPDATE shows SET available_seats = 0 WHERE id=1; -- Result: 0 seats, but 2 bookings. OVERSOLD! 😱

Solutions — 3 Levels of Sophistication

Optimistic Locking with Version Number
SQL -- Atomic check-and-decrement with version check UPDATE inventory SET available = available - 1, version = version + 1 WHERE show_id = 123 AND available > 0 AND version = :expected_version; -- only update if unchanged -- affected_rows = 0 → someone else got there first → retry -- affected_rows = 1 → success! Create booking. -- This is atomic — database lock ensures mutual exclusion
Redis SETNX — Distributed Seat Lock
Python def reserve_seat(show_id, seat_id, user_id): lock_key = f"seat_lock:{show_id}:{seat_id}" # SETNX: set only if not exists. Atomic. Hold for 10 minutes. acquired = redis.set(lock_key, user_id, nx=True, ex=600) if not acquired: return "seat_taken" # someone else has it # User has 10 minutes to complete payment # If payment succeeds: create booking in DB # If payment fails or timeout: DEL lock_key → seat available again create_pending_booking(show_id, seat_id, user_id) return "reserved"
Virtual Queue — Fairest Solution

All 100K buyers enter a virtual queue. Tickets allocated in queue order.

Flow: User clicks "Buy" → added to Redis sorted set (score = arrival timestamp) → shown queue position and ETA. System processes N buyers per second from front of queue. Each buyer gets a 10-minute exclusive hold to complete purchase.
Benefits: Zero race conditions (sequential allocation). Fair (first-come, first-served). Users know exactly where they stand — much better UX than "try your luck" spinning.
Used by: Ticketmaster "Verified Fan" queue, Taylor Swift Eras tour (sort of).

Full Architecture

Flash sale / ticket booking system
graph TB Users --> LB["Load Balancer\n+ DDoS protection"] LB --> QS["Queue Service\n(virtual queue in Redis)"] QS -->|"next in queue"| BS["Booking Service"] BS -->|"SETNX seat_lock"| RC["Redis Cluster\n(seat locks)"] BS -->|"atomic UPDATE"| DB["PostgreSQL\n(inventory)"] BS --> PAY["Payment Service\n(Stripe)"] PAY -->|"success"| CF["Confirmation\n(email + ticket PDF)"] DB -->|"count"| INV["Inventory Tracker\n(real-time count in Redis)"] style RC fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style QS fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc
SD Interview Approach

The Trade-offs Mindset

System design has no "correct" answer — only trade-offs. The best engineers don't memorize solutions; they reason about what you gain and lose with every choice. This mindset is the most important skill you can develop.

The Golden Rule: Everything Is a Trade-off

Every design decision sacrifices something to gain something else. Your job is to know what you're giving up.

🎯 Analogy: Buying a Car

You want a car that is: fast, fuel-efficient, spacious, cheap, and safe. You cannot maximise all five simultaneously. A sports car is fast but not fuel-efficient or spacious. A mini is fuel-efficient but not spacious. A family SUV is spacious but not fast.

System design is identical. You trade off: speed vs consistency, availability vs consistency, simplicity vs scalability, cost vs performance.

The Core Trade-offs Every Engineer Must Know

Trade-offWhat you gainWhat you loseExample choice
Strong ConsistencyEveryone always sees the same dataHigher latency, lower availability during failuresBank balance must be consistent
Eventual ConsistencyLower latency, always availableUsers may see stale data brieflySocial media "likes" count
Normalisation (separate tables)No duplicate data, easier updatesSlower reads (need JOINs)Financial records
Denormalisation (duplicated data)Faster reads, no JOINsDuplicate data, harder updatesHigh-read social feeds
Synchronous callsImmediate response, simple codeCaller waits, coupling, cascading failuresReal-time payment check
Asynchronous / QueueDecoupled, resilient, scalableEventual result, harder to debugSending welcome email
More cachingFaster reads, lower DB loadStale data, cache invalidation complexityProduct catalogue page
No cachingAlways fresh dataHigher latency, more DB loadBank transaction history
Horizontal scalingUnlimited scale, fault tolerantComplex coordination, stateless constraintWeb app servers
Vertical scalingSimple, no coordination neededUpper limit, expensive, SPOFDatabase server (often)

How to Articulate Trade-offs in an Interview

The Perfect Answer Formula

"I'm choosing [option A] here because [reason]. The trade-off is [what we lose]. I'm accepting that because [why it's okay for this use case]. If our requirements change to [scenario], I'd switch to [option B]."

Real Example

"I'm choosing Cassandra (eventually consistent) here because we need to handle 500K writes/sec for user activity events, and losing 50ms of consistency is acceptable for an analytics feed. The trade-off is users might see their 'like' appear delayed by ~100ms on other devices. I'm accepting that because this is a social feature, not a financial transaction. If we were storing payment confirmations, I'd switch to PostgreSQL with strong consistency."

The RUM Conjecture — Storage Trade-off Triangle

What is the RUM Conjecture?Coined by researchers at Harvard, RUM stands for Read, Update (write), Memory (space). It states: you can optimise for at most TWO of these three at once — never all three.

B-tree (PostgreSQL): Fast reads, moderate space — but slower writes (in-place page rewrites).
LSM-tree (Cassandra): Fast writes, moderate reads — but uses more space (multiple sorted levels).
Hash map: Fast reads + fast writes — but no ordering, so range queries are impossible.
Core Fundamentals

ACID vs BASE

Two competing philosophies for how databases handle data. ACID prioritises correctness. BASE prioritises availability and speed. Understanding when each applies is fundamental to picking the right database.

ACID — The Safe Approach

ACID is a set of properties that guarantee database transactions are reliable. Think of it as the promise a bank makes you about your money.

🏦 Analogy: Bank Transfer

You transfer £500 from Account A to Account B. Two things must happen: debit £500 from A, credit £500 to B. What if the server crashes after the debit but before the credit? ACID guarantees this never leaves the database in a broken state.

A — Atomicity: All or Nothing

What does it mean?
A transaction either completes entirely or not at all. There is no "halfway". If ANY step fails, the database rolls back to the state before the transaction started.
Example

Transfer £500: (1) Debit £500 from A → (2) Credit £500 to B. If step 2 fails, step 1 is automatically undone. You never lose money into thin air.

C — Consistency: Valid State Always

What does it mean?
The database always moves from one valid state to another. All defined rules (constraints, foreign keys, triggers) are always satisfied. The application-level invariant that "total money in the system never changes" is maintained.

I — Isolation: Concurrent Transactions Don't Interfere

What does it mean?
If 1000 users are doing transactions simultaneously, each sees the database as if they were the only one. Their operations don't interfere with each other (to a configurable degree — see Isolation Levels page).
Example

Two people buy the last concert ticket simultaneously. Without isolation, both see "1 ticket available", both book, you've oversold. With proper isolation, one succeeds, the other gets an error.

D — Durability: Committed = Permanent

What does it mean?
Once a transaction is committed (the database said "success"), it stays committed even if the server immediately crashes. This is achieved by writing to disk (WAL — Write-Ahead Log) before acknowledging success.
ACID Databases: PostgreSQL, MySQL, Oracle, SQL Server, SQLiteUse for: financial systems, e-commerce orders, user accounts, anything where data correctness is non-negotiable.

BASE — The Available Approach

BASE is the philosophy of most NoSQL databases. They trade strict consistency for higher availability and lower latency.

LetterStands forMeaning
BABasically AvailableThe system always responds — but the response might not be the latest data. Even during failures, you get some answer.
SSoft StateThe state of the system may change over time even without new input, as replicas catch up to each other.
EEventually ConsistentGiven enough time with no new writes, all replicas will converge to the same value. Not immediately, but eventually.
Example: WhatsApp "Last Seen" Status

When your friend opens WhatsApp, their "last seen" timestamp updates. Across 2 billion users, it's okay if you see it 200ms stale on one phone vs another. It doesn't matter if "12:03 PM" vs "12:03:02 PM". This is BASE — eventually all devices will agree, and brief inconsistency is acceptable.

BASE Databases: Cassandra, DynamoDB, MongoDB (default), CouchDB, RiakUse for: social feeds, user activity logs, shopping carts, anything where brief staleness is tolerable and high availability + write throughput matter.

✅ Choose ACID When

  • Money is involved (banking, payments, billing)
  • Inventory counts must be exact (ticket booking)
  • Legal/compliance requirements (healthcare records)
  • Data loss = catastrophic business consequence
  • Team size is small and complexity must stay low

✅ Choose BASE When

  • Write throughput is extremely high (millions/sec)
  • Brief inconsistency has no real-world consequence
  • System must stay available during network failures
  • Data is naturally time-series or event-based
  • Global distribution requires data near each region
Core Fundamentals

Performance vs Scalability

These sound similar but they're very different problems with very different solutions. Confusing them leads to bad architecture decisions.

The Critical Distinction

Performance Problem

The system is slow for a single user. One request takes 5 seconds. This is a performance problem — the individual unit of work is too slow.

Fix: optimise the slow code path, add indexes, reduce N+1 queries, cache expensive computations.

Scalability Problem

The system is fast for 1 user but slow under heavy load. 1 user = 50ms. 10,000 concurrent users = 5 seconds. This is a scalability problem — the system can't handle more load.

Fix: add more servers (horizontal scale), add caching, add a queue, shard the database.

🍕 Analogy: Pizza Restaurant

Performance problem: One chef takes 45 minutes to make a pizza. The recipe or technique is inefficient. Solution: train the chef to be faster, buy better equipment.

Scalability problem: One chef takes 8 minutes per pizza (great!) but there's a 4-hour queue. Solution: hire more chefs, open more locations, run parallel pipelines.

Why This Matters for System Design

Don't add complexity to solve the wrong problemIf you have 100 users and your API is slow, adding more servers won't help — there's no load to distribute. You need to fix the slow code.

If you have 1M users and your (already-fast) API is timing out under load, adding more servers is exactly right.

How They Interact

A system can have both problems simultaneously. A poorly performing system (5s per request) will also not scale well (because threads are held for 5s each, exhausting server capacity quickly). Fix performance first, then scale.

Server Capacity ≈ 1 / (avg_response_time_seconds)If each request takes 0.1s → server handles 10 req/s. If you fix it to 0.01s → same server handles 100 req/s. Performance improvement = 10× scalability improvement for free.
Networking & Protocols

TCP vs UDP & QUIC

These are transport protocols — the rules that govern how data is sent across a network. Choosing between them affects reliability, speed, and what you can build on top.

What is a Transport Protocol?

Think of it as choosing between certified mail (TCP) and dropping a note in someone's postbox (UDP).

📮 Analogy: Postal Service

TCP = Registered Mail: You send a package. The post office confirms delivery, tells you if it was lost, re-sends if needed, and delivers everything in order. Slower, but you know it arrived.

UDP = Normal Mail: You drop letters in a postbox. They might arrive. They might not. They might arrive in a different order. But it's much faster — no confirmation overhead.

TCP — Transmission Control Protocol

The 3-Way Handshake — How TCP Connections Start

TCP Connection Establishment
sequenceDiagram participant C as Client participant S as Server C->>S: SYN (I want to connect, my seq=100) S->>C: SYN-ACK (OK! My seq=200, ack=101) C->>S: ACK (Got it! ack=201) Note over C,S: Connection established — 1 RTT cost C->>S: HTTP GET /page (actual data now)
Why does this 3-way handshake matter for performance?
Every new TCP connection costs 1 RTT (round-trip time) before any data flows. If you're in New York connecting to a London server (RTT ≈ 80ms), that's 80ms wasted before your first byte. This is why HTTP keep-alive, connection pooling, and TLS session resumption exist — to reuse connections and avoid paying this cost repeatedly.

TCP's Guarantees

  • Ordered delivery: packets 1, 2, 3 arrive in order even if they took different network paths
  • Reliable delivery: the sender retransmits any lost packets automatically
  • Flow control: receiver tells sender how fast to go (don't flood my buffer)
  • Congestion control: automatically slows down when the network is congested

TCP Head-of-Line Blocking Problem

The Hidden Cost of TCP OrderingIf packet 3 of a stream is lost, TCP waits for it before delivering packets 4, 5, 6, 7... even if they already arrived. All subsequent data is blocked. This "head-of-line blocking" is why video streams stutter when there's packet loss — even though only 0.1% of packets were lost, everything stops waiting for the missing one. HTTP/2 multiplexes streams on ONE TCP connection, so one lost packet blocks ALL streams. HTTP/3 (QUIC) fixes this.

UDP — User Datagram Protocol

No connection, no ordering, no delivery guarantee. Just send packets and hope. The application is responsible for any reliability it needs.

FeatureTCPUDP
Connection setup3-way handshake (1 RTT)None (0 RTT)
ReliabilityGuaranteed, retransmits lost packetsBest-effort, packets can be lost
OrderingIn-order delivery guaranteedCan arrive out of order
SpeedSlower due to overheadFaster — minimal overhead
Header size20 bytes8 bytes
Use casesWeb, email, file transfer, SSH, APIsGaming, DNS, VoIP, video streaming, QUIC
Why Online Games Use UDP

In a first-person shooter, your character's position is sent 60 times per second. If one packet is lost, the game could:
TCP approach: Stop and wait for retransmit → 50ms freeze → character teleports. Players hate this.
UDP approach: Drop the lost packet, move on. The next position update arrives 16ms later and the player barely notices one missed frame. Late data is worse than lost data in real-time games.

QUIC & HTTP/3 — The Best of Both Worlds

QUIC (Quick UDP Internet Connections) was built by Google and is now the foundation of HTTP/3. It reimplements TCP's reliability on top of UDP, but fixes TCP's worst problems.

Problem with TCP/TLSQUIC's Solution
1 RTT for TCP + 1-2 RTT for TLS = 2-3 RTT before data1-RTT handshake (0-RTT for returning clients)
HOL blocking: one lost packet stalls all streamsMultiple independent streams — one lost packet only stalls its own stream
IP address changes break connections (WiFi → 4G)Connection ID instead of IP — survives network changes
TLS is a separate layer, complex to updateTLS 1.3 built directly in, always encrypted
OS kernel controls TCP; slow to improveUser-space — Google/Facebook can update QUIC without kernel changes
When Does HTTP/3/QUIC Help Most?Mobile users on poor connections (packet loss 1-5%): QUIC's per-stream recovery vs TCP's full-stream stall = dramatic difference. High-latency connections: 0-RTT resumption eliminates TLS handshake cost. Already on fast fibre? Barely noticeable. That's why Cloudflare, YouTube, Google, and Facebook all use HTTP/3 — the tail latency (slow users on poor connections) improves dramatically.

When to Use Which in Your System Design

Default to TCP unless you have a specific reason not to: Use UDP/QUIC for: online gaming (real-time position updates), live video/voice (Zoom, WhatsApp calls), DNS lookups (one-packet question-answer), IoT telemetry (high-frequency small messages), anything where timeliness beats reliability.
Networking & Protocols

OSI Model & TLS Handshake

The OSI model is a framework for understanding how network communication is structured in layers. HTTPS/TLS is how the web secures that communication. Both come up frequently in system design discussions.

The OSI Model — 7 Layers

📦 Analogy: Sending a Letter via DHL

When you send a package internationally: you write the letter (content) → put it in an envelope (application layer) → address it (transport layer) → DHL adds routing labels (network layer) → truck carries it (data link/physical). Each layer doesn't need to know how the layers above/below work internally — they just pass to the next layer.

LayerNameWhat it doesProtocols/Examples
7ApplicationUser-facing data and servicesHTTP, FTP, DNS, SMTP, SSH
6PresentationData formatting, encryption, compressionTLS/SSL, JPEG, ASCII
5SessionManaging connections (open/close/resume)NetBIOS, RPC sessions
4TransportEnd-to-end delivery, ports, reliabilityTCP, UDP
3NetworkRouting packets across networks using IP addressesIP, ICMP, routing protocols
2Data LinkSending frames between directly-connected devicesEthernet, WiFi (802.11), MAC addresses
1PhysicalRaw bits over physical mediumFibre optic, cables, radio waves
Real-World MappingWhen you type "github.com" in Chrome: Layer 7 (Chrome generates HTTP request) → Layer 4 (TCP breaks it into segments) → Layer 3 (IP adds source/destination addresses) → Layer 2 (Ethernet adds MAC addresses for local delivery) → Layer 1 (bits sent as electrical signals or light pulses). On arrival, the reverse happens — each layer strips its header and passes up.

The Simplified TCP/IP Stack (What We Actually Use)

The real internet uses a simplified 4-layer model: Application (combines OSI 5-7), Transport (OSI 4), Internet/Network (OSI 3), Link/Physical (OSI 1-2).

HTTPS & TLS — How Secure Web Works

TLS (Transport Layer Security) is the technology behind HTTPS. It ensures: (1) You're talking to who you think you are (authentication), (2) No one can read your data in transit (encryption), (3) Data wasn't tampered with (integrity).

🔒 Analogy: Safety Deposit Box

You want to communicate privately with your bank. First, you verify the bank is who they claim to be (you check their official ID = certificate). Then you negotiate a secret code only you and the bank know. After that, all communication uses that secret code. Even if someone intercepts your messages, they just see gibberish without the secret.

TLS 1.3 Handshake — Step by Step

TLS 1.3 Handshake (1 RTT total)
sequenceDiagram participant C as Browser participant S as Server (github.com) Note over C,S: TCP handshake already done (1 RTT) C->>S: ClientHello: supported ciphers, TLS version, key share (public key) S->>C: ServerHello: chosen cipher, server public key, certificate S->>C: Encrypted Handshake (proves it has the private key) Note over C: Browser verifies cert: signed by trusted CA? Not expired? Domain matches? Note over C,S: Both derive session keys from key exchange — symmetric encryption now C->>S: Application Data (HTTP request, now encrypted) S->>C: Application Data (HTTP response, encrypted)

Key Concepts Explained

What is a Certificate Authority (CA)?
A trusted third party (like DigiCert, Let's Encrypt, Comodo) that verifies GitHub really is GitHub and signs their certificate. Your browser trusts ~150 root CAs pre-installed by the OS. When GitHub sends their certificate signed by DigiCert, and your browser trusts DigiCert, the chain is established.
What is Public Key Cryptography?
A mathematical pair of keys: a public key (you share with everyone) and a private key (you never share). Data encrypted with your public key can ONLY be decrypted with your private key. This means: I can send you a secret without us having shared a secret beforehand — I encrypt with your public key, only you can decrypt.
What is a symmetric key and why switch to it?
Asymmetric (public/private key) encryption is slow for large data. So TLS uses it ONLY to securely exchange a symmetric key (one shared secret). After that, all data is encrypted with the fast symmetric cipher (AES). Best of both worlds: secure key exchange + fast data encryption.
TLS 1.3 vs TLS 1.2 Speed DifferenceTLS 1.2: 2 RTT for full handshake (300ms on a 150ms RTT connection). TLS 1.3: 1 RTT (150ms). TLS 1.3 with 0-RTT (session resumption): 0 RTT for returning clients. 0-RTT sends data with the first packet. Google and Cloudflare saw 5-10% improvement in page load times by upgrading. Modern websites should use TLS 1.3 — Let's Encrypt makes free certificates trivial.
Storage & Databases

Storage Engines — LSM Trees vs B-Trees

A storage engine is the internal mechanism a database uses to store and retrieve data on disk. It's the engine under the hood — and choosing the wrong type for your workload can make your database 10× slower. This is one of the most important (and most neglected) system design topics.

Why Should You Care About Storage Engines?

📚 Analogy: Two Types of Filing Systems

B-Tree (like an organised filing cabinet): Papers (data) are stored in alphabetical order in labelled folders. Finding a file is fast — you go to the right folder immediately. But if you want to add a new paper in the middle, you have to shift everything else to make room. Updates hurt.

LSM-Tree (like a sticky note system): New papers go on a sticky note pile on your desk (fast to write). When the pile gets big, you sort it and put it in a box. Periodically, boxes get merged. Writing is fast, but reading might mean checking the desk, then box 1, then box 2...

B-Tree — How PostgreSQL/MySQL Store Data

Structure: Balanced Tree of Pages

A B-tree organises data as a tree of fixed-size "pages" (typically 4–16KB). Each page holds a sorted list of keys and pointers to child pages. The leaf pages (bottom level) contain the actual data.

B-Tree structure — finding key "251"
graph TD Root["Root Page\n[100, 200, 300, 400]"] --> P1["Page\n[100–200]"] Root --> P2["Page\n[200–300]"] Root --> P3["Page\n[300–400]"] P2 --> L1["Leaf\n[200,210,220]"] P2 --> L2["Leaf\n[230,240,251]"] P2 --> L3["Leaf\n[260,270,280]"] style L2 fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757,stroke-width:2px style Root fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc
How a B-Tree Lookup Works (Finding user_id = 251)

1. Start at root page: is 251 between 200 and 300? Yes → go to that child.
2. Check that child page: is 251 between 240 and 260? Yes → go to that leaf.
3. Found the leaf page — binary search finds 251 in ~2ms.
Total: 3 page reads (3 disk seeks). A 4-level tree can hold billions of rows with only 4 page reads!

B-Tree Write Path — Why Writes Are Slower

  1. Write to WAL first (Write-Ahead Log)

    Before changing any page, the change is written to a sequential log file. This protects against crashes — if the server dies mid-write, the WAL lets us redo the operation on restart.

  2. Find the right leaf page

    Traverse the tree (3-4 disk reads) to find where this key belongs.

  3. Update the page in place

    Overwrite the page on disk with the new data. If the page is full, split it into two pages and update the parent — potentially causing a cascade of page splits up the tree.

Write AmplificationOne logical write may cause many physical disk writes: WAL write + page write + potentially parent page rewrites from splits. This "write amplification" is why B-trees can be slower than LSM-trees for write-heavy workloads. In benchmarks, WiredTiger (B-tree) had 268× write amplification vs RocksDB's (LSM) 38×.

LSM-Tree — How Cassandra/RocksDB/LevelDB Store Data

LSM stands for Log-Structured Merge-tree. Invented in 1996, it's the modern approach for write-heavy workloads. The core insight: sequential disk writes are 10–1000× faster than random disk writes, so always write sequentially and handle complexity during background compaction.

The LSM Write Path — Always Sequential

LSM-tree write and read paths
flowchart LR W["Write: key='user123',\nval={name:'Loki'}"] --> WAL["WAL\n(crash recovery,\nappend-only)"] W --> MT["Memtable\n(in RAM, sorted,\nred-black tree)"] MT -->|"threshold\n(~64MB)"| SST0["SSTable\nLevel 0\n(sorted, immutable)"] SST0 -->|"Compaction"| SST1["SSTable\nLevel 1\n(larger)"] SST1 -->|"Compaction"| SST2["SSTable\nLevel 2\n(largest)"] R["Read: GET 'user123'"] --> MT2["Check Memtable\n(newest)"] MT2 -->|"miss"| BF["Bloom Filter per SSTable\n(maybe present?)"] BF -->|"possibly"| SCAN["Scan SSTable\n(find key)"] style MT fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style WAL fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

Step by Step — What Happens When You Write to Cassandra

  1. Write to WAL (Commit Log)

    The write is appended to a sequential log file on disk. This is fast (sequential I/O) and ensures durability — if Cassandra crashes, this log lets it recover. Takes ~1ms.

  2. Write to Memtable (RAM)

    The data is also inserted into a sorted in-memory data structure (like a Java TreeMap). Writes to RAM are nanoseconds. Success is returned to the client now.

  3. Flush Memtable to SSTable (Background)

    When the memtable reaches a size threshold (~64MB), it's flushed to disk as an immutable SSTable (Sorted String Table). The flush is one sequential write — very fast. The old WAL entries are now safe to delete.

  4. Compaction (Background)

    Over time, many SSTables accumulate. A background process merges them, removing overwritten/deleted values. This is the "cleanup" phase. Different strategies exist (size-tiered vs leveled).

SSTables — Sorted String Tables

What is an SSTable?
An immutable file on disk where key-value pairs are stored in sorted order. "Immutable" means once written, it never changes — updates create a NEW SSTable with the new value. The merge process (compaction) reconciles duplicates later, keeping only the latest version.

Bloom Filters — Avoiding Wasted Disk Reads

What is a Bloom Filter and why is it critical for LSM-trees?
A Bloom filter is a compact data structure that answers "is this key possibly in this SSTable?" with no false negatives but possible false positives. Before reading an SSTable from disk, we check its Bloom filter. If it says "definitely not here" (100% certain), we skip the SSTable entirely. This dramatically reduces read amplification — instead of reading 10 SSTables, we might only read 1-2.

Size: ~10 bits per element for ~1% false positive rate. For 1M keys per SSTable: only 1.25MB to potentially skip an entire SSTable file.

Compaction Strategies

Size-Tiered Compaction Strategy (STCS)

Rule: When you have N similar-sized SSTables, merge them into one larger one.

Level 0: [10MB, 10MB, 10MB, 10MB] → merge → [40MB] Level 1
Level 1: [40MB, 40MB, 40MB, 40MB] → merge → [160MB] Level 2

✅ Low write amplification — each byte is written fewer times during compaction.
❌ High space amplification — during merging, you temporarily have 2× the data on disk.
❌ More files to check on read → slightly higher read amplification.
Best for: write-heavy workloads, time-series data, ingestion pipelines.

Leveled Compaction Strategy (LCS)

Rule: Each level is one sorted run (no overlapping keys). Total size grows by a factor per level.

L1 max: 10MB, L2 max: 100MB, L3 max: 1GB (10× factor).
When L1 overflows, one SSTable is merged into L2, resolving overlapping key ranges.

✅ Low read amplification — at most 1 SSTable per level to check (no overlap = binary search).
✅ Low space amplification — no temporary 2× bloat.
❌ Higher write amplification — a byte gets rewritten more often as it cascades through levels.
Best for: read-heavy workloads, random reads, workloads where space is tight.

B-Tree vs LSM-Tree — Full Comparison

AspectB-Tree (PostgreSQL, MySQL, WiredTiger)LSM-Tree (Cassandra, RocksDB, ScyllaDB)
Write performanceModerate — in-place updates + WALExcellent — sequential writes always
Read performanceExcellent — direct tree traversalGood — may check multiple SSTables
Write amplificationHigh (268× in benchmarks)Low (38× in same benchmarks)
Read amplificationLow (tree depth = 3-4 reads)Moderate (multiple SSTable checks)
Space amplificationModerate (~30% overhead)Higher during compaction (temp 2×)
Range queriesExcellent — keys adjacent in pagesGood — keys sorted within level
DeletesMark as deleted, vacuum reclaimsTombstone markers, removed at compaction
Who uses itPostgreSQL, MySQL InnoDB, SQLite, MongoDB WiredTigerCassandra, ScyllaDB, RocksDB, LevelDB, HBase
The Discord Migration StoryDiscord stored trillions of messages in Cassandra (LSM-based, Java). Problem: JVM garbage collection pauses caused latency spikes, and hot partitions were hit-or-miss. They migrated to ScyllaDB (same Cassandra protocol, but rewritten in C++ with no GC). Result: p99 read latency dropped from 40-125ms to 15ms. p99 write latency from 5-70ms to 5ms. Same LSM design, but C++ eliminated GC pauses. Lesson: the data structure matters AND the implementation matters.
Storage & Databases

Database Indexing — Every Type, Fully Explained

Indexes are the single highest-impact database performance tool. They can make a query go from 30 seconds to 3 milliseconds. But they have costs. This page covers every index type with concrete examples for each.

What Is an Index? Why Does It Exist?

📖 Analogy: Book Index

Imagine a 1000-page textbook. To find all pages mentioning "Kafka", you could read every single page (full table scan = O(n)), or check the back-of-book index which tells you exactly: "Kafka: pages 234, 567, 891" (index lookup = O(log n) to find 'K', then jump to pages).

Database indexes work identically. Without an index, the database reads every row. With an index, it jumps directly to the matching rows. The trade-off: the index takes up space and must be updated on every INSERT/UPDATE/DELETE.

Without index: O(n) — scan every row
With B-tree index: O(log n) — tree traversalFor 1 billion rows: O(n) = up to 1B comparisons. O(log n) = ~30 comparisons. That's 33 million× faster!

Index Type 1: B-Tree Index (The Default)

Used by: PostgreSQL, MySQL, Oracle. This is what you get when you do CREATE INDEX without specifying a type.

What it stores: A sorted copy of the indexed column(s) + pointer to the row location.

Supports: Equality (=), Ranges (<, >, BETWEEN), Prefix matching (LIKE 'Jo%'), Sorting (ORDER BY), Minimum/Maximum (efficient without scanning all rows).

Does NOT support: Suffix matching (LIKE '%son') — the tree is sorted left-to-right, so you can't binary search on the end of a string.

SQL -- Create a B-tree index on email column CREATE INDEX idx_users_email ON users(email); -- Now this query is O(log n) instead of O(n) SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = 'loki@asgard.com'; -- Range query also uses the index SELECT * FROM orders WHERE created_at BETWEEN '2024-01-01' AND '2024-12-31'; -- This does NOT use the index efficiently (LIKE with leading wildcard) SELECT * FROM users WHERE name LIKE '%son'; -- full scan ❌ -- This DOES use the index (LIKE with trailing wildcard only) SELECT * FROM users WHERE name LIKE 'Jo%'; -- index seek ✅

Index Type 2: B+Tree (The Actual B-Tree Variant Used)

Most databases actually implement B+Trees, not B-Trees. The difference matters for range queries.

B-Tree

  • Keys AND data stored in all nodes (root, internal, leaf)
  • Range scan: must traverse tree for each next value

B+Tree (what DBs actually use)

  • Only keys in internal nodes; ALL data in leaf nodes
  • Leaf nodes linked in a doubly linked list → range scans just walk the list!
  • Range query: find start key via tree traversal → walk linked leaves sequentially
Why the linked leaf nodes matterFor WHERE created_at BETWEEN '2024-01-01' AND '2024-01-31', a B+Tree finds the first matching leaf in O(log n), then walks the linked list to the end of the range. No tree traversal for each next row. This makes range queries dramatically faster — one sequential disk read for thousands of consecutive rows.

Index Type 3: Composite Index (Multiple Columns)

An index on multiple columns together. The order of columns matters enormously.

⚠️ The Leftmost Prefix Rule — Critical for Interviews

A composite index on (a, b, c) is equivalent to having three indexes: (a), (a, b), and (a, b, c). It is NOT equivalent to (b), (c), (b, c), or any combination that doesn't start with a.

SQL CREATE INDEX idx_composite ON posts (user_id, status, created_at); -- ✅ USES the index (starts from leftmost column) WHERE user_id = 123 WHERE user_id = 123 AND status = 'active' WHERE user_id = 123 AND status = 'active' AND created_at > '2024-01-01' -- ❌ Does NOT use the index efficiently (skips leftmost column) WHERE status = 'active' -- no user_id filter → full scan WHERE user_id = 123 AND created_at > '2024-01-01' -- status skipped → partial -- RULE: Equality columns first, range columns last -- For: WHERE status = 'active' AND created_at > X AND user_id = 123 -- Best index: (user_id, status, created_at) — user_id + status are equality, created_at is range

Index Type 4: Covering Index (Index-Only Scan)

An index that contains ALL the columns a query needs — so the database never has to touch the actual table rows.

SQL -- Without covering index: DB reads index, then fetches rows from table (2 operations) CREATE INDEX idx_user ON posts(user_id); SELECT id, title, created_at FROM posts WHERE user_id = 123; -- ^ DB must fetch rows to get title and created_at (heap fetch) -- WITH covering index: query answered entirely from index (1 operation, much faster) CREATE INDEX idx_user_covering ON posts(user_id, created_at DESC) INCLUDE(id, title); -- PostgreSQL 11+: INCLUDE stores extra columns in leaf pages SELECT id, title, created_at FROM posts WHERE user_id = 123; -- ^ Now an INDEX ONLY SCAN — never touches the table. 3-10× faster. -- EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows: "Index Only Scan using idx_user_covering"

Index Type 5: Partial Index

An index that only covers a subset of rows, defined by a WHERE clause. Smaller, faster, lower maintenance cost.

SQL -- Full index on status — indexes ALL 10 million rows CREATE INDEX idx_all ON orders(status); -- Partial index — only indexes 'pending' orders (maybe 50,000 of 10M rows) CREATE INDEX idx_pending ON orders(created_at) WHERE status = 'pending'; -- This query uses the tiny partial index instead of the huge full index SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'pending' ORDER BY created_at; -- 50K rows indexed vs 10M → 200× smaller index → much faster + less RAM needed

Index Type 6: Hash Index

Uses a hash function to directly compute where a key lives. O(1) lookup — faster than B-tree for pure equality searches.

✅ When Hash Index Wins

  • Only need equality queries (= operator)
  • Keys are very long (hash is constant size regardless of key length)
  • High-throughput exact lookups (session tokens, cache keys)

❌ Hash Index Cannot Do

  • Range queries (BETWEEN, <, >) — no ordering
  • Prefix matches (LIKE 'Jo%')
  • Sorting (ORDER BY)
  • Multi-column searches beyond equality

Index Type 7: Bitmap Index

Creates a bit vector (array of 0s and 1s) for each distinct value of a column. Perfect for low-cardinality columns (few distinct values) in analytical queries.

How Bitmap Indexes Work

Column: gender with values M/F/Other. Table has 8 rows.

bitmap structure Row: 1 2 3 4 5 6 7 8 M: 1 0 1 0 0 1 0 1 (rows where gender = M) F: 0 1 0 1 1 0 1 0 (rows where gender = F) Other: 0 0 0 0 0 0 0 0 -- Query: gender = M AND status = 'active' -- Bitmap AND operation: each bit takes 1 nanosecond M bitmap: 1 0 1 0 0 1 0 1 active bitmap: 1 1 1 0 0 1 0 0 Result: 1 0 1 0 0 1 0 0 → rows 1, 3, 6

Multi-condition queries become lightning fast via bitwise AND/OR operations. Processing 1 billion rows of low-cardinality data: milliseconds instead of minutes.

Bitmap Indexes and Writes Don't MixEvery INSERT/UPDATE requires updating the bit vector for that value. Under high write load, this creates lock contention. PostgreSQL doesn't have persistent on-disk bitmap indexes — it builds them in memory during queries as "Bitmap Heap Scans". Oracle, Redshift, and other OLAP systems have persistent bitmap indexes. Rule: bitmap indexes = read-heavy OLAP/warehouse workloads, not OLTP.

Index Type 8: Inverted Index (Full-Text Search)

Maps each word → list of documents containing it. The foundation of every search engine and full-text search system.

Building an Inverted Index
concept Doc 1: "the quick brown fox" Doc 2: "the brown dog" Doc 3: "a quick blue fox" Inverted Index: "the" → [Doc1, Doc2] "quick" → [Doc1, Doc3] "brown" → [Doc1, Doc2] "fox" → [Doc1, Doc3] "dog" → [Doc2] "blue" → [Doc3] Query: "quick fox" → intersection of quick:[Doc1,Doc3] AND fox:[Doc1,Doc3] → Result: [Doc1, Doc3] ← O(k) where k=result size, not O(n) total docs

PostgreSQL implements inverted indexes via GIN (Generalized Inverted Index). Elasticsearch/Lucene use their own high-performance inverted index implementation.

PostgreSQL-Specific Index Types

Index TypeUse CaseWhen to Use
GINArrays, JSONB, full-text (tsvector)Querying inside JSON documents, array containment (@>), full-text search. Slower to write, faster to read.
GiSTGeometric types, ranges, nearest-neighbour (KNN)PostGIS spatial queries, range types, nearest restaurant. Faster to write than GIN, slower to read.
SP-GiSTNon-balanced data like QuadTrees, K-D treesIP address ranges, phone numbers, 2D point data with non-uniform distribution
BRINVery large tables with natural physical orderingTime-series data where rows are inserted in timestamp order. Tiny index size (pages, not rows). Only useful if data physically ordered.

Clustered vs Non-Clustered Index

Clustered Index

  • The table's physical row order IS the index order
  • Only ONE per table (can't sort data two ways at once)
  • MySQL InnoDB: Primary key IS the clustered index (table = index)
  • Range queries on clustered column = sequential disk reads = fast

Non-Clustered Index

  • Separate structure that stores key + pointer to row
  • Multiple per table allowed
  • Two lookups: index traversal + heap fetch (row retrieval)
  • PostgreSQL: ALL indexes are non-clustered by default (can cluster manually)

How to Read EXPLAIN ANALYZE Output

PostgreSQL EXPLAIN output EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) SELECT id, title FROM posts WHERE user_id = 123 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20; -- GOOD output: Index Only Scan (fastest possible) -- Index Only Scan using idx_posts_user_created on posts -- (cost=0.43..8.45 rows=20 width=36) (actual time=0.123..0.456 rows=20 loops=1) -- Index Cond: (user_id = 123) -- Heap Fetches: 0 ← key! 0 means 100% answered from index -- Buffers: shared hit=5 ← all in memory cache -- Planning: 0.123 ms Execution: 0.456 ms ✅ -- BAD output: Seq Scan (missing index!) -- Seq Scan on posts -- (cost=0.00..45234.00 rows=1000000 width=36) (actual time=0.050..4521.234 rows=20 loops=1) -- Filter: (user_id = 123) ← scanning ALL rows, filtering after -- Rows Removed by Filter: 999980 ← reading 1M rows to return 20! -- Execution: 4521.234 ms ❌ → add index on user_id

Index Maintenance Trade-offs

Indexes Aren't FreeEvery index on a table:
• Slows down INSERT by ~5-10% per index (must update all indexes)
• Slows down UPDATE on indexed columns
• Slows down DELETE (must remove from indexes)
• Uses disk space and RAM (buffer cache)

Rule: index the columns in your WHERE, ORDER BY, and JOIN clauses. Don't index columns that are rarely queried or have very low cardinality (boolean fields with 50% true/false rarely benefit from an index — the optimizer may prefer a seq scan).
Storage & Databases

Replication — Deep Dive

Replication means keeping copies of your data on multiple machines. Why? High availability (if one machine dies, others serve data), scalability (spread read load), and lower latency (put data near users). This page covers every replication strategy from DDIA Chapter 5.

Why Replicate?

📋 Analogy: Photocopying Important Documents

You have one copy of your passport. If you lose it, you're stuck. If you have 3 photocopies in different locations: one in your bag, one at home, one with a trusted friend — losing one copy is inconvenient but not catastrophic. Replication does this for your database.

Strategy 1: Single-Leader (Primary-Replica)

One node is the "leader" (primary/master) and accepts ALL writes. Other nodes are "followers" (replicas/slaves) that receive changes from the leader and accept READ-ONLY queries.

Single-Leader Replication
graph LR App["Application"] -->|"ALL WRITES"| L["Leader\n(Primary)"] App -->|"READS"| R1["Replica 1"] App -->|"READS"| R2["Replica 2"] App -->|"READS"| R3["Replica 3"] L -->|"replication log\n(async or sync)"| R1 L --> R2 L --> R3 style L fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757,stroke-width:2px

Synchronous vs Asynchronous Replication

SynchronousAsynchronousSemi-Synchronous
When leader returns "success"After replica confirms receiptImmediately, without waitingAfter at least one replica confirms
Data loss on leader failureZero — replica has everythingCan lose recent writesZero for one replica; asynchronous risk for others
Write latencyLeader RTT + replica RTT (2× slower)Local disk only (fastest)Leader + fastest replica RTT
AvailabilityIf replica is slow, leader blocksLeader always progressesModerate
