Used byPostgreSQL synchronous_standby_namesMySQL default, most systemsMySQL semi-sync, common compromise

Replication Lag Problems (The Hidden Dangers)

Problem 1: Read-Your-Writes Violation

Scenario

User updates their profile photo. Write goes to leader. Page reloads and reads from replica (which lags by 500ms). User sees their OLD photo. They think the update failed. They submit again. The photo is double-uploaded.

Fix: For data the user just modified, read from the leader for 1 minute after a write. Or use sticky sessions to always route a user's reads to one consistent replica.

Problem 2: Monotonic Reads Violation

Scenario

A user refreshes their Twitter feed. First request → Replica 1 (slightly fresh) → sees a tweet. Second request → Replica 2 (more lagged) → that tweet has "disappeared". The user sees data going backward in time.

Fix: Pin a user to the same replica for all reads (e.g., hash(user_id) → replica). That replica might be stale, but the user sees consistent progress.

Problem 3: Consistent Prefix Reads

Scenario

A: "What time is it?" B: "3pm." Different replicas handle different partitions. Observer sees B's reply before A's question — as if B answered a question that wasn't asked yet. Causally related writes appear out of order.

Fix: Write causally-related data to the same partition/shard. Or use vector clocks to track causal relationships.

How Replication Logs Work

MethodWhat is sent to replicasProsCons
Statement-basedThe SQL statement itself (INSERT/UPDATE)Very compactNon-deterministic functions (NOW(), RAND()) give different results on replicas
WAL shippingRaw disk bytes (physical changes)Exact replica stateTightly coupled to storage format — can't run different DB versions on leader vs replica
Logical/Row-basedRow-level changes (inserted/updated/deleted values)Format-independent, enables CDCLarger log size
Change Data Capture (CDC)Logical replication enables CDC — streaming every database change to other systems in real-time. Tools like Debezium tail the transaction log and publish row changes to Kafka. This enables: keeping an Elasticsearch search index in sync with your PostgreSQL data, feeding a data warehouse, or propagating changes to a cache. No dual-writes needed — the database is the single source of truth.

Strategy 2: Multi-Leader (Multi-Master)

Multiple nodes accept writes. Used for multi-datacenter setups where you need low-latency writes in each region.

The Write Conflict ProblemUser A (in London) updates a doc title to "Budget 2024". User B (in New York) simultaneously updates the same doc title to "Annual Budget". Each write succeeds locally. When the two leaders sync, there's a conflict. Who wins?

Solutions: (1) Last-Write-Wins (LWW) — use timestamps; data loss possible. (2) Custom conflict resolution in application code. (3) CRDTs — special data types that mathematically merge without conflicts. (4) Avoid multi-leader when possible — it's complex.

Strategy 3: Leaderless (Dynamo-Style)

No special leader node. Writes and reads go to multiple nodes simultaneously using quorums.

Quorum Reads and Writes

Configuration: N=3 replicas, W=2 (write quorum), R=2 (read quorum)

Write: Send to all 3 replicas. Wait for 2 to confirm. Return success. 1 replica can be slow/down.
Read: Send to all 3 replicas. Wait for 2 responses. Take the one with the highest version number (most recent).
Why W+R > N guarantees consistency: W=2 + R=2 = 4 > N=3. Therefore, at least one node must be in both the write quorum and the read quorum — that node has the latest data, so the read will see it.
Used by: Amazon DynamoDB, Cassandra (tunable), Riak.

Split Brain — The Catastrophic Failure Mode

Split Brain ExplainedA network partition splits your cluster into two groups. Each group can't see the other. If your system promotes a new leader in each group — now you have TWO leaders accepting writes. When the partition heals, you have conflicting data that can never be automatically reconciled.

Prevention: Use quorum-based leader election. A leader is only valid if it has acknowledgement from a majority (N/2 + 1) of nodes. If a group doesn't have a majority, it refuses to elect a leader. This means: a minority group goes offline (availability loss) rather than creating a split brain (consistency loss).

Fencing Tokens — Protecting Against Stale Leaders

Why Fencing Tokens Are Needed

Old leader is slow due to a GC pause (garbage collection). System elects a new leader. Old leader "wakes up" and thinks it's still leader. It tries to write with its old authority.

Fencing Token solution: Each time a leader is elected, it gets a monotonically increasing token (33, 34, 35...). Every write includes this token. The storage system rejects any write with a lower token than the highest it's seen. Old leader (token=33) wakes up → storage sees new leader had token=34 → rejects old leader's writes. Safe.

Storage & Databases

Partitioning & Sharding — Full Guide

When a single database machine can't handle your data anymore, you split (shard) it across multiple machines. This is called partitioning. It's one of the most complex topics in database design — done wrong, it creates problems worse than the original scaling issue.

When Do You Need Sharding?

📚 Analogy: Library Branches

One library branch has 100,000 books. It's full. Adding more shelves doesn't help — there's no room. Solution: open multiple branches, each holding a subset of books (A-F at Branch 1, G-M at Branch 2, etc.). Now each branch handles its own books. But if you want books from multiple branches, you need to visit multiple locations (scatter-gather queries).

Signals You Need to Shard

  • Single DB can't handle write throughput (even with replicas — replicas help reads, not writes)
  • Dataset doesn't fit on the largest single machine
  • Query latency increasing despite indexes (table too large to fit in RAM buffer pool)
  • Typical threshold: >1TB of data or >100K writes/second sustained
Don't shard prematurelySharding adds enormous complexity: cross-shard queries are hard, transactions across shards are nearly impossible with ACID, schema changes require coordinating all shards. Try these first: better indexing, query optimisation, read replicas, vertical scaling, caching.

Partitioning Strategy 1: Key Range

Example: Sharding Users by Last Name Initial

Shard 1: A-F | Shard 2: G-M | Shard 3: N-S | Shard 4: T-Z

✅ Range queries are efficient (all A-F users on Shard 1, one query)
✅ Easy to understand and reason about
❌ Hotspots: if most users start with "S", Shard 3 is overloaded
❌ A viral event (everyone named "Smith" posts simultaneously) creates a hotspot
✅ Used by: Google Bigtable, HBase (by row key range)

Partitioning Strategy 2: Hash Partitioning

Example: Hash(user_id) mod 4 shards

hash("user_123") = 7482936 → 7482936 mod 4 = 0 → Shard 0
hash("user_456") = 2947163 → 2947163 mod 4 = 3 → Shard 3

✅ Even distribution — no natural hotspots
✅ Any given key is on exactly one predictable shard
❌ Range queries are killed — "users created in January" touches ALL shards (scatter-gather)
❌ Adding a shard (5 instead of 4): all assignments change (hash mod 4 ≠ hash mod 5)
Used by: many custom sharding implementations

The Resharding Problem — Why Consistent Hashing Exists

The Math Problem with Simple Hash Mod NYou have 4 shards. Key "user_123" hashes to shard 0. You add a 5th shard. Now hash mod 5 ≠ hash mod 4 for most keys. Result: ~80% of all your data is now on the wrong shard and needs to be moved. This requires migrating terabytes of data while serving live traffic. Disastrous.
Consistent Hashing — Adding a Node Moves Only ~1/N Keys
graph LR subgraph "Hash Ring (0 to 2^32)" K1["Key: user123\nhash=45°"] --> N1["Node A\n0°-90°"] K2["Key: post456\nhash=200°"] --> N3["Node C\n180°-270°"] K3["Key: msg789\nhash=310°"] --> N4["Node D\n270°-360°"] end subgraph "Add Node E at 225°" N3b["Node C\n180°-225°"] --> NE["New Node E\n225°-270°"] end
Consistent Hashing — How It Works

Arrange a circle (hash ring) from 0 to 2^32. Place node names at positions on the ring using hash(node_name). Place keys on the ring using hash(key). Each key belongs to the nearest node clockwise from it.

Adding a node: New node E placed at position 225°. Only keys between 180° and 225° (previously on Node C) move to E. ~1/N keys migrate, not ~(N-1)/N.
Removing a node: Keys on the removed node move to the next clockwise node. Again, ~1/N keys move.
Virtual nodes: Each physical server gets 100-256 positions on the ring. This improves load balancing (one position per server leads to uneven distribution). Servers with more capacity get more virtual nodes.
Used by: Cassandra, DynamoDB, Redis Cluster, Memcached (ketama), Nginx consistent hashing

Handling Hotspots

The Celebrity Problem

Hash(user_id) distributes load evenly for normal users. But when Cristiano Ronaldo (600M followers) posts, ALL that activity maps to the same shard key (his user_id).

Solution 1: Add a random suffix to hot keys. user_ronaldo_0, user_ronaldo_1, ..., user_ronaldo_99 → 100 different shards handle his traffic. Application must read from all 100 and merge.
Solution 2: Identify hot keys and give them dedicated shards. Application code detects "celebrity" and routes specially.
Solution 3: Request coalescing at the service layer (Discord's approach) — many requests for the same hot key get coalesced into one DB query, results broadcast to all waiters.

Secondary Indexes on Sharded Databases

Local Secondary Index (by document)

  • Each shard maintains its own index for its local data
  • Write: fast — update only the local shard's index
  • Read: scatter-gather — query ALL shards and merge results
  • Used by: DynamoDB local secondary index

Global Secondary Index (by term)

  • Index is itself sharded by index key (not data key)
  • Write: slow — a write may update index shards on OTHER machines
  • Read: fast — query only the relevant index shard
  • Used by: DynamoDB global secondary index
Storage & Databases

Isolation Levels & MVCC

When multiple database transactions run simultaneously, they can interfere with each other in nasty ways. Isolation levels control how much transactions are shielded from each other. MVCC is how modern databases implement isolation without blocking reads.

The Concurrency Anomalies — What Can Go Wrong

👥 Analogy: Two People Editing a Shared Document

You and your colleague both open the same Google Doc. You read paragraph 3, decide to change it. They also read paragraph 3, also decide to change it. You save yours. They save theirs — overwriting yours. Who wins? This is the lost update problem, and databases face versions of this constantly.

Anomaly 1: Dirty Read

Timeline T1: BEGIN; UPDATE balance SET amount = 1000 WHERE user='Alice'; -- not committed yet T2: SELECT amount FROM balance WHERE user='Alice'; -- reads 1000 (dirty!) T1: ROLLBACK; -- T1 decided to abort -- T2 read data that never officially existed. T2 made decisions on phantom data.

Prevention: Read Committed isolation level (don't read uncommitted data).

Anomaly 2: Non-Repeatable Read

Timeline T1: SELECT amount FROM balance WHERE user='Alice'; -- returns 500 T2: UPDATE balance SET amount = 900 WHERE user='Alice'; COMMIT; T1: SELECT amount FROM balance WHERE user='Alice'; -- returns 900 (different!) -- T1 read the same row twice and got different values. Very confusing for T1's logic.

Prevention: Repeatable Read / Snapshot Isolation (take a snapshot at transaction start).

Anomaly 3: Phantom Read

Timeline T1: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM bookings WHERE date='2024-01-15'; -- returns 10 T2: INSERT INTO bookings(date, ...) VALUES('2024-01-15', ...); COMMIT; T1: SELECT COUNT(*) FROM bookings WHERE date='2024-01-15'; -- returns 11! -- T1 ran same query twice, got different result counts. A new "phantom" row appeared.

Prevention: Serializable isolation (next-key locks or SSI).

Anomaly 4: Write Skew (The Hardest One)

Timeline — Doctor On-Call Problem -- Hospital requires: at least 1 doctor on call at all times -- Current state: Alice (on call), Bob (on call) → 2 doctors on call T1 (Alice): SELECT COUNT(*) FROM doctors WHERE on_call=TRUE; -- returns 2 ✅ T2 (Bob): SELECT COUNT(*) FROM doctors WHERE on_call=TRUE; -- returns 2 ✅ -- Both see 2 on-call doctors. Both think it's safe to go off-call. T1 (Alice): UPDATE doctors SET on_call=FALSE WHERE name='Alice'; COMMIT; T2 (Bob): UPDATE doctors SET on_call=FALSE WHERE name='Bob'; COMMIT; -- Result: 0 doctors on call! Hospital constraint violated! -- Each transaction was individually valid, but together they're invalid. -- Only SERIALIZABLE isolation prevents this.

Isolation Levels — The Hierarchy

LevelDirty ReadNon-RepeatablePhantomWrite SkewPerformance
Read Uncommitted❌ Possible❌ Possible❌ Possible❌ PossibleFastest (avoid!)
Read Committed (most common default)✅ Safe❌ Possible❌ Possible❌ PossibleFast
Repeatable Read✅✅ Safe⚠️ (MySQL: possible, PostgreSQL: safe via MVCC)❌ PossibleModerate
Serializable✅✅✅✅ SafeSlowest (serializable in PostgreSQL uses SSI — very good)

MVCC — Multi-Version Concurrency Control

The mechanism behind PostgreSQL's isolation. The key insight: instead of locking rows (which blocks readers), keep multiple versions of each row. Readers see a consistent snapshot; writers create new versions.

📸 Analogy: Git for Database Rows

In Git, when you change a file, Git creates a new commit — the old version still exists. You can check out the old version at any time. MVCC works identically: each update creates a new row version. Old versions are kept for ongoing transactions that started before the update. When no transaction needs the old version anymore, it's vacuumed (garbage collected).

PostgreSQL MVCC Internal View -- Actual stored data (simplified) — PostgreSQL adds xmin/xmax to every row -- xmin = transaction ID that created this version -- xmax = transaction ID that deleted/updated this version (0 = still current) id | name | salary | xmin | xmax 1 | Alice | 50000 | 100 | 200 ← version created by txn 100, deleted by txn 200 1 | Alice | 60000 | 200 | 0 ← current version created by txn 200 (salary raise) 2 | Bob | 55000 | 100 | 0 ← current version -- Transaction 150 (started before txn 200) reads: sees Alice's salary as 50000 -- Transaction 250 (started after txn 200) reads: sees Alice's salary as 60000 -- No locks! Readers never block writers, writers never block readers. -- This is the GOLDEN RULE of MVCC.

SSI — Serializable Snapshot Isolation (PostgreSQL 9.1+)

PostgreSQL's implementation of true Serializable isolation WITHOUT using 2PL (Two-Phase Locking). SSI runs on MVCC, tracks read/write dependencies between transactions, and detects if a dangerous combination of reads/writes would violate serializability. If detected, it aborts one transaction (which then retries). Result: true serializability with the performance of snapshot isolation in many workloads.

Practical advice: PostgreSQL default is Read Committed. For financial transactions or anything with write skew risk, use SERIALIZABLE (PostgreSQL's SSI makes it surprisingly performant). Avoid Read Uncommitted — there's almost never a reason to use it.
Storage & Databases

Database Normalization — 1NF to BCNF

Normalization is the process of structuring a relational database to reduce data redundancy and improve data integrity. It was invented by E.F. Codd in 1970. This page teaches every normal form with concrete examples — what's wrong and how to fix it.

Why Does Normalization Matter?

📋 Analogy: Contact List

You store contacts like: "John Smith, 020-1234, john@email.com, Acme Corp, CEO, 10 Baker St, London"
John gets promoted to CTO. You update his title. But you stored him 5 times (different contexts). Now 4 copies still say CEO and 1 says CTO. Which is right? This inconsistency is exactly what normalization prevents.

Problems Normalization Solves

  • Insertion anomaly: Can't add a department until there's at least one employee in it (department is stored in the employee table)
  • Update anomaly: If a department name changes, you must update it in every employee row — miss one and data is inconsistent
  • Deletion anomaly: Deleting the last employee in a department deletes the department itself (data loss)

First Normal Form (1NF)

Rule: Each column must hold atomic (indivisible) values — no lists, no sets, no repeating groups. Each row must be uniquely identifiable.

❌ Violates 1NF — Multiple Values in One Cell
Bad table student_id | name | subjects 1 | Alice | Math, Physics, Chemistry 2 | Bob | English, History
✅ Fixed — Satisfies 1NF
Good table student_id | name | subject 1 | Alice | Math 1 | Alice | Physics 1 | Alice | Chemistry 2 | Bob | English 2 | Bob | History

Now each cell is atomic. Primary key is (student_id, subject).

Second Normal Form (2NF)

Rule: Must be in 1NF AND every non-key column must depend on the ENTIRE primary key (no partial dependencies).

Only relevant when the primary key is composite (multiple columns).

❌ Violates 2NF — Partial Dependency
Bad table -- PK: (student_id, course_id) student_id | course_id | student_name | course_name | grade 1 | CS101 | Alice | Databases | A 1 | CS102 | Alice | Networking | B -- Problem: student_name depends only on student_id (not on course_id!) -- course_name depends only on course_id (not on student_id!) -- These are partial dependencies — violates 2NF
✅ Fixed — Satisfies 2NF
Three separate tables -- Students table: student_name depends on student_id only students(student_id PK, student_name) -- Courses table: course_name depends on course_id only courses(course_id PK, course_name) -- Enrollments table: grade depends on BOTH student_id AND course_id enrollments(student_id FK, course_id FK, grade) -- PK: (student_id, course_id)

Third Normal Form (3NF)

Rule: Must be in 2NF AND no transitive dependencies (non-key column depends on another non-key column).

❌ Violates 3NF — Transitive Dependency
Bad table employees(emp_id PK, name, dept_id, dept_name, dept_budget) -- dept_name and dept_budget depend on dept_id, not emp_id! -- dept_id → dept_name → this is a transitive dependency through dept_id -- If "Sales" department changes name to "Revenue", you must update every employee row
✅ Fixed — Satisfies 3NF
Separate tables employees(emp_id PK, name, dept_id FK) departments(dept_id PK, dept_name, dept_budget) -- Now dept_name change: update ONE row in departments table. Done.

Boyce-Codd Normal Form (BCNF)

Rule: Stricter 3NF. For every functional dependency A → B, A must be a superkey (determines all attributes of the row). Handles the edge case where there are overlapping candidate keys.

❌ Violates BCNF — A non-superkey determines another column
Bad table — Teaching assignments -- A professor teaches only one subject but may teach multiple students -- A student-subject pair has only one professor (student_id, subject, professor) -- Candidate Keys: (student_id, subject) OR (student_id, professor) -- Problem: professor → subject (a professor only teaches one subject) -- But 'professor' is NOT a superkey — it violates BCNF student_id | subject | professor 1 | CS101 | Dr. Smith 2 | CS101 | Dr. Smith 3 | CS102 | Dr. Jones
✅ Fixed — Satisfies BCNF
Decomposed tables professor_subject(professor PK, subject) -- professor → subject student_professor(student_id, professor) -- which professor teaches each student

When to Denormalize

Normalization is the default for OLTP. Denormalize only when you have evidence of a read performance problem caused by joins.

Common denormalization patterns:
• Materialised views: Pre-computed JOIN results stored as a table, auto-updated by DB triggers or periodically. PostgreSQL supports CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW.
• Inline common lookups: Store user.name in the orders table alongside user_id to avoid a JOIN on every order query.
• Star schema: OLAP/warehouse pattern — one large "fact" table surrounded by "dimension" tables. Joins are pre-materialized or happen in the analytical engine.
Storage & Databases

OLTP vs OLAP & Data Warehouses

OLTP is the database for running your business. OLAP is the database for understanding your business. They have completely different requirements and completely different architectures.

OLTP vs OLAP — The Core Difference

DimensionOLTP (Online Transaction Processing)OLAP (Online Analytical Processing)
PurposeRun day-to-day operationsAnalyse historical data, extract insights
UsersApp servers, end users (millions)Data analysts, business intelligence (tens)
Query typeShort, frequent: read/write individual rowsLong, infrequent: aggregate millions of rows
Query exampleSELECT * FROM users WHERE id=123 (single user)SELECT AVG(revenue) GROUP BY month (all orders ever)
Data volumeGB to low TBTB to PB
IndexesMany (B-tree on query columns)Few or none; column compression instead
Data freshnessReal-time (millisecond freshness)Hourly/daily batch + some real-time streaming
ExamplesPostgreSQL, MySQL, DynamoDBClickHouse, Snowflake, BigQuery, Redshift, Apache Druid
Why You Need Two Separate Systems

Analyst runs: "Calculate total revenue by country for the last 3 years, grouped by month, for all product categories." This scans 50 million orders on your OLTP PostgreSQL database → takes 45 minutes → holds locks → your app servers slow to a crawl → customers can't check out.

Solution: ETL/CDC pipeline copies data to a separate analytical database. Analyst queries that. OLTP database untouched.

Column-Oriented Storage — Why OLAP Databases Are Different

📊 Analogy: Filing Cabinet vs Spreadsheet

Row-oriented (OLTP): Like a filing cabinet — all data about one person in one folder. Reading one person is fast. Calculating the average age of everyone requires opening every folder.

Column-oriented (OLAP): Like a spreadsheet where you can pull an entire column at once. Calculating average age reads ONLY the age column — skips names, emails, addresses. Far less I/O for analytical queries.

Row vs Column Storage -- Table: orders(order_id, user_id, amount, country, date) -- Row-oriented (PostgreSQL) stores: -- [1, 123, 99.99, "India", "2024-01-01"] [2, 456, 149.99, "US", "2024-01-02"] ... -- To calculate SUM(amount): reads ALL columns of ALL rows -- Column-oriented (ClickHouse) stores: -- order_id column: [1, 2, 3, 4, 5...] -- user_id column: [123, 456, 789...] -- amount column: [99.99, 149.99, 79.99...] ← only THIS is read for SUM(amount)! -- country column: ["India", "US", "UK"...] -- date column: ["2024-01-01", "2024-01-02"...] -- Query: SELECT country, SUM(amount) FROM orders GROUP BY country -- Column-oriented reads ONLY amount and country columns → ~10× less I/O than row-oriented

Column Compression — Why Analytical DBs Are Compact

Column-oriented databases store values of the same type together, enabling powerful compression:

  • Run-length encoding: country column: ["India","India","India","India","US","US"] → [("India", 4), ("US", 2)]. 6 values → 2 values. 3× compression.
  • Dictionary encoding: Replace repeated strings with integers. "United States" → 0, "India" → 1, "Germany" → 2. Huge savings when text columns repeat.
  • Delta encoding: For timestamps: [1704067200, 1704067260, 1704067320] → [1704067200, +60, +60]. Store differences instead of absolute values.
  • Typical result: Column stores achieve 5-20× compression vs row stores. More data fits in RAM → faster queries.

ETL / CDC — Getting Data Into the Warehouse

Data pipeline: OLTP → Warehouse
graph LR PG["PostgreSQL\n(OLTP)"] -->|"CDC via\nDebezium"| K["Kafka\n(event stream)"] K --> T["Transform\n(dbt, Spark)"] T --> DW["Snowflake / BigQuery\n(OLAP Warehouse)"] DW --> BI["BI Tools\n(Tableau, Looker)"] PG2["MySQL\n(OLTP)"] -->|"CDC"| K MONGO["MongoDB\n(OLTP)"] -->|"CDC"| K
Storage & Databases

Federation & Denormalization

Federation (functional partitioning) splits a monolithic database into several smaller databases by function. Denormalization adds redundant data to avoid expensive joins. Both are strategies for scaling databases beyond what one server can handle.

Federation — Split by Function

🏢 Analogy: Company Departments

A startup starts with one office (monolithic database). As it grows: sales team gets their own CRM system, engineering gets GitHub, HR gets their own system, finance gets QuickBooks. Each team owns their data. No single bottleneck. This is federation.

Monolithic DB → Federated (Functional Partitioning)
graph TB Old["One Big PostgreSQL\nusers + products +\norders + payments +\nreviews + analytics"] New1["Users DB\n(auth, profiles)"] New2["Products DB\n(catalogue, inventory)"] New3["Orders DB\n(checkout, history)"] New4["Payments DB\n(billing, refunds)"] Old -->|"grows to 5TB+,\n100K writes/sec"| New1 Old --> New2 Old --> New3 Old --> New4

Benefits of Federation

  • Smaller databases fit better in RAM → more cache hits
  • Less replication lag (each DB replicates independently)
  • Teams own their data (Conway's Law alignment)
  • Can use different DB types per function (Users: Postgres, Sessions: Redis, Products: Elasticsearch)
  • Writes are parallelised — no single serialisation bottleneck

When Federation Isn't Enough

Federation helps until a single function (e.g., the Orders database) itself outgrows one machine. At that point, you need sharding within that function. Federation and sharding work together — federation splits by function, sharding splits by data within a function.

The Cross-Database JOIN ProblemThe biggest cost of federation: you can no longer do SELECT orders.*, users.name FROM orders JOIN users if those tables are on different servers. You must: (1) Fetch from both DBs separately in application code, (2) Denormalize (store user_name in orders table), or (3) Use a distributed query engine. This is why you should denormalize after federating.

Denormalization — Strategic Data Duplication

After federation/sharding makes JOINs expensive, the cure is denormalization: store the data you need in multiple places so you never need to JOIN across services.

SQL — Before and After Denormalization -- BEFORE (normalized, requires join across services) -- To show a tweet: fetch tweet (tweets service), then fetch user info (users service) → 2 network calls tweets(tweet_id, user_id, content, created_at) users(user_id, name, avatar_url, username) -- Query requires JOIN across two separate databases → expensive SELECT t.content, u.name, u.avatar_url FROM tweets t JOIN users u ON t.user_id = u.user_id WHERE t.tweet_id = 12345; -- AFTER (denormalized — author data stored directly in tweet) tweets(tweet_id, user_id, content, created_at, author_name, -- duplicated from users table author_username, -- duplicated author_avatar_url -- duplicated ) -- Query hits ONE table, ONE service, ONE database → fast and simple SELECT content, author_name, author_avatar_url FROM tweets WHERE tweet_id = 12345; -- Trade-off: if user changes their name, must update all their tweet rows too
Materialized Views — Semi-automated DenormalizationPostgreSQL's CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW stores the result of a complex query as a physical table. It must be manually refreshed (REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY) or triggered by events. Much better for dashboard-style read-heavy queries where a few minutes of staleness is acceptable.
Storage & Databases

SQL Performance Tuning

A well-written query can be 10,000× faster than a poorly written one on the same data. This page covers the tools and techniques to find, diagnose, and fix slow SQL queries.

The Performance Tuning Process

  1. Measure First — Don't Guess

    Enable the slow query log (MySQL) or check pg_stat_statements (PostgreSQL) to find the actual slow queries. Never optimise a query you haven't measured as slow.

  2. EXPLAIN ANALYZE — Understand the Query Plan

    Run EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) to see what the database actually does. Look for: Seq Scan on large tables (missing index), high row estimates vs actual (stale statistics), and high cost nodes.

  3. Fix the Bottleneck

    Add an index, rewrite the query, add a covering index, partition the table, materialise a common sub-query. Then measure again.

  4. Verify Improvement

    Re-run EXPLAIN ANALYZE and the actual query. Confirm the improvement is real and the plan changed as expected.

Common Anti-Patterns and Fixes

Anti-Pattern 1: SELECT *

SQL -- ❌ Fetches all 50 columns even though you only need 3 SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = 'alice@example.com'; -- ✅ Fetch only what you need → enables covering index, less I/O SELECT id, name, role FROM users WHERE email = 'alice@example.com';

Anti-Pattern 2: OFFSET Pagination (at scale)

SQL -- ❌ Slow at large pages — DB scans 100,000 rows to skip them SELECT id, title FROM posts ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20 OFFSET 100000; -- ✅ Cursor pagination — always fast (uses index seek) SELECT id, title FROM posts WHERE created_at < '2024-01-15T10:00:00Z' -- last item from previous page ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20; -- At any page depth, this is always O(log n) not O(n)

Anti-Pattern 3: N+1 Queries

Python (Django ORM example) # ❌ N+1: 1 query for posts + N queries for each author (100 posts = 101 queries) posts = Post.objects.all()[:100] for post in posts: print(post.author.name) # triggers a new SQL query each time! # ✅ Eager loading: 2 queries total (posts + all authors in one IN clause) posts = Post.objects.select_related('author').all()[:100] for post in posts: print(post.author.name) # no extra query — already loaded

Anti-Pattern 4: Functions on Indexed Columns (Index Killer)

SQL -- ❌ Function on column prevents index use → full scan! SELECT * FROM users WHERE LOWER(email) = 'alice@example.com'; SELECT * FROM orders WHERE DATE(created_at) = '2024-01-15'; -- ✅ Rewrite to keep column bare SELECT * FROM users WHERE email = LOWER('Alice@Example.com'); -- Or create a functional index: CREATE INDEX idx_lower_email ON users (LOWER(email)); SELECT * FROM orders WHERE created_at >= '2024-01-15' AND created_at < '2024-01-16';

CHAR vs VARCHAR — Performance Nuance

Should I use CHAR or VARCHAR?
CHAR(n): fixed-width, padded with spaces. Fixed-width means random access is possible — to jump to the 5th character, the DB multiplies offset × 5 bytes. Simple and predictable.

VARCHAR(n): variable-width, stores only actual characters. Saves space. But finding the end of a string requires scanning forward — slightly more CPU work.

Practical advice: use VARCHAR for almost everything. Use CHAR(n) only for truly fixed-length data like ISO country codes (CHAR(2)), UUIDs (CHAR(36)), hash values.

Schema Design Tips from System Design Primer

  • Use NOT NULL constraints where possible — avoids three-valued logic and allows some optimisations
  • Use INT for foreign keys not BIGINT unless you have >2.1B rows
  • Use DECIMAL for currency (never FLOAT — floating point errors will corrupt your money)
  • Store large binary data (images/PDFs) in object storage (S3), store only the URL in the DB
  • Set table statistics sample sizes with ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN ... SET STATISTICS 500 for high-cardinality columns the optimiser gets wrong
  • Run VACUUM ANALYZE after bulk loads to refresh statistics

Connection Pooling — A Critical Query Performance Tool

Each new database connection is expensive: TCP handshake + authentication + allocating a server process (~5MB RAM in PostgreSQL). Connection pooling reuses a fixed set of connections. See the dedicated Connection Pooling page for full details including the formula.

Caching

Cache Failure Modes

Caches make systems fast, but when they fail, they can take down your entire database. These four failure patterns are well-documented production disasters. Understanding them — and their fixes — is essential for any production system.

Failure 1: Cache Penetration

🔍 Analogy: Phone Book Prank

Someone keeps calling the library asking for phone numbers of people who don't exist. Every call forces the librarian to check the entire phone book and confirm the person isn't listed. If you get 1000 such calls, the librarian is overwhelmed — even though there's nothing to find.

What it is: Requests for keys that don't exist in cache OR database. The cache misses, the DB query returns empty, nothing gets cached, every identical request hits the database.

Attack vector: Malicious user makes requests with user_id=-1, user_id=-2... (IDs that don't exist). Cache misses every time. Every request hits DB. DoS attack.

Cache Penetration Flow
graph LR A["Request: GET user_id=-999"] --> C{Cache?} C -->|"MISS"| D{Database?} D -->|"NOT FOUND"| N["Return null\n(nothing cached!)"] N -->|"Same request again\n×1000/sec"| A D -->|"1000 req/sec\nhitting DB"| BOOM["DB overwhelmed! 💥"]

Fixes for Cache Penetration

Python — Cache Null Values def get_user(user_id): cache_key = f"user:{user_id}" cached = redis.get(cache_key) if cached == b"NULL": # sentinel value for "definitely not in DB" return None if cached is not None: return json.loads(cached) user = db.query(f"SELECT * FROM users WHERE id={user_id}") if user is None: redis.setex(cache_key, 300, b"NULL") # cache the "not found" for 5min return None redis.setex(cache_key, 3600, json.dumps(user)) return user
Python — Bloom Filter (Better Solution) from pybloom_live import BloomFilter bloom = BloomFilter(capacity=10000000, error_rate=0.001) # 10M items, 0.1% FPR # Pre-populate at startup with all valid user IDs for uid in db.query("SELECT id FROM users"): bloom.add(uid) def get_user(user_id): if user_id not in bloom: return None # definitely not in DB (no false negatives!) → skip DB entirely # ... proceed with cache-aside lookup

Failure 2: Cache Breakdown (Hot Key Expiry)

What it is: A single very popular ("hot") key expires from cache. At the moment of expiry, thousands of concurrent requests all get a cache miss simultaneously and all hit the database for the same key. The DB gets hammered by thousands of identical queries.

🎬 Scenario: Movie Release

A new Marvel movie releases. The "latest movie" cache key (used by millions) expires. 500,000 users simultaneously refresh the homepage. All 500,000 requests hit the database at once. The database falls over trying to answer the same query 500,000 times.

Fixes for Cache Breakdown

Python — Distributed Lock (Mutex) import redis def get_hot_movie_data(movie_id): cached = redis.get(f"movie:{movie_id}") if cached: return json.loads(cached) lock_key = f"lock:movie:{movie_id}" lock_acquired = redis.set(lock_key, "1", nx=True, ex=10) # NX = only if not exists if lock_acquired: # Only ONE thread reaches here — rebuilds the cache data = db.query(f"SELECT * FROM movies WHERE id={movie_id}") redis.setex(f"movie:{movie_id}", 3600, json.dumps(data)) redis.delete(lock_key) return data else: # All other threads wait briefly and retry time.sleep(0.1) return get_hot_movie_data(movie_id) # now cache is populated

Alternative fix: Never expire hot keys. Run a background job to proactively refresh them before expiry. Identify hot keys via monitoring (high hit rate keys in Redis keyspace notifications).

Failure 3: Cache Avalanche (Thundering Herd)

What it is: MANY keys expire at the same time (not just one). All the traffic for those keys hits the database simultaneously. Often happens after a cache restart (cold start) or if you set the same TTL for many keys on the same deploy.

The Most Common Cause

Python — ❌ Same TTL for everything (dangerous!) # You deploy at midnight and cache 10,000 products with 1-hour TTL for product in products: redis.setex(f"product:{product.id}", 3600, json.dumps(product)) # At 1am: ALL 10,000 cache keys expire simultaneously → 10,000 DB queries in <1s → DB dies -- ✅ TTL Jitter — stagger expirations randomly import random for product in products: jitter = random.randint(0, 600) # 0-10 minutes random redis.setex(f"product:{product.id}", 3600 + jitter, json.dumps(product)) # Now keys expire over a 10-minute window → max 1,000/min DB queries → manageable

Failure 4: Cache Crash

What it is: The entire cache tier goes down. All requests fall through to the database. The database wasn't designed to handle this load (that's why you had a cache!).

Fixes

Prevention

  • Redis cluster (3+ nodes, automatic failover)
  • Redis Sentinel (monitors + promotes replica)
  • Multi-AZ deployment

Recovery

  • Circuit breaker — stop cache-backed endpoints, serve degraded responses
  • Rate limiting — limit DB requests to what it can handle
  • Load shedding — reject non-critical requests to protect core functionality
Messaging & Streaming

Back Pressure

Back pressure is the mechanism by which a slow consumer tells a fast producer to slow down. Without it, queues grow without bound until the system runs out of memory and crashes. Understanding back pressure is crucial for designing resilient systems.

The Problem: Unbounded Queues

🚰 Analogy: Water Pipes

You have a large pipe feeding into a small pipe. If you pour water faster than the small pipe can drain, it backs up into a reservoir (queue). If the reservoir has no upper limit, it fills until it overflows (OOM crash). Back pressure = the reservoir telling the large pipe to slow its flow when it's getting full.

Without vs With Back Pressure
graph TB subgraph "❌ No Back Pressure" P1["Producer: 10,000 msgs/sec"] --> Q1["Queue: ∞ size\nGrows to 10GB... 50GB..."] Q1 --> C1["Consumer: 1,000 msgs/sec"] Q1 --> OOM["💥 Out of Memory"] end subgraph "✅ With Back Pressure" P2["Producer: starts at 10,000 msgs/sec"] --> Q2["Queue: max 10,000 items\nWhen full: reject new items"] Q2 -->|"429 Too Many Requests\nor blocking"| P3["Producer slows to 1,000 msgs/sec"] Q2 --> C2["Consumer: 1,000 msgs/sec"] end

Implementation Patterns

Pattern 1: Bounded Queues

Python import asyncio # Bounded queue — max 1000 items. Putting to a full queue BLOCKS the producer. queue = asyncio.Queue(maxsize=1000) async def producer(): while True: item = await generate_item() await queue.put(item) # blocks if queue is full → natural back pressure async def consumer(): while True: item = await queue.get() await process_item(item) queue.task_done()

Pattern 2: HTTP 429 (Rate Limiting as Back Pressure)

HTTP Response HTTP/1.1 429 Too Many Requests Retry-After: 5 Content-Type: application/json {"error": "rate_limited", "retry_after_seconds": 5} -- Client should back off and retry after 5 seconds -- With exponential backoff: wait 5s, then 10s, then 20s...

Pattern 3: Message Broker Prefetch Limit (Celery/RabbitMQ)

Python (Celery) # Without prefetch limit: Celery worker grabs ALL available tasks at startup # Each task is big (100MB image processing), worker runs OOM # With prefetch limit: worker only takes 1 task at a time CELERY_WORKER_PREFETCH_MULTIPLIER = 1 # take one task, process it, take next # Now worker RAM usage is bounded: 1 task × 100MB = 100MB. Safe.

Pattern 4: TCP Flow Control — Back Pressure Built into the Protocol

TCP has built-in back pressure: the receiver advertises a "receive window" (how much buffer space it has). The sender cannot send more than the window size without getting an ack. When the receiver's buffer fills, the window shrinks to 0, and the sender stops. This is why HTTP file uploads automatically throttle when the network is slow — TCP is applying back pressure.

Reactive Streams — Standardised Back Pressure

The Reactive Streams specification (implemented by RxJava, Project Reactor, Akka Streams) formalises back pressure as a demand signal: the subscriber tells the publisher "I want 10 items". The publisher sends exactly 10, then waits for the next demand signal. This prevents any part of the pipeline from running ahead of what downstream can handle.

Back Pressure in System Design InterviewsWhen designing a pipeline (ETL, processing service, streaming), always ask: "What happens if the consumer slows down?" If the answer is "the queue grows until we crash," you're missing back pressure. Mention bounded queues, rate limiting on ingestion, or reactive-streams demand signaling as your solution.
Messaging & Streaming

AWS SQS vs SNS vs EventBridge vs Kinesis

AWS has four major messaging services, each designed for different patterns. Picking the wrong one is a common system design mistake. This page explains each one and gives you a clear decision matrix.

The Four Services at a Glance

ServiceModelAnalogyBest For
SQSQueue (point-to-point)A task inbox — one person reads each messageDecoupling services, job queues, buffering
SNSPub/Sub (fan-out)A group notification — everyone subscribed gets itSending notifications to multiple targets
EventBridgeEvent Bus (content-based routing)Smart router — routes based on event contentComplex event routing, SaaS integrations, rules-based routing
KinesisOrdered streamA conveyor belt — ordered, replayableReal-time analytics, ordered processing, high-volume streams

AWS SQS — Simple Queue Service

Architecture: One message, one consumer

An order is placed → OrderService puts a message in SQS queue → PaymentWorker reads it → PaymentWorker processes payment → deletes message from queue. The message is consumed exactly once (SQS standard: at-least-once; SQS FIFO: exactly-once).

Python (boto3) import boto3 sqs = boto3.client('sqs', region_name='us-east-1') # Send message sqs.send_message( QueueUrl='https://sqs.us-east-1.amazonaws.com/123/my-queue', MessageBody=json.dumps({'order_id': 12345, 'amount': 99.99}), MessageGroupId='payments' # FIFO queue grouping ) # Receive and process messages = sqs.receive_message(QueueUrl=queue_url, MaxNumberOfMessages=10) for msg in messages.get('Messages', []): process_payment(json.loads(msg['Body'])) sqs.delete_message(QueueUrl=queue_url, ReceiptHandle=msg['ReceiptHandle'])
SQS Visibility TimeoutWhen a consumer receives a message, SQS hides it from other consumers for a "visibility timeout" (default 30s). If the consumer processes and deletes it: done. If the consumer crashes before deleting: the message becomes visible again after the timeout and another consumer picks it up. This is how SQS achieves at-least-once delivery.

AWS SNS — Simple Notification Service

Architecture: One message, many consumers

User registers → PublishEvent to SNS topic "user-registered" → SNS fans out to: SQS queue (email worker), SQS queue (analytics worker), Lambda (send Slack notification), email endpoint (admin alert). All receive the same message simultaneously.

SNS + SQS Fan-out Pattern (Most Common AWS Pattern)Publish to SNS → SNS delivers to multiple SQS queues → each queue has its own consumer(s). This decouples the producer from all consumers AND gives each consumer its own queue for buffering and retry. The Gold Standard for AWS event-driven architectures.

AWS EventBridge

Architecture: Route events by their content

An order event arrives: {"type": "ORDER_PLACED", "amount": 500, "country": "US", "risk_level": "high"}
EventBridge rules: if type=ORDER_PLACED AND risk_level=high → send to FraudDetectionService. If type=ORDER_PLACED AND amount>100 → send to RevenueAnalytics. If type=ORDER_SHIPPED → send to CustomerNotification.
One event, multiple intelligent routes based on content.

AWS Kinesis — Real-Time Stream Processing

Architecture: Ordered, replayable, high-volume stream

Clickstream data from 10 million users arrives at 100,000 events/sec. You want: (1) Real-time dashboard updating every second, (2) Fraud detection running on each event, (3) Replay capability — re-process last 7 days of events with a new ML model.
Kinesis handles all three simultaneously. Data is ordered per shard (partition key), retained for 24h–365d, and multiple consumers can read independently at different positions.

Decision Matrix

ScenarioServiceWhy
Process an image after uploadSQSOne-task, one-worker, retry on failure
Send welcome email + Slack notification + update analytics after signupSNS → multiple SQSFan-out to independent consumers
Route events from 50 microservices with complex rulesEventBridgeContent-based routing, no custom routing logic in code
Real-time fraud detection on transaction streamKinesisOrdered, high-throughput, multi-consumer, replayable
Already using Kafka, need AWS managed versionMSK (Managed Kafka)Kafka-compatible if you need Kafka wire protocol
Architecture Patterns

The Saga Pattern

When a business process spans multiple microservices (order → payment → inventory → shipping), you need everything to succeed or be rolled back. Traditional database transactions don't work across services. The Saga pattern solves this with a sequence of local transactions, each with a compensating action.

Why You Can't Use Database Transactions Across Microservices

🏦 The Multi-Bank Transfer Problem

You want to move money from your HSBC account to a Barclays account. A single transaction across two separate banks is impossible — they're different systems with no shared database. Instead, you: (1) Debit HSBC, (2) Credit Barclays. If step 2 fails, you run a compensating action: refund HSBC.

Microservices have the same problem. Each service has its own database. There's no distributed ACID transaction. The Saga pattern is the solution.

Saga Style 1: Choreography (Event-Driven)

Choreography Saga — No central coordinator
sequenceDiagram participant OS as Order Service participant K as Kafka participant PS as Payment Service participant IS as Inventory Service participant SS as Shipping Service OS->>K: OrderCreated {order_id: 123, total: $99} K->>PS: OrderCreated event PS->>PS: Charge credit card ✅ PS->>K: PaymentCharged {order_id: 123} K->>IS: PaymentCharged event IS->>IS: Reserve items ✅ IS->>K: ItemsReserved {order_id: 123} K->>SS: ItemsReserved event SS->>SS: Create shipment ✅
Compensating Actions (Rollback)What if Payment fails? PaymentService publishes: PaymentFailed. OrderService listens → cancels order. What if Inventory fails after Payment? InventoryService publishes: InventoryFailed. PaymentService listens → issues refund. Each service knows how to "undo" its action — this is the compensating transaction.

Saga Style 2: Orchestration (Central Coordinator)

Orchestration Saga — Central Saga Orchestrator directs each step
sequenceDiagram participant SO as Saga Orchestrator participant PS as Payment Service participant IS as Inventory Service participant SS as Shipping Service SO->>PS: Command: ChargePayment($99) PS-->>SO: PaymentSuccess SO->>IS: Command: ReserveItems(items) IS-->>SO: ItemsFailed (out of stock) SO->>PS: Command: RefundPayment($99) ← compensating action PS-->>SO: RefundSuccess SO->>SO: Saga Complete (failed, refunded)

Choreography vs Orchestration — When to Use Each

ChoreographyOrchestration
CoordinationServices react to events — no central authorityOne orchestrator commands each step
CouplingLow — services only know about eventsHigher — orchestrator knows all services
VisibilityHard to trace — business logic distributed across servicesEasy — orchestrator has full saga state
ComplexitySimple for 2-3 steps; grows complex beyond thatBetter for 4+ steps or branching logic
TestingHard — must simulate the full event chainEasy — orchestrator can be unit tested
Use whenSimple linear flows, loose coupling priorityComplex business workflows, visibility needed

Critical Rules for Sagas

Every step must be:
1. Idempotent: If a step runs twice (due to retry), the second run produces the same result. "Charge $99 for order_id=123" must check if it was already charged and skip if so.
2. Paired with a compensating action: Every step has an inverse. ChargePayment → RefundPayment. ReserveItems → ReleaseItems. The compensation must also be idempotent.
3. The "pivot transaction": There's always one step that once completed, the saga must roll forward (not back). Typically this is the payment step. Before pivot → can rollback. After pivot → must compensate forward.
Architecture Patterns

The Outbox Pattern

One of the most important patterns for event-driven microservices. Solves the "dual-write problem" — guaranteeing that a database write and an event publish either both happen or neither happens.

The Dual-Write Problem

The Problem Without Outbox
Python — Dangerous dual write def create_order(order): db.execute("INSERT INTO orders VALUES (?)", order) # Step 1: DB write # ⚠️ Server crashes HERE ← between the two writes kafka.publish("OrderCreated", order) # Step 2: Publish event # Order is in DB but event was never published! # Payment service, inventory service, shipping service all miss this order # Data is inconsistent forever.

You cannot make a database write and a Kafka publish atomic — they're two different systems. A crash between the two leaves you with a database record but no event, or an event with no database record.

The Outbox Pattern Solution

Outbox Pattern — Atomic write + reliable event delivery
graph LR A["Application"] -->|"Single DB transaction"| TXN["BEGIN TRANSACTION"] TXN --> T1["INSERT INTO orders\n(business data)"] TXN --> T2["INSERT INTO outbox\n(event to publish)"] TXN --> T3["COMMIT"] RELAY["Message Relay\n(Debezium CDC)"] -->|"polls outbox\nor tails WAL"| T3 RELAY -->|"publishes"| K["Kafka / Event Bus"] K --> P["Payment Service,\nInventory Service..."] style TXN fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757

Implementation

SQL + Python -- 1. Create the outbox table in your database CREATE TABLE outbox ( id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(), event_type VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL, payload JSONB NOT NULL, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), published_at TIMESTAMPTZ -- NULL = not yet published );
Python — Atomic write to orders + outbox def create_order(order_data): with db.transaction(): # SINGLE TRANSACTION order = db.execute("INSERT INTO orders ... RETURNING id", order_data) # Write the event to the SAME database in the SAME transaction db.execute(""" INSERT INTO outbox (event_type, payload) VALUES ('OrderCreated', ?) """, json.dumps({'order_id': order.id, 'total': order_data['total']})) # If ANY step fails: entire transaction rolls back. Atomicity guaranteed. # The outbox row is only written if the order is written. Perfect sync.
Python — Message Relay (publishes outbox events) # Runs as a separate process (or use Debezium CDC for automatic WAL-based relay) while True: events = db.execute("SELECT * FROM outbox WHERE published_at IS NULL ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 100") for event in events: kafka.publish(event.event_type, event.payload) db.execute("UPDATE outbox SET published_at=NOW() WHERE id=?", event.id) time.sleep(1)
Debezium — The Production-Grade ApproachDebezium is an open-source CDC tool that tails your database's transaction log (PostgreSQL WAL, MySQL binlog) and publishes changes to Kafka automatically. Rather than polling the outbox table, Debezium gets notified of new rows the moment they're committed. No polling delay, no extra queries, no missed events. Used by Shopify, Goldman Sachs, IBM, and thousands of others. The outbox pattern + Debezium = the gold standard for reliable event publishing in microservices.
Architecture Patterns

Service Discovery

In microservices, how does Service A know where to find Service B? IP addresses change (containers restart, pods scale). Service discovery solves this — a registry maps service names to current network locations.

The Problem Service Discovery Solves

📞 Analogy: Phone Book vs Contact Card

Your friend changes phone number every few weeks (like containers restarting with new IPs). If you hardcode their number, you'll have a stale number. Instead, they always update their name in a phone book. You call the phone book, get the current number. The phone book is the service registry.

Pattern 1: Client-Side Discovery

Client queries registry directly and load-balances
graph LR Client --> Reg["Service Registry\n(Eureka/Consul)"] Reg --> Client Client -->|"Round-robin to\nselected IP"| S1["Service B\nIP: 10.0.0.1:8080"] Client --> S2["Service B\nIP: 10.0.0.2:8080"] S1 & S2 -->|"heartbeat\nregistration"| Reg

How it works: Client asks registry "give me instances of Service B". Registry returns list of healthy IPs. Client picks one (round-robin, random, or other LB algorithm) and connects directly.

✅ Client has full control over load balancing. ✅ Low latency — direct connection, no proxy. ❌ Client code must implement discovery logic (registry client library for every language). ❌ If registry is down, clients can't find services.

Used by: Netflix Eureka + Ribbon, Consul with client libraries.

Pattern 2: Server-Side Discovery

Client talks to load balancer; LB queries registry
graph LR Client -->|"GET /orders\n(doesn't know IPs)"| LB["Load Balancer / API Gateway\n(nginx, Envoy, AWS ALB)"] LB -->|"queries registry"| Reg["Service Registry"] Reg -->|"healthy instances"| LB LB --> S1["Order Service\n10.0.0.1"] LB --> S2["Order Service\n10.0.0.2"]

✅ Client is completely ignorant of service topology — just talks to one endpoint. ✅ Works for any client (browser, mobile, third-party). ❌ Load balancer becomes a potential SPOF and bottleneck. ❌ Additional network hop.

Used by: Kubernetes (kube-proxy + Service DNS), AWS ALB with ECS/EKS, Nginx with Consul Template.

Key Service Registry Technologies

ToolConsensusWhat it providesUsed by
ConsulRaftService registry, health checks, K-V store, service mesh, DNS interfaceHashiCorp ecosystem, Kubernetes (alternative to etcd)
etcdRaftDistributed K-V store; Kubernetes uses it for all cluster stateKubernetes (core component), many cloud-native tools
ZooKeeperZAB (Paxos-like)Distributed coordination, leader election, config, namingApache Kafka (replacing with KRaft), HBase, older Hadoop ecosystem
Kubernetes DNSetcd-backedAutomatic DNS for Services: service-name.namespace.svc.cluster.localAll Kubernetes workloads

Health Checks — The Heartbeat Mechanism

Consul health check config { "service": { "name": "order-service", "port": 8080, "check": { "http": "http://localhost:8080/health", // HTTP health check endpoint "interval": "10s", // check every 10 seconds "timeout": "2s", // fail if no response in 2s "deregister_critical_service_after": "30s" // remove from registry after 30s down } } }
Kubernetes — Service Discovery is Built InIn Kubernetes, you define a Service object. Kubernetes automatically: assigns a stable DNS name (my-service.my-namespace.svc.cluster.local) → routes traffic to healthy Pods → load balances across replicas → updates routing when Pods restart. You never hardcode IP addresses. This is the most common service discovery mechanism in 2024.
Architecture Patterns

Task Queues & Celery

Task queues are for running background jobs — things that take too long to do in a web request. Celery is Python's de-facto standard task queue library, used by Instagram, Dropbox, Mozilla, and thousands of others.

Message Queue vs Task Queue — What's Different

Message Queue (SQS, RabbitMQ)Task Queue (Celery)
LevelTransport: moves opaque messagesApplication: runs Python functions
RetriesMessage re-delivered after visibility timeoutConfigurable retry logic with backoff
SchedulingNo built-in schedulingCelery Beat: cron-like scheduling
Result storageNoYes — results stored in result backend (Redis)
Rate limitingNoPer-task and per-worker rate limiting
RoutingQueue-basedRoute tasks to specific queues/workers

Celery Architecture

Celery Architecture
graph LR Web["Web Server\n(Django/Flask)"] -->|"task.delay()"| Broker["Message Broker\n(Redis or RabbitMQ)"] Broker --> W1["Celery Worker 1"] Broker --> W2["Celery Worker 2"] Broker --> W3["Celery Worker 3"] W1 & W2 & W3 -->|"store result"| RB["Result Backend\n(Redis)"] Web -->|"AsyncResult.get()"| RB Beat["Celery Beat\n(Scheduler)"] -->|"periodic tasks"| Broker

Getting Started with Celery

Python — Setup and basic task # celery.py from celery import Celery app = Celery( 'myapp', broker='redis://localhost:6379/0', # where tasks are queued backend='redis://localhost:6379/1' # where results are stored ) app.conf.update( task_serializer='json', result_serializer='json', accept_content=['json'], task_acks_late=True, # ack AFTER task completes, not before (crash safety) worker_prefetch_multiplier=1, # take one task at a time (back pressure) ) # tasks.py @app.task( bind=True, max_retries=3, autoretry_for=(Exception,), retry_backoff=True, # exponential backoff: 1s, 2s, 4s ) def send_welcome_email(self, user_id): user = User.objects.get(pk=user_id) try: email_service.send_welcome(user.email) except Exception as exc: raise self.retry(exc=exc, countdown=2 ** self.request.retries)
Python — Calling tasks from Django/Flask # In your view/API handler: def register_user(request): user = User.objects.create(**request.data) # Fire and forget — returns immediately, task runs in background send_welcome_email.delay(user.id) # With countdown (delay execution) send_followup_email.apply_async(args=[user.id], countdown=86400) # 1 day later # Chain tasks (output of one → input of next) chain = process_image.s(image_id) | generate_thumbnails.s() | update_cdn.s() chain.delay() return JsonResponse({'user_id': user.id})

Production Celery Configuration

Python — celery settings # Separate queues for different priority/type app.conf.task_routes = { 'tasks.send_email': {'queue': 'email'}, # fast queue 'tasks.process_video': {'queue': 'heavy'}, # slow queue, dedicated workers 'tasks.send_analytics': {'queue': 'low_priority'} # can lag } # Start workers for specific queues # celery -A myapp worker -Q email --concurrency=20 -P gevent # I/O-bound email # celery -A myapp worker -Q heavy --concurrency=4 # CPU-bound video # Celery Beat — periodic tasks (cron equivalent) app.conf.beat_schedule = { 'cleanup-old-sessions': { 'task': 'tasks.cleanup_sessions', 'schedule': crontab(hour=2, minute=0), # every day at 2am }, }
Celery Idempotency RequirementTasks must be idempotent — running twice must produce the same result. Celery guarantees at-least-once delivery (with acks_late=True). A task CAN run twice if: the worker crashes after starting but before ACKing, or during deployment. Always check "has this already been done?" at the start of a task: if email_already_sent(user_id): return.
Architecture Patterns

Connection Pooling

One of the most impactful but least discussed optimisations. Without connection pooling, your application crashes under moderate load. With it properly configured, you can handle 10× more traffic with the same database server.

Why Connection Pooling Matters

🚗 Analogy: Car Hire vs Car Park

Without pooling: every time you need a car, you rent a new one (takes 2 minutes), drive to your destination, return it. For 1000 trips/hour, you're renting 1000 cars — overwhelming the rental agency.

With pooling: you have a car park with 20 pre-reserved cars. Need a car? Take one from the park (instant). Done? Return it to the park. 1000 trips share 20 cars, rotating them efficiently.

The Cost of Creating a New Database Connection

  • TCP 3-way handshake: ~1ms (local network)
  • TLS handshake (if SSL): ~10ms
  • PostgreSQL authentication + startup: ~5ms
  • PostgreSQL forks a backend process: ~5MB RAM per connection
  • Total: ~15-20ms and ~5MB RAM per connection, every time

At 1000 requests/second, creating a new connection each time = 15–20 seconds of overhead per second of work. Impossible to sustain.

The Pool Sizing Formula

The HikariCP / PostgreSQL Formula

pool_size = (core_count × 2) + effective_spindle_countcore_count = CPU cores on the DATABASE server (not app server)
effective_spindle_count = number of disk spindles (usually 1 for SSD, 2+ for HDD RAID)

Example: 4-core DB server, 1 NVMe SSD → pool_size = (4×2) + 1 = 9
Example: 12-core DB server, 1 SSD → pool_size = (12×2) + 1 = 25
This is SURPRISINGLY small. Start here and measure.
Why Such a Small Pool?At any moment, the DB can only do work equal to its CPU cores. Extra connections don't add capacity — they add context-switching overhead and lock contention. Oracle's Real-World Performance group demonstrated shrinking a pool from 2048 to 96 connections improved throughput from 20,000 txn/min to 64,000 txn/min — 3× better with a SMALLER pool. Counter-intuitive but well-documented.

PgBouncer — The PostgreSQL Connection Multiplexer

PgBouncer sits between your app servers and PostgreSQL, multiplexing many application connections onto a small pool of actual DB connections.

PgBouncer Architecture
graph LR A1["App Pod 1\n10 connections"] --> PG["PgBouncer\npool_size=25"] A2["App Pod 2\n10 connections"] --> PG A3["App Pod 3\n10 connections"] --> PG A4["App Pod 4\n10 connections"] --> PG A5["App Pod 5\n10 connections"] --> PG PG -->|"only 25 actual\nDB connections"| DB["PostgreSQL\n(max_connections=100)"]
pgbouncer.ini [databases] myapp = host=postgres.internal port=5432 dbname=myapp [pgbouncer] pool_mode = transaction # release connection after each transaction (most efficient) max_client_conn = 1000 # up to 1000 app connections default_pool_size = 25 # only 25 actual PostgreSQL connections min_pool_size = 5 max_db_connections = 25

PgBouncer Pool Modes

ModeWhen connection releasedSession featuresBest for
SessionWhen client disconnectsAll session features workLong-lived connections, session variables needed
Transaction (recommended)After each transaction endsSET variables, prepared statements need careMost web apps — best utilisation
StatementAfter each SQL statementNo multi-statement transactionsRead-only, simple queries only

HikariCP — The Java Pool

Java/Spring Boot — application.yml spring: datasource: hikari: pool-name: MyHikariPool maximum-pool-size: 10 # per JVM instance; total = instances × 10 minimum-idle: 2 connection-timeout: 30000 # 30s to get a connection from pool idle-timeout: 600000 # 10 min idle before closing max-lifetime: 1800000 # 30 min max connection lifetime
Common Pool Size Disaster10 Kubernetes pods × 100 connections each = 1000 total connections. PostgreSQL default max_connections = 100. Your app pods can't connect — "too many clients" error. Fix: pool_size per pod = (total_desired_connections) / number_of_pods. With PgBouncer, the pool_size per pod can be large but PgBouncer handles multiplexing to the DB's actual limit.
Distributed Systems

Distributed Systems Fundamentals

Distributed systems are fundamentally different from single-machine systems. The network is not reliable. Clocks are not synchronized. Processes can fail silently. Everything you know about "normal" programming has asterisks when you distribute across machines.

The Fundamental Difference: Partial Failure

🌊 Analogy: Solo Hike vs Team Expedition

On a solo hike, if you twist your ankle, you know exactly what happened. If you're leading a 20-person expedition and someone doesn't arrive at camp, you don't know: are they lost? Injured? Taking a different path? Still coming? Did they turn back? In distributed systems, a missing response from a server is equally ambiguous — it could be crashed, slow, network issue, or perfectly fine but the reply got lost.

Fallacies of Distributed Computing

Peter Deutsch's famous 8 fallacies — wrong assumptions programmers make when designing distributed systems:

FallacyRealityConsequence
The network is reliablePackets are dropped, corrupted, duplicatedAll network calls need timeouts + retries
Latency is zeroInter-datacenter = 1-100ms, inter-region = 50-300msNever do synchronous calls in tight loops
Bandwidth is infiniteYou can exhaust bandwidth with large payloadsPaginate, compress, only fetch what you need
The network is secureTraffic can be intercepted and modifiedAlways use TLS; authenticate service-to-service
Topology doesn't changeServers restart, IP addresses change, nodes come and goUse service discovery, not hardcoded IPs
There is one administratorMulti-team, multi-org dependenciesDesign for independent deployability
Transport cost is zeroSerialization, connection overhead, data transfer costsMinimize service-to-service calls; batch
The network is homogeneousDifferent hardware, OS, library versions everywhereUse versioned APIs, don't assume tech stack

Unreliable Networks

When you send a message to a remote server and get no response, you cannot distinguish:

  • The request was lost in transit (never received)
  • The server is down
  • The server received it, processed it, but the reply was lost
  • The server received it, processed it, and the reply is delayed (will arrive in 10 seconds)
  • The server received it, processed it, failed before sending the reply
The Implication: Idempotency is Non-NegotiableBecause you can't tell if your request succeeded (reply might just be delayed), you might retry. The server might process the same request twice. If the operation is not idempotent, you have a problem: double-charged payment, double-sent email, double-booked ticket. Design every service operation to be safely retryable.
Python — Idempotency via Idempotency Keys # Client sends a unique idempotency key with each request response = requests.post('https://api.payments.com/charge', headers={'Idempotency-Key': 'order-12345-charge-1'}, # unique per intent json={'amount': 9999, 'currency': 'USD'} ) # Server side: check if this key was already processed def charge_payment(idempotency_key, amount): existing = cache.get(f"idem:{idempotency_key}") if existing: return existing # return previous result, don't charge again result = do_charge(amount) cache.set(f"idem:{idempotency_key}", result, ttl=86400) # 24h window return result

Clock Skew — Why You Can't Use Time to Order Events

Why Timestamps Fail for Ordering

Node A's clock shows 10:00:00.100. Node B's clock shows 10:00:00.050 (50ms behind). If A writes first and B writes second:
A's write: timestamp 10:00:00.100
B's write: timestamp 10:00:00.050 + 10ms processing = 10:00:00.060

B's write has an EARLIER timestamp but happened LATER. If we sort by timestamp, A wins — but B's write was more recent!

AWS reports NTP clock drift on their EC2 instances of up to ±250ms. In a 10ms window of operations, timestamps can't tell you anything about order.

Solution: Use logical clocks (Lamport timestamps or vector clocks) to track causality, not wall-clock time. Or use a sequence service (database auto-increment, distributed ID generator like Snowflake) that provides globally ordered IDs.

Byzantine Faults

What is a Byzantine fault?
A Byzantine fault is when a node in a distributed system behaves maliciously or arbitrarily — sending different messages to different nodes, lying about its state, or actively trying to subvert the protocol.

Named after the "Byzantine Generals Problem" (Lamport, 1982): how do armies communicate to coordinate an attack when some generals might be traitors?

Most distributed systems (Raft, Paxos, Kafka, Kubernetes) assume crash fault tolerance (nodes either work correctly or crash) — they do NOT handle Byzantine faults. Blockchain (Bitcoin's Proof of Work, Ethereum) is designed for Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT) where you can't trust anyone.

The Two Generals Problem

What is the Two Generals Problem and why does it matter?
Two armies need to coordinate a simultaneous attack. They can only communicate by messenger through enemy territory (where messages can be intercepted = lost). How can they both be certain the attack will happen?

The mathematical proof: they cannot. There is no protocol that guarantees agreement in a network that can lose messages. This is why distributed systems always have edge cases — they're fundamentally impossible to make perfectly reliable with unreliable networks.

Practical implication: we design around it with timeouts, retries, idempotency, and accepting that some edge cases will require human intervention (or eventual consistency).

Timeout and Retry Strategies

Python — Exponential Backoff with Jitter import time, random def retry_with_backoff(fn, max_retries=5, base_delay=1): for attempt in range(max_retries): try: return fn() except TransientError as e: if attempt == max_retries - 1: raise # give up after max retries delay = base_delay * (2 ** attempt) # 1s, 2s, 4s, 8s, 16s jitter = random.uniform(0, delay * 0.1) # add 0-10% random jitter time.sleep(delay + jitter) # prevent thundering herd on retry # Set appropriate timeouts — don't wait forever! response = requests.get('https://api.service.com/data', timeout=(3.05, 30) # (connect_timeout, read_timeout) )
Distributed Systems

Consensus: Raft & Paxos

Consensus is the problem of getting multiple nodes in a distributed system to agree on a single value, even when some nodes fail. It's the foundation of reliable distributed systems — from leader election to distributed transactions to blockchain.

What is Consensus and Why Is It Hard?

🗳️ Analogy: Club Vote

A committee of 5 members votes on a decision. The rules: members can be absent (crash), but any member present must vote. Votes are sent by mail (can be delayed). Even if 2 members are absent, the remaining 3 should still be able to reach a binding decision. And once a decision is made, everyone who returns must accept it — no reversals.

Formal requirements for consensus:

  • Agreement: All non-faulty nodes decide on the same value
  • Integrity: Every node decides at most once (no flip-flopping)
  • Validity: The decided value was actually proposed by some node
  • Termination: Every non-faulty node eventually decides

Raft — Designed for Understandability

Raft was designed in 2013 by Diego Ongaro and John Ousterhout specifically to be easier to understand than Paxos. It's the consensus algorithm behind etcd (Kubernetes), CockroachDB, TiKV, and many others.

Raft Term + Leader Election

Raft Leader Election
sequenceDiagram participant A as Node A (Candidate) participant B as Node B participant C as Node C Note over A,C: All nodes start as Followers. Election timeout fires first on A. A->>A: Become Candidate, increment term to 2 A->>B: RequestVote (term=2, I want to be leader) A->>C: RequestVote (term=2) B-->>A: VoteGranted (haven't voted in term 2) C-->>A: VoteGranted Note over A: Majority (2/2) → A becomes Leader for term 2 A->>B: AppendEntries (heartbeat, term=2) A->>C: AppendEntries (heartbeat, term=2) Note over B,C: Reset election timers — leader is alive

Raft Log Replication — How Writes Work

Raft Log Replication
sequenceDiagram participant C as Client participant L as Leader (A) participant F1 as Follower B participant F2 as Follower C C->>L: Write: SET x=5 L->>L: Append to local log (uncommitted) L->>F1: AppendEntries {term:2, entry: SET x=5} L->>F2: AppendEntries {term:2, entry: SET x=5} F1-->>L: Success (appended) F2-->>L: Success (appended) Note over L: Majority (2/2 followers + self = 3/3) confirmed → COMMIT L->>L: Apply to state machine L->>C: Success (x=5 committed) L->>F1: Committed notification L->>F2: Committed notification

Raft Key Properties

What makes Raft work:
• Election timeout randomization: Each node picks a random timeout (150-300ms). The first node to time out becomes a candidate. This prevents split votes most of the time.
• Term numbers as fencing tokens: Each election increments the term number. Any message from a lower term is rejected. This prevents old leaders from confusing the cluster.
• Log Matching Property: If two logs have an entry with the same index and term, all entries before that point are identical. This guarantees consistency.
• Majority quorum for everything: A cluster of 2f+1 nodes can tolerate f failures. 3 nodes → 1 failure. 5 nodes → 2 failures. 7 nodes → 3 failures.

Paxos — The Original Consensus Algorithm

Paxos was invented by Leslie Lamport in 1989 (published 1998). It's notoriously difficult to understand and implement correctly. Raft is often described as "Paxos but understandable".

Paxos in production: Apache ZooKeeper uses ZAB (Paxos-like), Google Spanner uses Multi-Paxos, Apache Cassandra uses Paxos for lightweight transactions.

Raft vs Paxos — Which to Know for System DesignFor system design interviews, know Raft. It's the modern standard, easier to explain, and is what etcd/Kubernetes/CockroachDB use. Mention Paxos as the predecessor. The conceptual knowledge (leader election, quorums, log replication) transfers between both. You'll rarely need to implement either — use etcd, Consul, or ZooKeeper.
Distributed Systems

Two-Phase Commit & Distributed Transactions

When a business operation spans multiple databases or services, you face the distributed transaction problem: how to ensure all-or-nothing atomicity across systems you can't control together. 2PC is the classical solution — but it has a critical flaw.

The Problem: Cross-Service Atomicity

The Distributed Transaction Challenge

You're booking a holiday: reserve a hotel room (Hotel DB) + book a flight (Airline DB) + charge credit card (Payment DB). All three must succeed or all three must not happen. But you can't do a database ACID transaction across three separate databases on three separate servers.

What happens if the flight books and the hotel reserves, but the payment fails? You need to undo the flight booking and hotel reservation. How do you coordinate this?

Two-Phase Commit (2PC)

2PC Happy Path — All participants vote YES
sequenceDiagram participant C as Transaction Coordinator (TC) participant H as Hotel DB participant A as Airline DB participant P as Payment DB Note over C: Phase 1 — Prepare/Vote C->>H: PREPARE (can you commit hotel reservation?) C->>A: PREPARE (can you commit flight booking?) C->>P: PREPARE (can you commit payment charge?) H-->>C: YES (reservation locked, log written) A-->>C: YES (booking locked, log written) P-->>C: YES (charge ready, log written) Note over C: All YES → decide COMMIT. Write decision to coordinator log. Note over C: Phase 2 — Commit C->>H: COMMIT C->>A: COMMIT C->>P: COMMIT H-->>C: Done (reservation confirmed) A-->>C: Done (ticket issued) P-->>C: Done (charge processed)

The Fatal Flaw: Coordinator Failure After Vote

The Blocking Problem — Why 2PC Is Considered BrokenScenario: All three participants voted YES (Phase 1 complete). Coordinator crashes before sending COMMIT/ABORT (Phase 2).

Participants are now "in-doubt" — they voted YES (which means they CANNOT unilaterally abort), but they don't know if the coordinator decided COMMIT or ABORT. They must hold locks and wait for the coordinator to recover.

If the coordinator is down for 30 minutes: all three databases hold locks for 30 minutes. No other transactions can use those resources. Your entire system can grind to a halt waiting for a crashed coordinator.

This is 2PC's fatal flaw: it's a blocking protocol. A coordinator failure can block ALL participants indefinitely.

2PC vs Saga: When to Use What

2PCSaga
ConsistencyStrong ACID atomicityEventual consistency (no ACID)
Failure handlingCan block indefinitely on coordinator failureNever blocks — uses compensation
LockingHolds locks across multiple systemsNo distributed locks
ComplexitySimple protocol, hard failure modesComplex to design (must plan all compensations)
Use whenShort-lived transactions, same-org databases, latency acceptableLong-running workflows, cross-service, resilience required
ExamplesDatabase-internal distributed txns (CockroachDB, Spanner)Microservice workflows (order processing, booking systems)
Modern 2PC — What Actually WorksGoogle Spanner uses Multi-Paxos-backed 2PC with TrueTime (GPS + atomic clock synchronization) to make coordinator failure survivable. CockroachDB uses Raft-replicated coordinators. When the coordinator is itself a replicated state machine (not a single point of failure), 2PC's blocking problem is solved.

For microservices in AWS/GCP/Azure: use the Saga pattern (see Saga page). For databases from a single vendor that support distributed transactions (CockroachDB, YugabyteDB, Spanner): 2PC with replicated coordinator is fine.
Distributed Systems

Clocks & Ordering in Distributed Systems

How do you know which event happened first in a distributed system when clocks drift and networks have variable latency? Logical clocks are the answer — they track causality, not wall-clock time.

The Problem with Wall-Clock Time

Clock Drift Example

Node A: clock says 12:00:00.100 → writes user.name = "Alice"
Node B: clock says 12:00:00.080 (20ms behind) → writes user.name = "Bob" (this happened LATER in real time)

If we use Last-Write-Wins with timestamps: Alice wins (timestamp 12:00:00.100 > 12:00:00.080), even though Bob's write was more recent. We've lost Bob's update. Data corruption.

NTP sync only brings clocks to within a few milliseconds of each other — enough to corrupt ordering of fast operations.

Lamport Timestamps — Logical Ordering

Invented by Leslie Lamport (same person who invented Paxos). A Lamport timestamp is a simple counter that tracks causal ordering without relying on wall-clock time.

Rules for Lamport Timestamps # Each node maintains a counter (timestamp) # Rule 1: Every event on a node increments the counter by 1 # Rule 2: When sending a message, include your current counter # Rule 3: When receiving a message: counter = max(local, received) + 1 # Example: # Node A: events at ts=1, 2, 3. Sends message with ts=3 # Node B: was at ts=1. Receives A's message. New ts = max(1, 3) + 1 = 4 # Node B: next events at ts=5, 6, 7 # Lamport guarantee: if event A CAUSED event B, then ts(A) < ts(B) # LIMITATION: ts(A) < ts(B) does NOT mean A caused B (concurrent events can also satisfy this)

Vector Clocks — Detecting Concurrency

Vector clocks track not just ordering, but also CONCURRENCY. They can tell you: "Event A happened before B" OR "A and B happened concurrently (no causal relationship)".

Vector Clock Rules # N nodes → each node has a vector of N counters [A, B, C, ...] # Rule 1: On an event, increment your own counter: [1,0,0] → [2,0,0] # Rule 2: On send, include your vector clock # Rule 3: On receive: element-wise max + increment own counter # Receive [3,0,0]: my vector [1,2,0] → max=[3,2,0] → increment → [4,2,0] # Comparison: # VC1 < VC2 (A happened-before B): if EVERY element of VC1 ≤ VC2 and at least one < # VC1 || VC2 (concurrent): neither ≤ the other → possible conflict! # Example: Two users editing a shopping cart simultaneously # VC = [Alice's_count, Bob's_count] # Alice adds item: VC [1,0] # Bob adds item: VC [0,1] ← concurrent with Alice! # Neither [1,0] ≤ [0,1] nor [0,1] ≤ [1,0] → conflict detected! # System can show user both versions and ask to merge

Lamport vs Vector Clocks — When to Use

Lamport TimestampsVector Clocks
SizeSingle integer O(1)Vector of N integers O(N)
Can detect concurrency?No — only orderingYes — can tell if events are concurrent
Can detect causality?One direction only: if A→B, then ts(A)<ts(B)Both directions: iff A→B, then VC(A)<VC(B)
Use whenSimple total ordering needed (Kafka offsets, Snowflake IDs)Conflict detection needed (shopping carts, document editing, DynamoDB versioning)
Used byKafka (offsets), Cassandra (LWW timestamp)Amazon DynamoDB (versioning), Riak (conflict detection)

Google Spanner's TrueTime — Hardware-Assisted Clock Sync

TrueTime APIGoogle Spanner uses GPS receivers and atomic clocks in each datacenter, synchronized via NTP with uncertainty bounds. Instead of a single timestamp, TrueTime returns an interval: [earliest, latest]. When you commit a transaction, you wait until your TrueTime interval has passed for all other commits — guaranteeing external consistency even with physical clocks.

This enables truly global ACID transactions across multiple datacenters. Cloud Spanner offers 99.999% SLA and handles TrueTime automatically. For most applications, logical clocks (Lamport/vector) or a single ordered sequence service is sufficient.
Distributed Systems

Probabilistic Data Structures

These are clever data structures that trade perfect accuracy for dramatic space savings and speed. Knowing when and how to use them is a mark of an experienced system designer.

Why "Probabilistic"?

🎭 Analogy: Quick Survey vs Full Census
