Cursor pagination: get posts with timestamp < before_timestamp max_score = before_timestamp if before_timestamp else '+inf' post_ids = r.zrevrangebyscore(feed_key, max_score, '-inf', start=0, num=limit) if not post_ids: # Feed cache empty (new user, cache expired) — build from DB return rebuild_feed_from_db(user_id) # For celebrities they follow: merge in their recent posts on read celebrities = db.query("SELECT following_id FROM follows WHERE follower_id=%s AND is_celebrity=true", user_id) celebrity_posts = get_celebrity_posts(celebrities, before_timestamp) # Merge and re-sort by time all_post_ids = sorted(set(post_ids) | set(celebrity_posts), reverse=True)[:limit] # Fetch actual post data (from Redis cache or DB) return [get_post_data(pid) for pid in all_post_ids]

    Problem 2: Feed Ranking — Not Just Chronological

    Why Chronological Isn't Enough

    You follow 500 accounts. 200 tweeted in the last hour. Purely chronological = the most recent 20 posts, regardless of quality. You might miss a post from your best friend because 10 accounts you barely care about posted in the last 5 minutes.

    Python — Simplified Feed Ranking Score import math def calculate_feed_score(post: dict, viewer: dict) -> float: """ Factors that increase score: - Recency (exponential decay — old posts become irrelevant fast) - Relationship strength (close friend vs. account you never interact with) - Engagement (posts with many likes/comments are probably good) - Media type (videos typically get 3x engagement of text) """ now = time.time() age_hours = (now - post['created_at']) / 3600 # 1. Recency score (Hacker News-style decay) # Score halves every 6 hours recency = 1.0 / ((1 + age_hours) ** 1.5) # 2. Relationship strength (from interaction history) # Have you liked/commented/DMd this account in the last 30 days? relationship = db.query_one( "SELECT score FROM relationship_scores WHERE follower=%s AND following=%s", viewer['id'], post['author_id'] ) or 0.1 # default 0.1 if no history # 3. Engagement score engagement = math.log(1 + post['likes'] + post['comments'] * 2) # 4. Content type multiplier type_mult = {'video': 1.5, 'image': 1.2, 'text': 1.0}.get(post['type'], 1.0) return recency * relationship * engagement * type_mult # Sort feed by score before returning to user # In production: ML model replaces this formula (Instagram uses a multi-layer neural net)

    Problem 3: Infinite Scroll Pagination — Cursor vs Offset

    ❌ OFFSET Pagination (Wrong for feeds)

    SQL -- Page 2 of feed SELECT * FROM posts WHERE author_id IN (following_ids) ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20 OFFSET 20; -- Problem 1: OFFSET=1000 scans 1020 rows to return 20 -- Problem 2: New post appears → everything shifts by 1 -- User scrolls back = sees same post twice or skips one

    ✅ Cursor Pagination (Correct)

    SQL -- "Give me posts older than the last one I saw" SELECT * FROM posts WHERE author_id IN (following_ids) AND created_at < '2024-01-15T10:30:00' -- cursor = timestamp of last item shown ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20; -- Always fast: uses index on created_at -- New posts don't affect pagination position -- Stable scrolling experience
    Mastery — Build It From Scratch

    Kafka in Practice — Complete Producer to Consumer

    Kafka is mentioned in almost every large-scale system design. But knowing its concepts isn't enough — you need to know how to actually use it, what goes wrong, and how to configure it for production.

    Why Kafka? When NOT to Use Kafka?

    Use Kafka when ALL of these are true:
    ✅ You need to fan-out events to multiple consumers independently
    ✅ You need to replay events (reprocess last 7 days of orders with a new algorithm)
    ✅ High throughput: millions of events per second
    ✅ Events need to be ordered within a partition (all events for user_id=123 in order)

    Don't use Kafka when:
    ❌ You just need a simple job queue → use SQS or Celery with Redis
    ❌ Events are small & infrequent (1000/day) → SQS is simpler
    ❌ You need request-reply pattern → use gRPC or REST
    ❌ Team has no Kafka experience → operational complexity is real

    Kafka Core Concepts — Properly Explained

  • Topic — The Logical Channel

    A topic is like a named mailbox. Producers write to topics. Consumers read from topics. Topic: "order-events". You publish: "order placed", "order shipped", "order delivered" all to the same topic. Different consumer services (billing, shipping, analytics) each subscribe and process independently.

  • Partition — The Parallelism Unit

    A topic is split into N partitions. Each partition is an ordered, immutable log. More partitions = more parallelism (more consumer threads can process simultaneously). Rule: choose partition count based on desired throughput. 1 partition = 1 consumer at a time. 100 partitions = 100 consumers in parallel.

  • Partition Key — Ordering Guarantee

    When you publish a message, you specify a key. All messages with the same key go to the same partition. Same partition = same order. Example: key=user_id ensures all events for user 123 are in order — you'll never see "order delivered" before "order placed" for the same user.

  • Consumer Group — Independent Readers

    Multiple services can each have their own consumer group. Each group gets ALL messages independently. Analytics group sees every event. Billing group sees every event. They progress at their own pace — Kafka tracks their offset (position) independently. If billing crashes and restarts: it resumes from where it left off, independent of analytics.

    • Kafka: One topic, two consumer groups
    graph LR P["Producer\n(Order Service)"] --> T["Topic: order-events\n3 partitions"] T -->|"reads all events"| CG1["Consumer Group: billing\n3 consumers\n(one per partition)"] T -->|"reads all events"| CG2["Consumer Group: analytics\n3 consumers\n(one per partition)"] T -->|"reads all events"| CG3["Consumer Group: shipping\n3 consumers\n(one per partition)"] style T fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757

    Complete Producer and Consumer Code

    Python — Kafka Producer (confluent-kafka) from confluent_kafka import Producer import json, uuid producer = Producer({ 'bootstrap.servers': 'kafka-1:9092,kafka-2:9092,kafka-3:9092', 'acks': 'all', # Wait for ALL replicas to ack (strongest durability) 'retries': 5, # Retry on transient failure 'retry.backoff.ms': 500, 'enable.idempotence': True, # Exactly-once delivery (no duplicates from retries) 'linger.ms': 5, # Batch messages up to 5ms for throughput 'compression.type': 'snappy' # Compress batches (~50% smaller) }) def publish_order_event(order_id: str, event_type: str, order_data: dict): message = { 'event_id': str(uuid.uuid4()), # Unique ID for deduplication 'event_type': event_type, 'order_id': order_id, 'timestamp': datetime.utcnow().isoformat(), 'data': order_data } producer.produce( topic='order-events', key=order_id, # All events for same order → same partition → ordered value=json.dumps(message).encode(), on_delivery=lambda err, msg: print(f"Delivered: {msg.offset()}" if not err else f"Failed: {err}") ) producer.flush() # Ensure delivery before returning # Usage: publish_order_event("order-123", "ORDER_PLACED", {"total": 99.99, "items": [...]})
    Python — Kafka Consumer (reliable, production pattern) from confluent_kafka import Consumer, KafkaError consumer = Consumer({ 'bootstrap.servers': 'kafka-1:9092,kafka-2:9092,kafka-3:9092', 'group.id': 'billing-service', # Unique group per service 'auto.offset.reset': 'earliest', # If no stored offset: start from beginning 'enable.auto.commit': False, # CRITICAL: manual commit for at-least-once 'max.poll.interval.ms': 300000, # 5 min max for processing one batch }) consumer.subscribe(['order-events']) def run_billing_consumer(): while True: msg = consumer.poll(timeout=1.0) if msg is None: continue if msg.error(): if msg.error().code() == KafkaError._PARTITION_EOF: continue # Reached end of partition — not an error raise Exception(f"Kafka error: {msg.error()}") try: event = json.loads(msg.value().decode('utf-8')) # CRITICAL: Idempotency check before processing # Kafka delivers at-least-once — same message might arrive twice on restart if already_processed(event['event_id']): consumer.commit(message=msg) continue if event['event_type'] == 'ORDER_PLACED': process_billing(event['data']) mark_as_processed(event['event_id']) # Only commit AFTER successful processing # If we crash before commit: message redelivered → idempotency handles it consumer.commit(message=msg) except Exception as e: # Don't commit — message will be redelivered # Send to DLQ after N failures handle_processing_error(msg, e)

    Common Kafka Mistakes (and How to Fix Them)

    MistakeSymptomFix
    Auto-commit enabled (default)Messages lost on crash — committed before processing doneSet enable.auto.commit=false, commit manually after processing
    Not handling duplicatesSame order billed twice on consumer restartStore processed event IDs, check before processing
    Too few partitionsThroughput limited — can't add more consumers than partitionsCreate topics with 3x expected peak consumer count
    No key set on produceEvents for same entity arrive out of orderAlways set key=entity_id when order matters
    Slow consumer not caughtConsumer lag grows, events processed hours lateMonitor consumer_group_lag metric, alert on lag > threshold
    acks=1 (default)Messages lost when leader broker crashesSet acks=all + replication.factor=3 for durability
    Mastery — Build It From Scratch

    Database Schema Design — From Requirements to Production Schema

    Schema design is often the most important technical decision you'll make — it's extremely hard to change later. This teaches you how to go from business requirements to a well-designed, scalable PostgreSQL schema with every decision justified.

    Case Study: Design the Database for a Twitter-Like App

    Requirements: Users can post tweets. Users follow other users. Users can like and reply to tweets. Users can be mentioned. Hashtags. Direct messages. Analytics (view counts, engagement).

    Step 1: Identify the Entities

    List every "thing" the system manages. Each thing is usually a table.

    • User (the actor)
    • Tweet (the content)
    • Follow (user-to-user relationship)
    • Like (user-to-tweet relationship)
    • Reply (tweet-to-tweet relationship)
    • Hashtag (tag entity)
    • TweetHashtag (tweet-to-hashtag many-to-many)
    • Mention (user mentioned in tweet)
    • DirectMessage (private message)

    Step 2: Design Each Table with Justifications

    SQL — Complete Twitter-like Schema with Reasoning -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── -- USERS TABLE -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── CREATE TABLE users ( id BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY, -- BIGINT not INT: 2.1B limit for INT. Twitter has 400M users — play it safe username VARCHAR(50) UNIQUE NOT NULL, -- VARCHAR(50) not TEXT: enforce max length at DB layer, not just app layer email VARCHAR(320) UNIQUE NOT NULL, -- 320 = RFC 5321 max email length password_hash CHAR(60) NOT NULL, -- CHAR not VARCHAR: bcrypt hashes are always exactly 60 chars — CHAR is optimal display_name VARCHAR(100), bio TEXT, profile_img_url TEXT, -- Store URL to S3, not the image itself — images in DB = catastrophic follower_count BIGINT DEFAULT 0, following_count BIGINT DEFAULT 0, tweet_count BIGINT DEFAULT 0, -- Denormalized counters: avoid COUNT(*) on follows table at read time -- Update these with triggers or application code on follow/unfollow/tweet is_verified BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE, is_private BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW() NOT NULL, -- TIMESTAMPTZ not TIMESTAMP: always store with timezone (UTC) last_active_at TIMESTAMPTZ ); CREATE INDEX idx_users_username ON users(username); -- Profile lookup by username CREATE INDEX idx_users_email ON users(email); -- Auth lookup CREATE INDEX idx_users_created ON users(created_at DESC); -- "Newest users" query -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── -- TWEETS TABLE -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── CREATE TABLE tweets ( id BIGINT GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY, -- Consider UUID or Snowflake if you need distributed generation without DB author_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE, -- ON DELETE CASCADE: user deleted → their tweets deleted automatically content VARCHAR(280) NOT NULL, -- Twitter's 280-char limit enforced at DB layer too reply_to_id BIGINT REFERENCES tweets(id) ON DELETE SET NULL, -- Self-referential FK: NULL = original tweet, non-null = reply -- ON DELETE SET NULL: if parent tweet deleted, reply becomes orphan (not deleted) retweet_of_id BIGINT REFERENCES tweets(id) ON DELETE SET NULL, media_urls TEXT[], -- PostgreSQL array: store multiple S3 URLs without a separate media table -- Trade-off: arrays are good for simple lists, bad for querying individual elements like_count BIGINT DEFAULT 0, reply_count BIGINT DEFAULT 0, retweet_count BIGINT DEFAULT 0, view_count BIGINT DEFAULT 0, -- Denormalized: expensive to COUNT(likes) on every tweet read -- Update via triggers on likes/replies tables, or application code language CHAR(2), -- ISO 639-1 language code: always 2 chars → CHAR(2) optimal is_deleted BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE, -- Soft delete: never actually DELETE rows — users may want to restore -- Hard delete via background job later created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW() NOT NULL ); CREATE INDEX idx_tweets_author_created ON tweets(author_id, created_at DESC); -- Composite index for "get user's tweets sorted by time" — the most common query CREATE INDEX idx_tweets_reply_to ON tweets(reply_to_id) WHERE reply_to_id IS NOT NULL; -- Partial index: only index rows that are replies (skip NULL rows = smaller index) CREATE INDEX idx_tweets_created ON tweets(created_at DESC) WHERE is_deleted = FALSE; -- Partial index on non-deleted tweets only — global feed query -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── -- FOLLOWS TABLE (User-to-User relationship) -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── CREATE TABLE follows ( follower_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE, following_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), PRIMARY KEY (follower_id, following_id), -- Composite PK: automatically prevents duplicate follows AND creates an index CHECK (follower_id != following_id) -- Constraint: you can't follow yourself ); CREATE INDEX idx_follows_following ON follows(following_id); -- The PK covers (follower_id, following_id) — queries for "who does A follow?" are fast -- This index covers "who follows A?" — needed for follower lists and fan-out -- TRIGGER: Update follower/following counts on follow/unfollow CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION update_follow_counts() RETURNS TRIGGER AS $$ BEGIN IF TG_OP = 'INSERT' THEN UPDATE users SET following_count = following_count + 1 WHERE id = NEW.follower_id; UPDATE users SET follower_count = follower_count + 1 WHERE id = NEW.following_id; ELSIF TG_OP = 'DELETE' THEN UPDATE users SET following_count = following_count - 1 WHERE id = OLD.follower_id; UPDATE users SET follower_count = follower_count - 1 WHERE id = OLD.following_id; END IF; RETURN NULL; END; $$ LANGUAGE plpgsql; CREATE TRIGGER follows_count_trigger AFTER INSERT OR DELETE ON follows FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION update_follow_counts(); -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── -- LIKES TABLE -- ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────── CREATE TABLE likes ( user_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE, tweet_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES tweets(id) ON DELETE CASCADE, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), PRIMARY KEY (user_id, tweet_id) -- Composite PK prevents duplicate likes AND indexes (user_id, tweet_id) together -- Query: "Did user X like tweet Y?" = O(log n) using PK index ); CREATE INDEX idx_likes_tweet ON likes(tweet_id, created_at DESC); -- "Who liked tweet Y and when?" — for likes list

    Step 3: Common Schema Mistakes to Avoid

    MistakeWhy It's BadFix
    Storing images/files in DBDB bloats, backups huge, no CDN, extremely slowStore in S3/object storage, keep URL in DB
    Using INT for IDs on big tablesINT max = 2.1B rows. Twitter has more tweets than thatUse BIGINT always for entity IDs
    TIMESTAMP without timezoneDST changes, server relocations corrupt time dataAlways use TIMESTAMPTZ (stores UTC)
    Counting with COUNT(*) at read timeCOUNT on 100M row likes table = seconds of query timeDenormalized counters updated via triggers
    Soft-delete with no index filterQueries scan deleted rows tooPartial index WHERE is_deleted=FALSE
    No created_at on every tableImpossible to audit, debug, or do time-range queriesAdd created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW() everywhere
    Not using ON DELETE CASCADE/SET NULLOrphaned records accumulate, referential integrity brokenAlways specify ON DELETE behavior for every FK
    Mastery — Build It From Scratch

    Microservices — When, How, and What Goes Wrong

    Microservices aren't an architecture style — they're a solution to specific problems at specific scale. Building them incorrectly creates complexity far worse than the problem they solve. This teaches you when microservices are right, how to structure them, and how to avoid the common disasters.

    The Honest Truth About Microservices

    Microservices are NOT for early-stage companies.
    Martin Fowler (who popularised the term) said: "Don't start with microservices. Start with a monolith, identify seams, extract carefully."

    Signs you're NOT ready for microservices:
    • Fewer than 50 engineers
    • Product requirements still changing frequently
    • No automated CI/CD pipeline
    • No distributed tracing or centralized logging
    • Team hasn't operated a distributed system before

    Premature microservices is the #1 cause of self-inflicted engineering disasters.

    The Right Migration Path: Monolith → Modular Monolith → Microservices

    Stage 1: 0–50 engineers
    Monolith (Required)
    Single deployable unit. All code in one repo. Fast development. No network calls between components. Deploy 10 times/day. This is where Instagram, Shopify, GitHub, and every successful startup began. Don't apologize for your monolith.
    Stage 2: 50–200 engineers
    Modular Monolith
    Still one deployable unit, but code is organized into clear modules (UserModule, PaymentModule, OrderModule) with enforced boundaries. No cross-module direct DB queries. Each module has its own service class. This is microservices-ready architecture without the operational overhead.
    Stage 3: 200+ engineers, clear bottlenecks
    Extract Services (Strangler Fig)
    Extract specific modules into services when they have clear justification: independent scaling needs, different release cadence, different tech stack, or team ownership boundaries. Never extract just because it "feels right architecturally."

    How to Structure a Microservice (Complete Example)

    Directory structure — Order Service order-service/ ├── api/ │ ├── routes.py # HTTP endpoints (FastAPI/Flask routes) │ └── schemas.py # Request/response Pydantic models ├── domain/ │ ├── order.py # Order entity, business rules (pure Python, no framework) │ ├── order_service.py # Business logic (create_order, cancel_order) │ └── events.py # Domain events (OrderCreated, OrderCancelled) ├── infra/ │ ├── db.py # SQLAlchemy models, queries │ ├── kafka.py # Kafka producer/consumer │ └── cache.py # Redis client ├── tests/ │ ├── unit/ # Test domain logic without any infra │ └── integration/ # Test with real DB/Kafka (testcontainers) ├── Dockerfile ├── docker-compose.yml # For local dev (postgres, redis, kafka) └── requirements.txt
    Python — Clean Service Architecture # domain/order.py — Pure business logic, no framework dependencies from dataclasses import dataclass from enum import Enum from datetime import datetime class OrderStatus(Enum): PENDING = "pending" CONFIRMED = "confirmed" CANCELLED = "cancelled" @dataclass class Order: id: str user_id: str items: list total: float status: OrderStatus = OrderStatus.PENDING def confirm(self): if self.status != OrderStatus.PENDING: raise ValueError(f"Can only confirm pending orders, got {self.status}") self.status = OrderStatus.CONFIRMED return [OrderConfirmedEvent(order_id=self.id)] # return domain events def cancel(self, reason: str): if self.status == OrderStatus.CANCELLED: raise ValueError("Already cancelled") self.status = OrderStatus.CANCELLED return [OrderCancelledEvent(order_id=self.id, reason=reason)] # domain/order_service.py — Orchestrates domain + infrastructure class OrderService: def __init__(self, order_repo, event_publisher): self.repo = order_repo self.publisher = event_publisher def create_order(self, user_id: str, items: list) -> Order: if not items: raise ValueError("Order must have at least one item") total = sum(item['price'] * item['qty'] for item in items) order = Order(id=generate_id(), user_id=user_id, items=items, total=total) self.repo.save(order) self.publisher.publish(OrderCreatedEvent(order_id=order.id, total=total)) return order

    Service-to-Service Communication — Synchronous vs Async

    Synchronous (HTTP/gRPC)

    Service A calls Service B and waits for response. Simple to understand. Use when: you need the response to complete your operation (payment confirmation before showing success screen).

    Problems: If B is down, A's request fails. Cascading failures. Tight temporal coupling.

    Must have: Timeouts, circuit breakers, retries with backoff.

    Asynchronous (Kafka/SQS)

    Service A publishes event, B consumes when ready. Decoupled. Use when: B's response isn't needed immediately (send email after order placed).

    Problems: Eventual consistency, harder to debug, idempotency required.

    Must have: Outbox pattern, dead letter queues, monitoring lag.

    Decision rule: Default to async. Use sync only when you genuinely need the response immediately (and can't redesign to avoid it). Every sync call is a dependency that can bring down your service.

    Circuit Breaker — The Resilience Pattern

    Python — Circuit Breaker from Scratch import time from enum import Enum class CircuitState(Enum): CLOSED = "closed" # Normal operation OPEN = "open" # Failing — reject immediately HALF_OPEN = "half_open" # Testing recovery class CircuitBreaker: def __init__(self, failure_threshold=5, timeout=60, success_threshold=2): self.failure_threshold = failure_threshold # Open after 5 failures self.timeout = timeout # Try again after 60s self.success_threshold = success_threshold # Close after 2 successes self.failure_count = 0 self.success_count = 0 self.state = CircuitState.CLOSED self.last_failure_time = None def call(self, fn, *args, **kwargs): if self.state == CircuitState.OPEN: if time.time() - self.last_failure_time > self.timeout: self.state = CircuitState.HALF_OPEN # Try recovery else: raise CircuitOpenError("Service unavailable (circuit open)") try: result = fn(*args, **kwargs) self.on_success() return result except Exception as e: self.on_failure() raise def on_success(self): self.failure_count = 0 if self.state == CircuitState.HALF_OPEN: self.success_count += 1 if self.success_count >= self.success_threshold: self.state = CircuitState.CLOSED # Fully recovered def on_failure(self): self.failure_count += 1 self.success_count = 0 self.last_failure_time = time.time() if self.failure_count >= self.failure_threshold: self.state = CircuitState.OPEN # Usage: payment_circuit = CircuitBreaker(failure_threshold=3, timeout=30) try: result = payment_circuit.call(payment_service.charge, user_id, amount) except CircuitOpenError: return {"error": "Payment service temporarily unavailable"}, 503
    Mastery — Build It From Scratch

    Observability — Logs, Metrics, Traces in Practice

    You can't fix what you can't see. Observability is the practice of making your system's internal state understandable from the outside. The three pillars are logs, metrics, and traces. This teaches you to implement all three.

    The Three Pillars of Observability

    🏥 Analogy: Medical Monitoring

    Metrics: Like a patient's vital signs dashboard (heart rate, blood pressure, temperature). Numbers over time. Tell you WHEN something is wrong.
    Logs: Like a doctor's notes — what happened, when, in detail. Tell you WHAT happened.
    Traces: Like following a blood cell through the body. Track a specific request end-to-end across multiple services. Tell you WHERE the slowdown is.

    Pillar 1: Structured Logging

    Plain text logs are useless at scale. If you log "Error processing order", how do you find all errors for order_id=123? You can't grep for it across 100 servers. Use structured JSON logging so you can filter, aggregate, and search.
    Python — Structured Logging (production setup) import logging import json from datetime import datetime, timezone import uuid class StructuredLogger: def __init__(self, service_name: str): self.service = service_name def log(self, level: str, message: str, **context): entry = { "timestamp": datetime.now(timezone.utc).isoformat(), "level": level, "service": self.service, "message": message, "trace_id": context.pop("trace_id", None), # for distributed tracing "span_id": context.pop("span_id", None), **context } print(json.dumps(entry)) # stdout → log aggregator picks it up logger = StructuredLogger("order-service") # Usage — include context that helps you debug later def process_order(order_id: str, user_id: str, trace_id: str): logger.log("INFO", "Processing order", order_id=order_id, user_id=user_id, trace_id=trace_id) try: result = payment_service.charge(order_id) logger.log("INFO", "Order processed successfully", order_id=order_id, duration_ms=result.duration, trace_id=trace_id) except PaymentError as e: logger.log("ERROR", "Payment failed", order_id=order_id, error=str(e), error_code=e.code, trace_id=trace_id) raise # Output: {"timestamp": "2024-01-15T10:30:00Z", "level": "ERROR", # "service": "order-service", "message": "Payment failed", # "order_id": "ord-123", "error": "Insufficient funds", # "error_code": "CARD_DECLINED", "trace_id": "abc-def-123"} # Now searchable: "show all ERROR logs where order_id=ord-123 in last 1hr"

    Pillar 2: Metrics with Prometheus

    Python — Prometheus Metrics (complete setup) from prometheus_client import Counter, Histogram, Gauge, start_http_server import time # Counter: monotonically increasing — total requests, total errors REQUEST_COUNT = Counter( 'http_requests_total', 'Total HTTP requests', ['method', 'endpoint', 'status_code'] # labels for filtering ) # Histogram: distribution of values — response times, payload sizes REQUEST_LATENCY = Histogram( 'http_request_duration_seconds', 'HTTP request latency', ['method', 'endpoint'], buckets=[0.001, 0.01, 0.05, 0.1, 0.25, 0.5, 1.0, 2.5, 5.0] # Buckets let you query: "% of requests under 100ms" — the p99 question ) # Gauge: current value that can go up/down — active connections, queue depth ACTIVE_ORDERS = Gauge('active_orders_total', 'Orders currently being processed') # Flask middleware to auto-measure all requests def metrics_middleware(app): @app.before_request def before(): g.start_time = time.time() @app.after_request def after(response): duration = time.time() - g.start_time REQUEST_COUNT.labels( method=request.method, endpoint=request.endpoint, status_code=response.status_code ).inc() REQUEST_LATENCY.labels( method=request.method, endpoint=request.endpoint ).observe(duration) return response start_http_server(8001) # Prometheus scrapes /metrics on this port
    Prometheus Query Language (PromQL) — Key Queries # Error rate over last 5 minutes rate(http_requests_total{status_code=~"5.."}[5m]) / rate(http_requests_total[5m]) # 99th percentile response time histogram_quantile(0.99, rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])) # Requests per second rate(http_requests_total[1m]) # Alert: if error rate > 1% for 5 minutes ALERT HighErrorRate IF rate(http_requests_total{status_code=~"5.."}[5m]) / rate(http_requests_total[5m]) > 0.01 FOR 5m LABELS { severity = "critical" } ANNOTATIONS { description = "Error rate above 1% for 5 minutes" }

    Pillar 3: Distributed Tracing with OpenTelemetry

    Why Tracing? The Microservices Debugging Problem

    A user reports "checkout was slow (8 seconds)." You have logs from 6 services (API Gateway, Order Service, Payment Service, Inventory Service, Email Service, User Service). How do you find which service was slow?

    Without tracing: search 6 log streams for the user's request ID, manually correlate timestamps. Takes 2 hours.
    With tracing: one waterfall diagram shows: API Gateway 10ms → Order Service 20ms → Inventory Service 7.9 seconds (the culprit!) → Payment Service 30ms. Fixed in 5 minutes.

    Python — OpenTelemetry Tracing (auto-instrument) from opentelemetry import trace from opentelemetry.sdk.trace import TracerProvider from opentelemetry.exporter.otlp.proto.grpc.trace_exporter import OTLPSpanExporter from opentelemetry.sdk.trace.export import BatchSpanProcessor from opentelemetry.instrumentation.flask import FlaskInstrumentor from opentelemetry.instrumentation.sqlalchemy import SQLAlchemyInstrumentor from opentelemetry.instrumentation.redis import RedisInstrumentor # Setup — do this once at startup provider = TracerProvider() exporter = OTLPSpanExporter(endpoint="http://jaeger:4317") provider.add_span_processor(BatchSpanProcessor(exporter)) trace.set_tracer_provider(provider) # Auto-instrument: all Flask requests, SQLAlchemy queries, Redis calls # automatically get trace spans — no code changes needed! FlaskInstrumentor().instrument_app(app) SQLAlchemyInstrumentor().instrument(engine=db_engine) RedisInstrumentor().instrument() # Manual span for custom operations tracer = trace.get_tracer(__name__) def process_order(order_id: str): with tracer.start_as_current_span("process_order") as span: span.set_attribute("order.id", order_id) with tracer.start_as_current_span("validate_inventory"): check_inventory(order_id) # Creates child span with tracer.start_as_current_span("charge_payment"): charge_payment(order_id) # Creates child span # Jaeger/Tempo UI shows: process_order [200ms] # ├─ validate_inventory [10ms] # └─ charge_payment [190ms] ← SLOW! this is your bottleneck

    The Golden Signals — 4 Metrics Every Service Needs

    Google SRE Book: The Four Golden Signals

    SignalWhat to measureAlert when
    🔴 LatencyTime to serve a request (p50, p95, p99)p99 > SLA threshold for 5 min
    🟡 TrafficRequests per second, messages per secondDrops to near zero (complete outage) or unexpected spike (DDoS)
    🔴 Errors% of requests returning 5xx or failingError rate > 1% for more than 5 min
    🟠 SaturationHow full is the most constrained resource (CPU%, queue depth, connection pool usage)CPU > 80% sustained, connection pool > 90% used
    Mastery — Apply It

    The System Design Scalability Checklist

    When designing any system, run through this checklist to make sure you've thought through every dimension of scale. Each item explains WHY it matters and WHEN you need it.

    Use This in Interviews and in Real Projects

    How to use this: For any system you're designing, go through each category. Ask: "Have I addressed this? Do I need to address it at this scale?" Not every system needs everything — the skill is knowing what's relevant for your specific requirements.

    1. Compute Layer Checklist

    ConcernSolutionWhen you need it
    Single server can't handle loadHorizontal scaling + load balancer>1,000 req/sec or >80% CPU sustained
    Stateful servers prevent scalingExternalize session to Redis. Make servers stateless.Before horizontal scaling — must do this first
    Uneven load distributionL7 load balancer with least-connections or IP-hashWhen some endpoints are 10x heavier than others
    Traffic spikes (flash sale, viral post)Auto-scaling (AWS ASG), queue-based load levelingWhen traffic variance is high (10x peak vs average)
    Global users with high latencyMulti-region deployment, CDN, GeoDNS routingWhen users are spread globally and latency matters

    2. Database Layer Checklist

    ConcernSolutionWhen you need it
    Read load too highRead replicas, read-through cache>10K reads/sec or DB CPU >70% from reads
    Write load too highSharding (hash partition by entity ID)>10K writes/sec — replicas don't help writes
    Database too large for RAMVertical scaling first, then shardingDataset >2x available RAM (excessive cache misses)
    Single point of failurePrimary-replica with automatic failover (AWS RDS Multi-AZ)Any production system with SLA requirement
    Slow analytical queries hurting OLTPSeparate OLAP warehouse (BigQuery, Redshift) + ETL pipelineWhen analysts run queries taking >10s on prod DB
    Schema migrations on large tablespg_repack, gh-ost, shadow tables. Never lock prod tables.Any migration on table >1M rows in prod

    3. Caching Layer Checklist

    ConcernSolutionWhen you need it
    Same DB queries repeated constantlyCache-aside with Redis, TTL-based expirySame query >10 times/sec with same result
    Cache stampede / thundering herdMutex lock on cache miss, probabilistic early expiryAny hot key with significant read load
    Cache penetration (non-existent keys)Cache null values, Bloom filter for existence checkAny public-facing API where users can query arbitrary IDs
    Cache consistency after writeCache-aside with delete-on-write, CDC-based invalidationAny time data freshness matters (<1s stale)
    Cache memory too expensiveTiered caching: local in-process + distributed RedisRedis costs significant $, cache hit rate needs improvement

    4. Asynchronous Processing Checklist

    ConcernSolutionWhen you need it
    Request takes too long to process synchronouslyAsync task queue (Celery), return 202 Accepted immediatelyAny operation taking >200ms that doesn't need real-time result
    Downstream service is slow/unreliableQueue + async processing + retry with backoffAny time you call an external API (email, SMS, payment)
    Fan-out to many subscribersKafka/SNS → multiple independent consumersWhen an event must trigger actions in 3+ different services
    Worker crashes before finishingCelery acks_late=True, idempotent tasksAny production task queue

    5. Reliability Checklist

    ConcernSolutionWhen you need it
    Dependency failure cascadesCircuit breaker (fail fast, return cached/degraded response)Any synchronous service-to-service call
    Retry storms amplify downstream failureExponential backoff + jitter on all retriesAny retry logic anywhere in the system
    Overloaded service crashes under spikeRate limiting + load shedding (drop low-priority requests)Any public-facing API, any service under variable load
    Data loss on component failureWrite-ahead logs, durable message queues, synchronous replicationAny data you cannot afford to lose
    Slow query takes down DBQuery timeout at app level (2-5s max), statement_timeout in PostgreSQLAny production DB — always

    The Interview Answer for "How Would You Scale This?"

    The Scaling Ladder — Go in Order

    When asked "how would you scale your system to 10x users?", answer in this order:

    1. Measure first — "I'd first identify the bottleneck with metrics. Is it CPU, memory, DB queries, or network?"
    2. Optimize before scaling — "Can we fix the bottleneck without adding hardware? Better indexes, query optimization, caching."
    3. Vertical scaling — "If still insufficient, scale up the bottlenecked component. Fast to implement, no code changes."
    4. Horizontal scaling (stateless) — "Add more app servers behind the load balancer. Requires stateless design (sessions in Redis)."
    5. Cache layer — "Add Redis to absorb read load from the database."
    6. Read replicas — "If DB reads are still the bottleneck, add read replicas for read-heavy queries."
    7. Async processing — "Move non-critical work to background workers (Celery + Redis/RabbitMQ)."
    8. Database sharding — "Only if write throughput is still insufficient after all above steps."
    9. Multi-region — "For global users or disaster recovery requirements."

    Distributed Systems

    Consistent Hashing — Complete Mastery

    Consistent hashing is the algorithm that makes distributed caching, Kafka partitioning, Cassandra, and DynamoDB work. Every senior engineering interview touches this. This page teaches it from zero to implementation.

    The Problem Consistent Hashing Solves

    🍕 Analogy: Splitting a Pizza Fairly When People Leave/Join

    6 people at a party. You cut a pizza into 6 slices — each person gets 1. Simple hash: person = slice_number % 6.

    2 people leave → now 4 people. Old rule: slice_number % 4. Person who got slice 3 with 6 people now gets a different slice with 4 people. Nearly everyone's slice assignment changed! Everyone has to swap slices — chaotic.

    Consistent hashing: cut a circular pizza. Each person "owns" a region of the circle. When 2 people leave, only their region redistributes to their neighbors. Everyone else keeps their region unchanged.

    Step 1: Naive Hash (The Problem)

    Python — Naive modulo hashing — breaks on rescale def naive_shard(key: str, num_shards: int) -> int: return hash(key) % num_shards # 4 shards (nodes: A, B, C, D) naive_shard("user:123", 4) # → shard 2 naive_shard("user:456", 4) # → shard 0 # ❌ Add 1 node (now 5 shards) naive_shard("user:123", 5) # → shard 3 (DIFFERENT! was 2) naive_shard("user:456", 5) # → shard 1 (DIFFERENT! was 0) # Result: ~80% of all keys move to different nodes. # In a cache: 80% cache miss rate until all data is migrated. # In a DB: must migrate terabytes while serving live traffic. Catastrophic.

    Step 2: The Hash Ring Solution

  • Create a circular hash space (the "ring")

    Hash space: 0 to 2³² - 1 (about 4 billion positions), arranged as a circle. Think of a clock face with 4 billion tick marks instead of 12.

  • Place nodes on the ring

    Hash each node's name: hash("NodeA") → 100, hash("NodeB") → 500, hash("NodeC") → 800. Each node "owns" a region of the ring (from its predecessor's position to its own position, going clockwise).

  • Place keys on the ring

    Hash each key: hash("user:123") → 350. Walk clockwise from 350 until you hit a node. First node clockwise from 350 is NodeB (at 500). So user:123 lives on NodeB.

  • Add/remove nodes — minimal disruption

    Add NodeD at position 300. Only keys between 100 and 300 (previously owned by NodeB) now move to NodeD. Everything else stays put. That's roughly 1/N of keys — not 80%.

    • Consistent Hash Ring — Key Assignment
    graph TD subgraph "Hash Ring (0 → 2³²)" A["Node A @ pos 100\nOwns: 801 → 100"] B["Node B @ pos 500\nOwns: 101 → 500"] C["Node C @ pos 800\nOwns: 501 → 800"] K1["Key user:123\nhash=350 → Node B"] K2["Key product:99\nhash=650 → Node C"] K3["Key session:xyz\nhash=50 → Node A"] end

    Step 3: Full Implementation in Python

    Python — Complete Consistent Hash Ring with Virtual Nodes import hashlib import bisect from typing import Optional class ConsistentHashRing: def __init__(self, virtual_nodes: int = 150): """ virtual_nodes: how many positions each physical node gets on the ring. More virtual nodes = better load distribution but more memory. 150-300 is typical for production. """ self.virtual_nodes = virtual_nodes self.ring: dict[int, str] = {} # position → node_name self.sorted_keys: list[int] = [] # sorted positions for binary search def _hash(self, key: str) -> int: # Use MD5 for consistent cross-platform hashing return int(hashlib.md5(key.encode()).hexdigest(), 16) def add_node(self, node_name: str): # Each physical node gets `virtual_nodes` positions on the ring for i in range(self.virtual_nodes): # Create unique virtual node name: "NodeA#0", "NodeA#1", ... "NodeA#149" virtual_key = f"{node_name}#{i}" position = self._hash(virtual_key) self.ring[position] = node_name # Insert into sorted list (bisect.insort maintains sorted order efficiently) bisect.insort(self.sorted_keys, position) def remove_node(self, node_name: str): for i in range(self.virtual_nodes): virtual_key = f"{node_name}#{i}" position = self._hash(virtual_key) if position in self.ring: del self.ring[position] self.sorted_keys.remove(position) def get_node(self, key: str) -> Optional[str]: "Find which node owns this key" if not self.ring: return None position = self._hash(key) # Binary search: find index of first position >= our hash idx = bisect.bisect_left(self.sorted_keys, position) # Wrap around: if past the last node, go to first node (the ring) idx = idx % len(self.sorted_keys) return self.ring[self.sorted_keys[idx]] def get_nodes_for_key(self, key: str, n: int) -> list[str]: "Get N distinct physical nodes (for replication — Cassandra does this)" if not self.ring: return [] position = self._hash(key) idx = bisect.bisect_left(self.sorted_keys, position) nodes = [] seen = set() for i in range(len(self.sorted_keys)): candidate = self.ring[self.sorted_keys[(idx + i) % len(self.sorted_keys)]] if candidate not in seen: nodes.append(candidate) seen.add(candidate) if len(nodes) == n: break return nodes # ── Usage Example ────────────────────────────── ring = ConsistentHashRing(virtual_nodes=150) # Set up 3 cache nodes ring.add_node("redis-1") ring.add_node("redis-2") ring.add_node("redis-3") # Route cache keys to nodes for key in ["user:123", "user:456", "product:99", "session:abc"]: node = ring.get_node(key) print(f"{key} → {node}") # Add a 4th node — only ~25% of keys reassigned (not 75%!) ring.add_node("redis-4") # For Cassandra-style replication factor=3: replicas = ring.get_nodes_for_key("user:123", n=3) print(f"user:123 replicated on: {replicas}") # → ["redis-2", "redis-4", "redis-1"] — 3 different physical nodes

    Why Virtual Nodes? The Load Balance Problem

    Without virtual nodes, load distribution is terrible. 3 nodes, 3 positions on the ring: the ring is divided into 3 arcs of random length. If NodeA's arc spans 60% of the ring, it gets 60% of all keys. Not 33%.

    With 150 virtual nodes per server: each physical server has 150 positions. The law of large numbers takes over — each server gets close to 33% of keys. Variance shrinks dramatically.
    Python — Demonstrate load balance with and without virtual nodes import hashlib from collections import Counter def test_distribution(virtual_nodes: int, num_keys: int = 100_000): ring = ConsistentHashRing(virtual_nodes=virtual_nodes) for node in ["node-A", "node-B", "node-C"]: ring.add_node(node) counts = Counter(ring.get_node(f"key:{i}") for i in range(num_keys)) for node, count in sorted(counts.items()): pct = count / num_keys * 100 print(f" {node}: {count:,} keys ({pct:.1f}%)") print("--- 1 virtual node (terrible) ---") test_distribution(1) # node-A: 62,341 keys (62.3%) ← way too much # node-B: 31,205 keys (31.2%) # node-C: 6,454 keys (6.5%) ← way too little print("--- 150 virtual nodes (good) ---") test_distribution(150) # node-A: 33,421 keys (33.4%) ← nearly perfect # node-B: 33,287 keys (33.3%) # node-C: 33,292 keys (33.3%)

    Where Consistent Hashing Is Used in Real Systems

    SystemHow it uses consistent hashingKey detail
    CassandraEvery row's partition key is hashed. The hash determines which node is the coordinator (primary owner). Replication factor = copy to N next nodes on ring.Token ranges visible via nodetool ring
    DynamoDBPartition key hashed to determine physical partition. Dynamo paper uses consistent hashing with virtual nodes.Hot partitions are AWS's #1 Dynamo performance problem
    Redis Cluster16,384 hash slots. Each key → CRC16(key) % 16384 → slot → node. Not pure consistent hashing but similar idea.Slot ranges assigned to nodes, resharding moves slots
    Memcached (ketama)Client-side consistent hashing. All clients independently compute same node assignment. No central coordinator needed.libmemcached uses ketama by default
    Nginxconsistent_hash $request_uri directive. Sticky routing of URLs to upstream servers with graceful node changes.Used for reverse proxy cache consistency
    KafkaDefaultPartitioner: hash(key) % num_partitions. NOT consistent hashing — adding partitions reassigns many keys. Kafka docs warn against changing partition count.Different! Use sticky partition or custom partitioner

    Interview Questions on Consistent Hashing

    Why does Cassandra use virtual nodes?
    Without virtual nodes, each node owns one contiguous arc of the ring. When a node fails, all its load goes to one neighbor — causing a cascade failure. With virtual nodes (each node owns 256 non-contiguous positions), a failed node's load is distributed across all other nodes. Also: heterogeneous hardware — a server with 2× RAM gets 2× virtual nodes and handles 2× the keys.
    What's the difference between consistent hashing and Kafka's partitioning?
    Kafka uses hash(key) % num_partitions — simple modulo. If you add a partition, most keys reassign. Consistent hashing is designed so adding/removing nodes only moves ~1/N keys. Kafka's partition count is intentionally hard to change for this reason — choose it large up front. For routing between consumers, Kafka uses consumer group rebalancing, not consistent hashing.
    How does Redis Cluster decide which node holds a key?
    CRC16(key) % 16384 gives a "hash slot" (0–16383). Each node in the cluster owns a range of hash slots. When you read/write a key, the client computes the slot, checks its routing table, and talks directly to the right node. If you're talking to the wrong node, it returns a MOVED redirect. Hash tags let you force keys to the same slot: {user:123}:profile and {user:123}:settings both use "user:123" for slot calculation, guaranteed same node.
    Distributed Systems

    Concurrency, Locking & Race Conditions

    Race conditions silently corrupt data. Overselling tickets, double-charging customers, losing likes — all caused by bad concurrency handling. This teaches every concurrency problem and its production solution.

    What Is a Race Condition?

    🏦 Analogy: Two People Withdrawing from the Same Bank Account Simultaneously

    Account balance: $100. Alice checks balance ($100, OK to withdraw $80). Bob checks balance ($100, OK to withdraw $80). Alice withdraws: $100 - $80 = $20. Bob withdraws: $100 - $80 = $20. Final balance: $20 (should be -$60, but nobody was blocked). $60 disappeared. This is a race condition — the check-then-act sequence is not atomic.

    Python — The Race Condition (broken code) def withdraw_broken(account_id: int, amount: float) -> bool: balance = db.query_one("SELECT balance FROM accounts WHERE id=%s", account_id) if balance >= amount: # ← GAP: another request can run here between check and update! db.execute("UPDATE accounts SET balance=balance-%s WHERE id=%s", amount, account_id) return True return False # If two requests run simultaneously: # Thread 1: reads balance=100, sees 100>=80, enters gap... # Thread 2: reads balance=100, sees 100>=80, updates: balance=20 # Thread 1: ...continues, updates: balance=100-80=20 (reads OLD value!) # Result: both succeed, balance=20 instead of -60 (money created from nothing)

    Solution 1: Database-Level Locking

    Pessimistic Locking — SELECT FOR UPDATE

    🔒 Analogy: Locking the bathroom door before entering

    Before checking the balance, lock the row. Other transactions trying to read or update that row BLOCK until you're done. Guarantees correctness. Cost: blocked transactions = lower throughput, potential deadlocks.

    Python — Pessimistic lock with SELECT FOR UPDATE def withdraw_pessimistic(account_id: int, amount: float) -> bool: with db.transaction(): # SELECT FOR UPDATE: locks the row for the duration of this transaction # Any other transaction trying to SELECT FOR UPDATE same row will WAIT here balance = db.query_one( "SELECT balance FROM accounts WHERE id=%s FOR UPDATE", account_id ) if balance['balance'] >= amount: db.execute( "UPDATE accounts SET balance=balance-%s WHERE id=%s", amount, account_id ) return True return False # Lock released when transaction commits or rolls back # Now: Thread 2 blocks at SELECT FOR UPDATE until Thread 1 commits. # Thread 1 commits (balance=20). Thread 2 unblocks, reads balance=20. # 20 < 80 → Thread 2 returns False. Correct! # DEADLOCK RISK: # Thread 1: LOCK account A, then tries to LOCK account B # Thread 2: LOCK account B, then tries to LOCK account A # Both wait forever! # Fix: always lock multiple rows in the same order (lock by ascending ID)
    Use pessimistic locking when: High contention on the same rows (many transactions fighting for same data). Correctness is critical (money, inventory). Lock duration is short (sub-millisecond DB operations).
    Avoid pessimistic locking when: Low contention (most of the time nobody fights for same row — wasting lock overhead). Long operations inside the lock (holding lock for seconds kills throughput). Use optimistic locking instead.

    Optimistic Locking — Version Numbers

    🗳️ Analogy: Checking if someone edited the document while you were writing

    You open a document (version 5). You make changes. Before saving, you check: is it still version 5? If yes: save + increment to version 6. If no (someone else saved version 6 while you were writing): your save fails with a conflict error. You must re-read and retry. No locks held during your writing — higher throughput. Occasional retries when conflict happens.

    SQL + Python — Optimistic locking with version column -- Schema: add version column to track modifications ALTER TABLE accounts ADD COLUMN version INTEGER DEFAULT 0; -- Transaction: read, modify, check version, update def withdraw_optimistic(account_id: int, amount: float, max_retries: int = 3) -> bool: for attempt in range(max_retries): # 1. Read WITHOUT locking row = db.query_one("SELECT balance, version FROM accounts WHERE id=%s", account_id) balance, version = row['balance'], row['version'] if balance < amount: return False # 2. Update ONLY IF version hasn't changed (optimistic check) rows_affected = db.execute(""" UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - %s, version = version + 1 WHERE id = %s AND version = %s """, amount, account_id, version) if rows_affected == 1: return True # Success — version matched, update applied # rows_affected=0 means another transaction changed version # Retry: re-read fresh data and try again time.sleep(0.001 * (2 ** attempt)) # exponential backoff return False # Max retries exceeded
    Use optimistic locking when: Conflicts are rare (most transactions don't fight for the same rows). Long operations — don't hold DB lock during HTTP calls or business logic. High read:write ratio. Works across languages/frameworks without DB-specific lock syntax.

    Atomic Database Operations — Simplest Fix

    ⚛️ The best solution: eliminate the gap

    The race condition exists because read and write are separate operations. If the database can do both atomically (check + modify in one indivisible operation), there's no gap for a race.

    SQL — Atomic operations eliminate race conditions -- ❌ BAD: Two separate operations (race condition possible) balance = SELECT balance FROM accounts WHERE id = 1 IF balance >= 80: UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 80 -- ✅ GOOD: Atomic conditional update — no race condition UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 80 WHERE id = 1 AND balance >= 80; -- Check happens atomically with update -- Returns rows_affected: 1 = success, 0 = insufficient balance -- Database row-level lock is held only for the update's duration (nanoseconds) -- ✅ GOOD: Atomic counter increment (can't go negative) UPDATE inventory SET quantity = quantity - 1 WHERE product_id = 42 AND quantity > 0; -- Redis atomic operations (no DB needed for simple counters) # INCR is atomic — safe for counters across multiple servers redis.incr("likes:post:123") # atomic increment redis.decr("inventory:sku:99") # atomic decrement # Redis SETNX (SET if Not eXists) — atomic reservation acquired = redis.setnx("lock:booking:seat-42A", "user-123") # Returns 1 if key didn't exist (lock acquired), 0 if it did (lock taken)

    Distributed Locks — Redis SETNX Pattern

    When do you need a distributed lock? When multiple servers must coordinate, and a DB lock won't work because: the operation spans multiple services, or you need a lock at the application level (not just one DB row).

    Example: Only one server should process a given cron job at a time. SELECT FOR UPDATE won't help — the lock needs to exist at the application level.
    Python — Production-grade distributed lock with Redis import redis, uuid, time r = redis.Redis(decode_responses=True) class DistributedLock: def __init__(self, key: str, timeout: int = 30): self.key = f"distlock:{key}" self.timeout = timeout self.token = str(uuid.uuid4()) # unique token — only WE can release OUR lock def acquire(self, wait: float = 10) -> bool: deadline = time.time() + wait while time.time() < deadline: # SET key value NX EX timeout — atomic: set only if not exists, with expiry result = r.set(self.key, self.token, nx=True, ex=self.timeout) if result: return True # Lock acquired time.sleep(0.05) # Poll every 50ms return False # Timed out waiting def release(self): # CRITICAL: Only release if WE own the lock (token matches) # Lua script ensures check-and-delete is atomic lua = """ if redis.call('get', KEYS[1]) == ARGV[1] then return redis.call('del', KEYS[1]) else return 0 end """ r.eval(lua, 1, self.key, self.token) def __enter__(self): if not self.acquire(): raise TimeoutError(f"Could not acquire lock: {self.key}") return self def __exit__(self, *args): self.release() # Usage — ticket booking: def book_seat(seat_id: str, user_id: str) -> bool: with DistributedLock(f"seat:{seat_id}", timeout=10): # Only ONE server at a time executes this block for seat_id seat = db.query_one("SELECT * FROM seats WHERE id=%s", seat_id) if seat['status'] != 'available': return False db.execute("UPDATE seats SET status='booked', user_id=%s WHERE id=%s", user_id, seat_id) return True # Lock automatically released on exit, even if exception thrown
    Why the token check matters: Server A acquires lock (token="abc"). A is slow. Lock expires automatically after 30s (protection against crashes). Server B acquires same lock (token="xyz"). Server A finally finishes and tries to release — but we check: does the lock still have token "abc"? No, it has "xyz". A's release is rejected. B's lock is safe. Without this, A would accidentally release B's lock.

    Common Concurrency Problems

    ProblemWhat HappensFix
    Lost UpdateTwo writes race — last writer wins, first writer's changes lostOptimistic locking (version column) or SELECT FOR UPDATE
    Double-spend / OversellCheck balance/inventory, then decrement — race in the gapAtomic conditional update: UPDATE ... WHERE quantity > 0
    Duplicate job executionTwo cron servers run same job simultaneouslyRedis distributed lock with SETNX
    Phantom readA transaction sees different rows on two reads within the same transaction (another transaction inserted rows in between)SERIALIZABLE isolation level or range locks
    DeadlockThread A locks row 1, waits for row 2. Thread B locks row 2, waits for row 1. Both wait forever.Always lock in same order (by ID ascending). DB detects and kills one transaction automatically.
    ABA problemValue changes A→B→A. Compare-and-swap thinks nothing changed because current value equals original.Use version numbers not value comparison. Version always increases.
    Databases — Deep Dive

    Indexing — Complete Mastery: Every Type, When It Hurts, How to Read EXPLAIN

    Index knowledge separates juniors from seniors. This covers every index type with real code, cardinality effects, when indexes make things WORSE, and how to diagnose slow queries with EXPLAIN ANALYZE.

    When Does an Index HURT Performance?

    This is what most tutorials never tell you. Indexes are not free. Every index you create:
    • Slows down every INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE on that table (index must be maintained)
    • Consumes disk space and RAM (buffer pool)
    • May not even be used if cardinality is too low

    A table with 20 indexes can have INSERT performance 20× slower than with no indexes. Choose carefully.
    SQL — EXPLAIN ANALYZE: Reading the Output -- Run this to see the query plan and ACTUAL execution time EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM orders WHERE user_id = 12345 AND status = 'pending' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20; /* EXPLAIN output to understand: Seq Scan on orders (cost=0.00..45231.00 rows=100 width=156) ↑ BAD: scanning all 2M rows. No index used. ↑ cost=startup_cost..total_cost (in arbitrary units) ↑ rows=estimated rows returned Index Scan using idx_orders_user_status on orders (cost=0.56..8.42 rows=20 width=156) ↑ GOOD: using an index. Index Cond: (user_id = 12345) AND (status = 'pending') Bitmap Index Scan on idx_orders_user_id (cost=0.00..100.23 rows=1000 width=0) → Bitmap Heap Scan on orders (cost=100.23..542.12 rows=1000 width=156) ↑ MEDIUM: built a bitmap of matching rows, then fetched them. ↑ Used for low-selectivity indexes or multiple indexes combined. Actual time: first_row_ms..all_rows_ms loops=1 ↑ "actual time=0.042..15.234 rows=20 loops=1" ↑ 15.234ms total for this step. Compare with estimated! Key red flags in EXPLAIN: - "Seq Scan" on a large table (usually means missing index) - "rows=1000000" estimated but "rows=5" actual (bad statistics, run ANALYZE) - "cost" much higher than neighbors (this step is the bottleneck) - "Nested Loop" with huge row estimates (can be O(n²)) - "Sort Method: external merge Disk" (sort doesn't fit in work_mem — increase it) */

    Cardinality — The Most Important Index Decision Factor

    📊 Cardinality = How Many Distinct Values a Column Has

    Indexing a column with 2 distinct values (boolean: true/false) is almost useless. If 50% of rows are true and 50% are false, an index on this column can only eliminate 50% of rows — the optimizer often decides a full table scan is faster (sequential reads are faster than random index seeks for large result sets).

    Indexing a column with millions of distinct values (email, UUID) is extremely valuable — the index can narrow from millions of rows to exactly 1.

    ColumnCardinalityIndex helps?Why
    user_id (UUID)Very high (millions of distinct values)✅ ExtremelyIndex narrows to exactly 1 row. Selectivity ~100%.
    emailVery high (unique)✅ ExtremelySame as UUID.
    created_at (timestamp)Very high✅ Excellent for rangesB+Tree walks range efficiently.
    country_codeMedium (~250 distinct)✅ Often usefulNarrows to ~0.4% of rows per country.
    status (enum: 5 values)Low (5 values)⚠️ SometimesIf one status is rare (0.1% of rows), index is useful. If evenly distributed (20% each), optimizer may skip index for large result sets.
    is_deleted (boolean)Very low (2 values)❌ Usually not50% of rows match. Faster to full scan than do random heap lookups.
    is_deleted WHERE false (partial index)— (partial index)✅ YES with partialPartial index on WHERE is_deleted=false covers only 1% of rows (deleted rows excluded). Now highly selective!
    SQL — Checking cardinality and index usage -- Check cardinality of a column SELECT COUNT(DISTINCT status) AS distinct_values, COUNT(*) AS total_rows, (COUNT(DISTINCT status)::float / COUNT(*)) * 100 AS selectivity_pct FROM orders; -- Check which indexes exist on a table and their size SELECT indexname, pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(indexrelid)) AS size FROM pg_indexes JOIN pg_class ON pg_class.relname = indexname WHERE tablename = 'orders'; -- Find unused indexes (waste of write overhead) SELECT indexrelid::regclass AS index_name, relid::regclass AS table_name, idx_scan AS times_used FROM pg_stat_user_indexes WHERE idx_scan = 0 -- never used since last stats reset AND relname NOT LIKE 'pg_%'; -- Drop these! They slow writes with no benefit. -- Find missing indexes (slow sequential scans) SELECT relname AS table_name, seq_scan, seq_tup_read, idx_scan, seq_tup_read / seq_scan AS avg_rows_per_scan FROM pg_stat_user_tables WHERE seq_scan > 0 AND seq_tup_read / seq_scan > 10000 ORDER BY seq_tup_read DESC; -- High seq_tup_read = reading lots of rows per scan = probably needs an index

    Composite Index — Column Order Decision Framework

    The right column order in a composite index can be the difference between 0.1ms and 10 second queries. This is a common interview question and a common production mistake.
    SQL — How to choose composite index column order -- Table: orders (user_id, status, amount, created_at) -- Most common queries: -- Q1: WHERE user_id=123 ORDER BY created_at DESC (user's order history) -- Q2: WHERE user_id=123 AND status='pending' (user's pending orders) -- Q3: WHERE status='pending' ORDER BY created_at (admin: all pending orders) -- RULE 1: Equality columns first, range/sort columns last -- RULE 2: Higher cardinality first among equality columns (more selective first) -- RULE 3: The ORDER BY column should match the index's last column -- For Q1 + Q2: index on (user_id, status, created_at) CREATE INDEX idx_q1_q2 ON orders (user_id, status, created_at DESC); -- Q1: WHERE user_id=123 ORDER BY created_at DESC -- Uses prefix (user_id) ✅, skips status (OK for prefix!), sort by created_at ✅ -- → Index Scan using leftmost prefix + sorted leaf traversal -- Q2: WHERE user_id=123 AND status='pending' -- Uses (user_id, status) ✅ → very selective, fast -- Q3: WHERE status='pending' ORDER BY created_at -- Does NOT use index (status is not leftmost) ❌ -- → needs separate index: CREATE INDEX idx_q3 ON orders(status, created_at); -- The trick: one composite index cannot serve all query patterns. -- Create multiple targeted indexes based on your actual query workload. -- ═══ COVERING INDEX: Eliminate heap fetches entirely ═══ CREATE INDEX idx_covering ON orders (user_id, status) INCLUDE (id, amount, created_at); -- INCLUDE stores extra columns in the index leaf (not the key) -- Query: SELECT id, amount, created_at WHERE user_id=123 AND status='pending' -- → answered ENTIRELY from the index, never touches the main table -- → "Index Only Scan" in EXPLAIN — fastest possible read -- When to use INCLUDE vs adding to the key: -- Add to KEY: if you filter/sort by that column -- Add to INCLUDE: if you only SELECT (read) that column

    Every Index Type with Code

    Partial Index — Index Only the Rows You Actually Query

    SQL — Partial Indexes (huge wins for sparse conditions) -- Instead of indexing ALL orders, only index active ones -- If 99% of orders are 'completed', and you only query 'pending': -- ❌ Full index (1M rows indexed, 1% are 'pending') CREATE INDEX idx_bad ON orders(created_at) WHERE status = 'pending'; -- ✅ Partial index (only 10,000 rows indexed — 100× smaller!) CREATE INDEX idx_pending_orders ON orders (created_at DESC) WHERE status = 'pending'; -- This query uses it automatically: SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'pending' ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20; -- Other examples: -- Soft deletes: index only non-deleted rows CREATE INDEX idx_active_users ON users(email) WHERE is_deleted = FALSE; -- Nullable column: index only non-null values CREATE UNIQUE INDEX idx_verified_emails ON users(email) WHERE email_verified_at IS NOT NULL; -- Admin workflow: index rare statuses CREATE INDEX idx_refunds ON payments(created_at) WHERE status IN ('disputed', 'refunding');

    Expression / Functional Indexes

    SQL — Index on computed expressions -- Problem: case-insensitive search. LOWER(email) can't use a regular index. -- ❌ This does a full scan (function disables index use) SELECT * FROM users WHERE LOWER(email) = 'loki@asgard.com'; -- ✅ Create an index on the expression itself CREATE INDEX idx_email_lower ON users(LOWER(email)); -- Now this query uses the index: SELECT * FROM users WHERE LOWER(email) = 'loki@asgard.com'; -- ✅ Fast! -- Index on JSON field extraction (PostgreSQL) CREATE INDEX idx_user_tier ON users((metadata->>'tier')); SELECT * FROM users WHERE metadata->>'tier' = 'premium'; -- uses index ✅ -- Index on date extracted from timestamp (common for reporting) CREATE INDEX idx_order_date ON orders(DATE(created_at)); SELECT * FROM orders WHERE DATE(created_at) = '2024-01-15'; -- uses index ✅ -- Without this: WHERE created_at >= '2024-01-15' AND created_at < '2024-01-16' is faster -- (regular index on created_at) — prefer range queries over function indexes when possible

    GIN Index — Full-Text Search and Arrays

    SQL — GIN index for full-text search and JSONB -- Full-text search setup ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN search_vector tsvector; -- Populate the tsvector (tokenized, stemmed representation) UPDATE products SET search_vector = to_tsvector('english', coalesce(name, '') || ' ' || coalesce(description, '')); -- Create GIN index on the tsvector column CREATE INDEX idx_search ON products USING GIN(search_vector); -- Now full-text search is fast: SELECT name, ts_rank(search_vector, query) AS rank FROM products, to_tsquery('english', 'wireless & headphones') query WHERE search_vector @@ query ORDER BY rank DESC LIMIT 20; -- @@ operator: "does search_vector match this query?" -- ts_rank: relevance score (higher = better match) -- Auto-update with a trigger CREATE TRIGGER products_search_update BEFORE INSERT OR UPDATE ON products FOR EACH ROW EXECUTE FUNCTION tsvector_update_trigger(search_vector, 'pg_catalog.english', name, description); -- GIN for JSONB containment CREATE INDEX idx_metadata ON users USING GIN(metadata); SELECT * FROM users WHERE metadata @> '{"role": "admin", "verified": true}'; -- @> = "does JSON contain these key:value pairs?" — GIN makes this fast -- GIN for array containment CREATE INDEX idx_tags ON posts USING GIN(tags); SELECT * FROM posts WHERE tags @> '{postgresql, indexing}'; -- "posts whose tags array contains both 'postgresql' and 'indexing'"

    BRIN Index — Tiny Index for Correlated Data

    SQL — BRIN (Block Range INdex) for time-series -- BRIN stores min/max per block range, not per row. -- Storage: ~100× smaller than B-tree. -- Only works well when physical row order correlates with the indexed value. -- Best for: append-only time-series data (log tables, sensor readings, events) -- where created_at physically increases as you add rows. -- For an events table with 1 billion rows ordered by time: CREATE INDEX idx_events_brin ON events USING BRIN(created_at); -- Index is tiny: ~1MB instead of ~10GB for a B-tree on same column -- Works because rows added in time order: blocks 1-100 = Jan, 101-200 = Feb, etc. -- BRIN can skip most blocks for a date range query -- B-tree vs BRIN comparison for time-series: -- B-tree: 10GB index, O(log n) lookup, works for any column order -- BRIN: 10MB index, slightly slower scan, requires physical ordering -- Choose BRIN for: logs, IoT sensor data, event streams, anything INSERT-only sorted by time -- DO NOT use BRIN for: -- Tables where rows are updated frequently (physical order changes) -- Columns that don't correlate with physical insertion order (e.g., user_id on a users table)

    The Index Decision Flowchart

    Should I Create This Index?
    graph TD A["Query is slow?"] -->|"No"| B["Don't add index\n(don't optimize prematurely)"] A -->|"Yes, run EXPLAIN ANALYZE"| C{"Seq Scan on\nlarge table?"} C -->|"No - already using index"| D["Problem is elsewhere\n(query structure, stats, N+1)"] C -->|"Yes"| E{"Cardinality\nof filter column?"} E -->|"Low (<100 distinct values)"| F{"% of rows returned?"} F -->|"> 5-10%"| G["Full scan may be faster\nConsider partial index\nor denormalization"] F -->|"< 5%"| H["Index will help\nCreate it"] E -->|"High (millions distinct)"| H H --> I{"Query also\nORDER BY?"} I -->|"Yes"| J["Include sort column as\nlast column in index"] I -->|"No"| K{"SELECT only\na few columns?"} K -->|"Yes"| L["Covering index:\nCREATE INDEX ... INCLUDE(col1, col2)"] K -->|"No"| M["Standard index\nMeasure improvement"]
    Databases — Deep Dive

    Partition Keys — Choosing the Right Key for Cassandra, DynamoDB & Kafka

    The partition key is the most critical design decision in any distributed database. A wrong partition key causes hot spots, throttling, and cascading failures. This page teaches you exactly how to choose it — with production examples from Cassandra, DynamoDB, and Kafka.

    What Is a Partition Key?

    📂 Analogy: Filing Cabinet Drawer Labels

    A filing cabinet has 10 drawers (partitions). The partition key is the label on each drawer — it determines which drawer a document goes into. If all your documents are labeled "A" (because everyone's last name starts with A), drawer 1 is overflowing and drawers 2-10 are empty. That's a hot partition. The fix: use a more distributed labeling scheme (like the first two letters, or a hash of the name).

    Cassandra Partition Key Design

    In Cassandra: The partition key determines which node holds the data. All data with the same partition key lives on the same node (and its replicas). Row queries within a partition are sorted by the clustering key.
    CQL — Cassandra: Good vs Bad Partition Key -- SCENARIO: Store user messages (like WhatsApp) -- ❌ BAD: conversation_id as partition key alone CREATE TABLE messages_bad ( conversation_id UUID, message_id TIMEUUID, sender_id UUID, content TEXT, PRIMARY KEY (conversation_id, message_id) -- partition=conversation_id ); -- Problem: A group chat with 1M messages has a 1M-row partition. -- Cassandra partitions have a recommended max of ~100MB. -- 1M messages × 1KB each = 1GB partition. WAY too large. -- That node becomes a hot spot. Queries slow down. Node may crash. -- ✅ GOOD: Bucket the partition key by time period CREATE TABLE messages_good ( conversation_id UUID, bucket INT, -- e.g., YYYYMM = 202401, 202402... message_id TIMEUUID, sender_id UUID, content TEXT, PRIMARY KEY ((conversation_id, bucket), message_id) -- Composite partition key: (conversation_id, bucket) -- Each month's messages in each conversation = separate partition -- A 3-year-old conversation: 36 partitions, each manageable in size ); -- Writing a message: INSERT INTO messages_good (conversation_id, bucket, message_id, content) VALUES (conv_uuid, 202401, now(), 'Hello!'); -- Querying recent messages (most common query): SELECT * FROM messages_good WHERE conversation_id = conv_uuid AND bucket = 202408 -- app knows current month ORDER BY message_id DESC LIMIT 50; -- Loading older messages: query bucket=202407, 202406, etc. -- "Infinite scroll" = query backwards through monthly buckets
    CQL — More Cassandra partition key patterns -- Pattern: User activity feed (like Instagram home feed) CREATE TABLE user_feed ( user_id UUID, day DATE, -- bucket by day post_id TIMEUUID, author UUID, content TEXT, PRIMARY KEY ((user_id, day), post_id) ) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (post_id DESC); -- Each user's feed per day is one partition. -- Reads: fetch today's feed → partition (user_id, today) -- Max partition size: bounded by how many posts one user sees per day (manageable) -- Pattern: Sensor/IoT time series CREATE TABLE sensor_readings ( sensor_id TEXT, hour TIMESTAMP, -- truncated to hour: '2024-01-15 14:00:00' ts TIMESTAMP, temperature FLOAT, PRIMARY KEY ((sensor_id, hour), ts) ) WITH CLUSTERING ORDER BY (ts ASC); -- One partition = one sensor, one hour of readings -- If sensor reads every second: 3600 rows/partition = perfect

    DynamoDB Partition Key Design

    DynamoDB throttling is driven entirely by partition key distribution. DynamoDB allocates throughput per partition. If you use a low-cardinality partition key (e.g., status = "pending"), all writes go to the same partition, and you get throttled even if overall write rate is low.
    Python (boto3) — DynamoDB partition key strategies import boto3 import time dynamodb = boto3.resource('dynamodb') # ═══ CASE 1: E-commerce Order Table ═══ # ❌ BAD: Using order_status as partition key # All 'pending' orders on same partition → hot spot → throttled # PK: status, SK: created_at # ✅ GOOD: Use user_id as partition key + order_id as sort key table = dynamodb.Table('Orders') table.put_item(Item={ 'user_id': 'user-123', # PK: high cardinality, evenly distributed 'order_id': 'ord-456', # SK: unique per user, sortable 'status': 'pending', 'amount': 99.99, 'created_at': '2024-01-15T10:30:00Z' }) # Query all orders for a user (very efficient — same partition): response = table.query( KeyConditionExpression='user_id = :uid', ExpressionAttributeValues={':uid': 'user-123'} ) # ═══ CASE 2: Write-heavy global counter → Add random suffix ═══ # Problem: a single 'page_views' counter item gets throttled at ~1000 writes/sec # ✅ Write Sharding: distribute writes across N shards import random NUM_SHARDS = 10 def increment_page_view(page_id: str): shard = random.randint(0, NUM_SHARDS - 1) shard_key = f"{page_id}#shard{shard}" table.update_item( Key={'pk': shard_key, 'sk': 'counter'}, UpdateExpression='ADD view_count :one', ExpressionAttributeValues={':one': 1} ) def get_total_page_views(page_id: str) -> int: # Read all shards and sum total = 0 for shard in range(NUM_SHARDS): shard_key = f"{page_id}#shard{shard}" response = table.get_item(Key={'pk': shard_key, 'sk': 'counter'}) total += response.get('Item', {}).get('view_count', 0) return total # Now writes spread across 10 DynamoDB partitions = 10× the write throughput # Cost: reads must query all 10 shards. Use for write-heavy counters only.

    Kafka Partition Keys

    Python — Kafka partition key selection from confluent_kafka import Producer producer = Producer({'bootstrap.servers': 'kafka:9092'}) # ═══ RULE: Same key → same partition → guaranteed ordering ═══ # ✅ Good: order events keyed by order_id # All events for order-123 arrive in order at the consumer producer.produce(topic='order-events', key='order-123', value='{"status": "placed"}') producer.produce(topic='order-events', key='order-123', value='{"status": "shipped"}') producer.produce(topic='order-events', key='order-123', value='{"status": "delivered"}') # Consumer sees: placed → shipped → delivered (correct order) # ❌ BAD: No key (round-robin partitioning) producer.produce(topic='order-events', key=None, value='{"status": "placed"}') # Events for same order go to different partitions → consumer sees: # shipped, placed, delivered (wrong order!) → business logic breaks # ❌ BAD: Low-cardinality key causes hot partitions producer.produce(topic='order-events', key='US', value=json.dumps(event)) # If 80% of events are from US: one partition gets 80% of traffic. # That partition's consumer is overloaded, others are idle. # ✅ GOOD: High cardinality, meaningful grouping # Choose key = the unit of ordering you care about: # user events → user_id (all user's events ordered) # order events → order_id (all order state changes ordered) # payment events → payment_id # sensor data → sensor_id + bucket (for load balancing among many sensors) # Custom partitioner for celebrity/hot-key problem: def smart_partitioner(key: bytes, all_partitions: list, available_partitions: list) -> int: if key in HOT_KEYS: # Spread hot key's messages across multiple partitions (lose ordering) import random return random.choice(available_partitions) # Normal key: consistent hashing to partition return hash(key) % len(all_partitions)

    Partition Key Anti-Patterns Table

    Anti-patternProblemFix
    Low cardinality key (status, country)All data for common values piles up on one partitionUse high-cardinality key (user_id, order_id) + add low-cardinality as sort key or GSI
    Monotonically increasing key (timestamp, auto-increment)All writes go to the "latest" partition. Previous partitions idle.Add random prefix or suffix: shard_id + timestamp. Or hash-based partitioning.
    No partition key (full table scan pattern)Every query scans all partitions — O(n) at distributed scaleDesign data access patterns first, then choose partition key to match most common query
    Too-large partition (Cassandra)Partition exceeds 100MB — queries slow, node overloadedBucket the partition key by time: (user_id, YYYYMM)
    Celebrity/hot item (same key for viral data)One partition gets millions of requests/secWrite sharding (random suffix on writes, parallel reads and aggregate)
    Databases — Decision Guide

    Which Database Should You Use? — Complete Decision Guide

    Picking the wrong database is a 2-year mistake that costs rewrites and data migrations. This page gives you the exact decision framework — for every category of database, when to use it, when not to, and which specific product to pick.

    The Master Decision Framework

    🛠️ Analogy: Choosing the right tool from a toolbox

    A hammer, a screwdriver, a drill, and a wrench all "fasten things." But using a hammer on a screw is painful and slow. Databases are the same — PostgreSQL, Cassandra, Redis, and Elasticsearch all "store data," but each is optimised for completely different access patterns. Choosing wrong means fighting the tool every day.

  • Step 1: What is your primary access pattern?

    Do you mostly look up data by a unique key (key-value)? Run complex queries with joins (relational)? Search by text (search engine)? Store time-ordered events (time-series)? Store connected relationships (graph)? The access pattern almost always determines the category.

  • Step 2: What are your scale requirements?

    How many writes/sec? How much data? Do you need multi-region? Single-region relational DBs handle far more than people think — don't over-engineer early.

  • Step 3: What consistency do you need?

    Financial transactions need strong ACID. Social feeds can tolerate eventual consistency. This narrows options dramatically.

  • Step 4: What does your team know?

    An unfamiliar database in production is a 3 AM incident waiting to happen. PostgreSQL expertise is far more valuable than theoretically-optimal tech your team can't operate.

    • Category 1: Relational (SQL) — Use as Your Default

    PostgreSQL — Default choice for most systems

    Strengths: Full SQL + JSONB (document queries inside relational), full-text search with GIN, PostGIS for geospatial, excellent MVCC, massive extensions ecosystem (TimescaleDB, pgvector, Citus).

    Sweet spot: Any structured business data — users, orders, products, payments. Handles 100K+ writes/sec with good schema design. Works for 90% of use cases.

    Real users: Instagram, GitHub, Shopify, Notion — all started on PostgreSQL and many still use it at massive scale.

    MySQL — When team has deep expertise

    Strengths: Extremely battle-tested, great replication ecosystem, Vitess for horizontal scaling.

    Use when: Team has deep MySQL expertise, migrating from existing MySQL stack, or using Vitess for horizontal scale (YouTube uses this).

    Avoid for new projects: PostgreSQL has surpassed MySQL in almost every benchmark and feature set. Choose PostgreSQL unless you have a specific reason.

    When to use SQL (the default): You have relationships between entities. You need JOINs. You need ACID transactions. Your schema is mostly stable. You need complex reporting queries. Start here and only move away when you have a specific, measurable reason to.

    Category 2: Document Databases

    MongoDB

    Best for: Product catalogues (products have wildly different attributes), CMS content, user-generated content with variable structure, IoT device configurations.

    Strengths: Flexible schema. Horizontal scaling via sharding built-in. Arrays and nested objects are first-class citizens.

    Gotchas: No JOINs — must denormalise or embed. Complex queries require careful schema design. Easy to create schemas that become unmaintainable.

    Firebase Firestore
