    Best for: Mobile apps, real-time collaborative apps, games with live state.

    Strengths: Real-time sync to clients without polling. Offline support. Auto-scales. Zero ops.

    Gotchas: Limited querying (no JOINs, limited compound queries). Pricing explodes at scale. Vendor lock-in to Firebase ecosystem.

    The MongoDB trap: Many teams choose MongoDB for "flexibility" early, then fight the lack of joins and transactions as the app grows. PostgreSQL with JSONB columns gives you 80% of MongoDB's flexibility with 100% of SQL's power. Only choose MongoDB when you genuinely have highly variable document structures at true scale.

    Category 3: Wide-Column (NoSQL) — For Massive Write Scale

    DatabaseBest ForAvoid When
    CassandraWrite-heavy workloads (millions/sec), multi-region with local writes, time-series with known partition key, never-go-down requirement. Used by Netflix for watch history (100K+ writes/sec globally)Complex queries needed, JOINs, ad-hoc analytics, small teams without Cassandra expertise
    DynamoDBAWS-native apps, serverless workloads, simple key-value access, predictable access patterns. Pay per request, auto-scalesComplex queries, non-AWS shops, when you don't know access patterns upfront (partition key design is hard)
    HBaseHadoop/big-data ecosystems already in HDFS, sorted access by row key, massive datasetsAny new project — too much operational complexity. Use Cassandra instead

    Category 4: Key-Value Stores — For Caching and Sessions

    Redis ← 95% of the time, use this

    Use for: Caching (most common), session storage, rate limiting counters, leaderboards (sorted sets), distributed locks (SETNX), pub/sub, job queues (Celery backend), real-time analytics.

    Key property: In-memory = microsecond latency. Persistent with RDB/AOF. Cluster mode for scale-out. Rich data structures go far beyond simple key-value.

    Not for: Primary storage (data loss risk, limited by RAM size), complex queries.

    Memcached ← Only when you need multithreading

    Use for: Pure caching where Redis features aren't needed. Multi-threaded (Redis is single-threaded per shard). Slightly higher throughput for simple get/set under high core-count CPUs.

    Avoid: Anything that needs data structures, persistence, pub/sub, or Lua scripting. Redis is almost always the better choice. The only time Memcached wins is if you're CPU-bottlenecked on a very high core-count machine doing only simple get/set.

    Category 5: Search Engines — For Full-Text and Faceted Search

    ProductBest ForTrade-off
    ElasticsearchFull-text search (typo-tolerant, relevance-scored), log aggregation (ELK stack), faceted search with filters, analytics on text data at scaleNear-real-time (1s refresh). Operationally complex. Expensive at scale. Never use as primary DB — always sync from PostgreSQL via CDC.
    OpenSearchAWS-managed Elasticsearch fork. Same features, managed serviceAWS vendor-specific. Good if you want managed ES on AWS.
    Typesense / MeilisearchProduct search on small-medium datasets. Developer-friendly APIs. Typo-tolerant out of the box. Much simpler to operate than Elasticsearch.Less scalable than Elasticsearch for massive datasets or complex aggregations.

    Category 6: Time-Series Databases — For Metrics and Sensor Data

    DatabaseBest ForKey Feature
    InfluxDBApplication metrics, IoT sensors, custom time-series. Purpose-built.Automatic time-based downsampling. Built-in retention policies. InfluxQL/Flux query language.
    TimescaleDBPostgreSQL users who need time-series scale. Full SQL + time-series performance.PostgreSQL extension. Automatic partitioning by time (hypertables). Continuous aggregates. Best choice if team knows Postgres.
    PrometheusKubernetes/cloud-native application metrics. Industry standard for infrastructure monitoring.Pull-based scraping. PromQL. Pairs with Grafana. Not for long-term storage (use Thanos/VictoriaMetrics for retention).
    ClickHouseOLAP analytics on time-series data at massive scale. Real-time ingest + fast analytical queries.Columnar storage. Fastest analytical query engine. Real-time ingestion at millions/sec. Used by Cloudflare, Uber, ByteDance for analytics.
    When to use time-series: You're storing metrics (CPU%, request latency, temperature readings). The timestamp is always in the WHERE clause. You need downsampling/aggregation over time windows. Regular PostgreSQL struggles because time-series creates append-heavy workloads that balloon table sizes — TimescaleDB solves this while keeping SQL.

    Category 7: Graph Databases — For Deeply Connected Data

    Neo4j

    Use for: Social network features ("friends of friends who also bought X"), fraud detection (transaction graphs reveal anomalies), recommendation engines, knowledge graphs, access control trees.

    Cypher query example: "Find all users within 3 hops of connection from Loki who also live in Bengaluru" — two lines in Cypher, multiple recursive CTEs and self-joins in SQL.

    When to reach for it: You're writing recursive SQL CTEs to traverse relationships. JOIN depth is more than 3-4 levels. Relationship traversal is the core of your queries.

    Amazon Neptune / ArangoDB

    Neptune: AWS-managed graph DB. Supports property graph (Gremlin) and RDF/SPARQL. Choose if on AWS and need managed ops with no server management.

    ArangoDB: Multi-model — document + graph + key-value in one system. Good if you need flexibility between models without running multiple databases.

    Category 8: Object/Blob Storage — For Files

    ProductUse ForKey Property
    AWS S3Images, videos, backups, static assets, data lake files, ML training dataUnlimited storage, 11 nines durability, cheap ($0.023/GB/month), CDN-able via CloudFront, versioning, lifecycle policies
    Google Cloud StorageSame as S3, GCP ecosystemIdentical concept. Choose based on your cloud provider.
    MinIOSelf-hosted S3-compatible storage, on-prem or private cloudOpen source. S3 API compatible — code works unchanged. High performance. Good for data sovereignty requirements.
    NEVER store files in a relational database. Images, PDFs, videos in PostgreSQL = DB bloat, no CDN, no parallel downloads, massive backups. Store files in S3 or object storage. Store only the URL/key in your DB.

    Category 9: NewSQL — Global Distributed SQL

    DatabaseBest For
    Google SpannerGlobal ACID SQL writes with TrueTime. Financial systems requiring global consistency. Very expensive ($$$). Google Ads uses this.
    CockroachDBPostgreSQL-compatible, multi-region ACID, much cheaper than Spanner. Use when you've outgrown single-region PostgreSQL and need geo-distributed writes.
    PlanetScaleMySQL-compatible, Vitess underneath, branching for schema changes. Used by GitHub for massive MySQL horizontal scale.
    TiDBMySQL-compatible HTAP (OLTP + OLAP in one). When you need both operational and analytical queries on the same data without ETL pipelines.

    The Master Decision Table

    If you need...✅ Use This❌ Don't Use
    Structured data with relationships + ACIDPostgreSQLMongoDB (no real JOINs), Cassandra (no ACID)
    Full-text search with typo toleranceElasticsearch / TypesenseSQL LIKE queries (full scan), Cassandra (no text search)
    Caching, sessions, rate limiting, leaderboardsRedisPostgreSQL (too slow), Cassandra (overkill)
    Write-heavy (millions/sec), multi-region, no SPOFCassandra / DynamoDBPostgreSQL (single primary bottleneck on writes)
    Images, videos, large filesS3 / object storageAny relational DB — catastrophic performance
    Time-series metrics / sensor dataTimescaleDB / InfluxDB / ClickHousePlain PostgreSQL (OK for small scale, degrades fast)
    Graph traversal (social connections, fraud)Neo4j / NeptunePostgreSQL recursive CTEs (slow for deep graphs)
    Flexible JSON documents, variable schemaMongoDB or PostgreSQL+JSONBPlain MySQL (weak JSON support historically)
    Real-time live sync to mobile/web clientsFirebase FirestorePostgreSQL (needs WebSocket layer added)
    Global multi-region ACID writesSpanner / CockroachDBSingle-region PostgreSQL (primary is a bottleneck)
    Stream processing / event log / replayKafka + stream processorRedis (limited retention/replay), RabbitMQ (no replay)
    Vector/embedding similarity search (ML)Pinecone / pgvector / WeaviateStandard PostgreSQL (no ANN index without extension)

    How to Answer "Which Database?" in an Interview

    The 5-Question Framework — Use This Every Time

    1. Read:write ratio? Heavy reads → add cache (Redis) + read replicas. Heavy writes → Cassandra/DynamoDB or sharded SQL.
    2. Data structure? Tabular + relationships → SQL. Documents → PostgreSQL+JSONB or MongoDB. Graphs → Neo4j. Time-series → TimescaleDB.
    3. What queries will you run? "Get user by ID" → any DB. "Search products by text" → Elasticsearch. "Friends of friends" → Graph.
    4. Consistency requirement? Payment processing → ACID (PostgreSQL). Social feed → eventual OK (Cassandra).
    5. Scale? Under 1M users: PostgreSQL handles everything. 1M-100M: add Redis cache + read replicas. 100M+: evaluate Cassandra for writes, Elasticsearch for search.

    Interview tip: Always justify your choice. "I'd use PostgreSQL because we need ACID for payments and our scale is 100K users — we don't need the operational complexity of Cassandra yet." This beats confidently saying "Cassandra" with no justification.

    Low-Level Design — LLD

    LLD: Design a Parking Lot System

    The parking lot is the most common LLD interview question. It tests OOP design, class relationships, design patterns, and extensibility. This gives you a production-quality solution with every decision justified.

    What Is LLD vs HLD?

    🏗️ LLD vs HLD

    HLD: "What boxes and arrows make up the system?" — services, databases, queues, CDNs. System architecture.
    LLD: "What classes, methods, and data structures implement one component?" — OOP design, design patterns, class diagrams, method signatures.

    HLD = architecture blueprint. LLD = construction drawings for one specific room.

    Step 1: Clarify Requirements

  • Vehicle types and spot types?

    Motorcycle, Car, Bus. Spot types: Compact (motorcycle/small car), Regular (car), Large (bus). A motorcycle can park anywhere. A car can use Compact or Regular. A bus needs Large.

  • Multiple floors?

    Yes — multi-level parking garage. Each floor has spots of different types.

  • Payment model?

    Hourly rate based on vehicle type. Pay on exit. Rate = ceil(hours) × rate_per_hour.

    • Step 2: Class Diagram

    Parking Lot — Class Diagram
    classDiagram ParkingLot "1" *-- "many" Floor Floor "1" *-- "many" ParkingSpot ParkingSpot <|-- CompactSpot ParkingSpot <|-- RegularSpot ParkingSpot <|-- LargeSpot Vehicle <|-- Motorcycle Vehicle <|-- Car Vehicle <|-- Bus Ticket "1" o-- "1" ParkingSpot Ticket "1" o-- "1" Vehicle class ParkingLot{ +name: str +floors: List +park_vehicle(vehicle) Ticket +unpark_vehicle(ticket) float } class Vehicle{ +license_plate: str +vehicle_type: VehicleType +compatible_spots: List } class Ticket{ +ticket_id: str +entry_time: datetime +calculate_fee() float }

    Step 3: Full Implementation

    Python — Parking Lot LLD (complete) from enum import Enum from datetime import datetime from typing import Optional import uuid, math class SpotType(Enum): COMPACT = "compact" REGULAR = "regular" LARGE = "large" class VehicleType(Enum): MOTORCYCLE = "motorcycle" CAR = "car" BUS = "bus" class Vehicle: def __init__(self, plate: str, vtype: VehicleType): self.license_plate = plate self.vehicle_type = vtype self.compatible_spots = { VehicleType.MOTORCYCLE: [SpotType.COMPACT, SpotType.REGULAR, SpotType.LARGE], VehicleType.CAR: [SpotType.REGULAR, SpotType.LARGE], VehicleType.BUS: [SpotType.LARGE], }[vtype] class Car(Vehicle): def __init__(self, p): super().__init__(p, VehicleType.CAR) class Motorcycle(Vehicle): def __init__(self, p): super().__init__(p, VehicleType.MOTORCYCLE) class Bus(Vehicle): def __init__(self, p): super().__init__(p, VehicleType.BUS) HOURLY_RATES = {VehicleType.MOTORCYCLE: 20, VehicleType.CAR: 50, VehicleType.BUS: 100} class ParkingSpot: def __init__(self, spot_id, stype, floor): self.spot_id = spot_id self.spot_type = stype self.floor = floor self.is_occupied = False self.vehicle = None def can_fit(self, v): return (not self.is_occupied) and (self.spot_type in v.compatible_spots) def park(self, v): self.is_occupied, self.vehicle = True, v def vacate(self): self.is_occupied, self.vehicle = False, None class Ticket: def __init__(self, vehicle, spot): self.ticket_id = str(uuid.uuid4())[:8].upper() self.vehicle = vehicle self.spot = spot self.entry_time = datetime.now() self.exit_time = None def calculate_fee(self) -> float: end = self.exit_time or datetime.now() hours = math.ceil((end - self.entry_time).total_seconds() / 3600) return hours * HOURLY_RATES[self.vehicle.vehicle_type] class Floor: def __init__(self, num, spots): self.floor_number = num self.spots = spots def find_spot(self, vehicle) -> Optional[ParkingSpot]: # Prefer tightest-fitting spot (motorcycle gets compact first) for preferred_type in vehicle.compatible_spots: for spot in self.spots: if spot.spot_type == preferred_type and spot.can_fit(vehicle): return spot return None class ParkingLot: def __init__(self, name, floors): self.name = name self.floors = floors self.tickets = {} def park_vehicle(self, vehicle) -> Optional[Ticket]: for floor in self.floors: spot = floor.find_spot(vehicle) if spot: spot.park(vehicle) t = Ticket(vehicle, spot) self.tickets[t.ticket_id] = t print(f"Parked {vehicle.license_plate} → Floor {spot.floor}, Spot {spot.spot_id}. Ticket: {t.ticket_id}") return t print(f"No spot available for {vehicle.vehicle_type.value}") return None def unpark_vehicle(self, ticket_id: str) -> float: t = self.tickets.pop(ticket_id, None) if not t: raise ValueError(f"Invalid ticket") t.exit_time = datetime.now() fee = t.calculate_fee() t.spot.vacate() print(f"{t.vehicle.license_plate} exited. Fee: Rs.{fee:.0f}") return fee

    Design Patterns Used

    PatternWhereWhy
    Strategycompatible_spots per vehicle typeEach vehicle type has different spot-compatibility logic. Lets you add EV, motorcycle types without modifying existing code (Open/Closed Principle)
    FactoryCar(), Motorcycle(), Bus()Caller doesn't need to know about VehicleType enum internals — just instantiate the class they want
    Single ResponsibilityTicket calculates fee, Floor finds spots, Lot orchestratesEach class has exactly one reason to change
    Low-Level Design — LLD

    LLD: Design an Elevator System

    Tests state machines, scheduling algorithms (SCAN/LOOK), and dispatching logic. Appears at Google, Uber, and Flipkart interviews.

    Key Design Decisions

  • Scheduling Algorithm: SCAN (Elevator Algorithm)

    Go in one direction, serve all requests in that direction, then reverse. Like a disk seek algorithm. Prevents starvation (no floor waits forever). Simple and effective.

  • Two queues per elevator

    up_queue (min-heap for floors above current) and down_queue (max-heap for floors below). When going UP: pop from up_queue. When going DOWN: pop from down_queue. Switch direction when one empties.

  • Dispatcher: pick closest idle elevator

    Cost = abs(elevator.current_floor - requested_floor). Assign to elevator with minimum cost. Use SETNX if distributed.

    • Python — Elevator System with SCAN Algorithm from enum import Enum import heapq from typing import Optional class Direction(Enum): UP = "UP"; DOWN = "DOWN"; IDLE = "IDLE" class Elevator: def __init__(self, eid: int): self.id = eid self.current_floor = 1 self.direction = Direction.IDLE self.up_queue: list[int] = [] # min-heap self.down_queue: list[int] = [] # max-heap (negated values) def add_request(self, floor: int): if floor >= self.current_floor: if floor not in self.up_queue: heapq.heappush(self.up_queue, floor) else: if -floor not in self.down_queue: heapq.heappush(self.down_queue, -floor) def step(self): if self.direction == Direction.UP and self.up_queue: nxt = heapq.heappop(self.up_queue) self.move_to(nxt) if not self.up_queue: self.direction = Direction.DOWN if self.down_queue else Direction.IDLE elif self.direction == Direction.DOWN and self.down_queue: nxt = -heapq.heappop(self.down_queue) self.move_to(nxt) if not self.down_queue: self.direction = Direction.UP if self.up_queue else Direction.IDLE elif self.up_queue: self.direction = Direction.UP elif self.down_queue: self.direction = Direction.DOWN def move_to(self, floor: int): print(f" Elevator {self.id}: {self.current_floor} → {floor} [DOOR OPEN]") self.current_floor = floor def cost(self, floor: int) -> int: return abs(self.current_floor - floor) class ElevatorSystem: def __init__(self, n: int): self.elevators = [Elevator(i) for i in range(n)] def call(self, floor: int, direction: Direction): # Dispatch: pick nearest elevator best = min(self.elevators, key=lambda e: e.cost(floor)) best.add_request(floor) if best.direction == Direction.IDLE: best.direction = direction print(f"Dispatching Elevator {best.id} to floor {floor} ({direction.value})") def press(self, eid: int, floor: int): self.elevators[eid].add_request(floor) def simulate(self, steps: int = 8): for step in range(steps): print(f"\n--- Step {step+1} ---") for e in self.elevators: e.step() # Demo sys = ElevatorSystem(3) sys.call(5, Direction.UP) sys.call(12, Direction.DOWN) sys.press(0, 10) sys.simulate()
    Low-Level Design — LLD

    LLD: Library Management System

    Tests CRUD operations, entity relationships, borrowing state machines, and fine calculation. Common at Amazon and product-based company interviews.

    Python — Library Management System (complete) from enum import Enum from datetime import datetime, timedelta from typing import Optional import uuid class MembershipType(Enum): BASIC = "basic" # 3 books max, 14-day borrow PREMIUM = "premium" # 10 books max, 30-day borrow class Book: def __init__(self, isbn, title, author, genre, copies): self.isbn, self.title, self.author, self.genre = isbn, title, author, genre self.total_copies = self.available_copies = copies @property def is_available(self): return self.available_copies > 0 class Member: MAX_BOOKS = {MembershipType.BASIC: 3, MembershipType.PREMIUM: 10} LOAN_DAYS = {MembershipType.BASIC: 14, MembershipType.PREMIUM: 30} FINE_PER_DAY = 5 def __init__(self, name, email, mtype=MembershipType.BASIC): self.member_id = str(uuid.uuid4())[:8] self.name, self.email, self.membership = name, email, mtype self.borrowed: list[str] = [] self.total_fine = 0 def can_borrow(self): return len(self.borrowed) < self.MAX_BOOKS[self.membership] class BorrowRecord: def __init__(self, member_id, isbn, membership): self.record_id = str(uuid.uuid4())[:8] self.member_id = member_id self.isbn = isbn self.borrow_date = datetime.now() self.due_date = self.borrow_date + timedelta(days=Member.LOAN_DAYS[membership]) self.return_date = None def fine(self) -> float: end = self.return_date or datetime.now() if end <= self.due_date: return 0 return (end - self.due_date).days * Member.FINE_PER_DAY class Library: def __init__(self, name): self.name = name self.catalog: dict[str, Book] = {} self.members: dict[str, Member] = {} self.records: dict[str, BorrowRecord] = {} self.active: dict[str, str] = {} # "mid:isbn" → record_id def add_book(self, b: Book): self.catalog[b.isbn] = b def register(self, m: Member): self.members[m.member_id] = m print(f"Registered {m.name} (ID: {m.member_id}, {m.membership.value})") def search(self, q: str, by: str = "title") -> list[Book]: return [b for b in self.catalog.values() if q.lower() in getattr(b, by, "").lower()] def borrow(self, member_id, isbn) -> Optional[BorrowRecord]: m, b = self.members.get(member_id), self.catalog.get(isbn) if not m: print("Member not found"); return None if not b: print("Book not found"); return None if not m.can_borrow(): print(f"{m.name} at borrow limit"); return None if not b.is_available: print(f"'{b.title}' not available"); return None r = BorrowRecord(member_id, isbn, m.membership) self.records[r.record_id] = r self.active[f"{member_id}:{isbn}"] = r.record_id m.borrowed.append(isbn) b.available_copies -= 1 print(f"{m.name} borrowed '{b.title}'. Due: {r.due_date.date()}") return r def return_book(self, member_id, isbn) -> float: rid = self.active.get(f"{member_id}:{isbn}") if not rid: print("No active borrow"); return 0 r = self.records[rid] r.return_date = datetime.now() fine = r.fine() self.members[member_id].borrowed.remove(isbn) self.members[member_id].total_fine += fine self.catalog[isbn].available_copies += 1 del self.active[f"{member_id}:{isbn}"] print(f"'{self.catalog[isbn].title}' returned. Fine: Rs.{fine}") return fine # Demo lib = Library("RVCE Central Library") lib.add_book(Book("978-0-13-110362-7", "The C Programming Language", "Kernighan", "CS", 3)) lib.add_book(Book("978-0-201-63361-0", "Design Patterns", "Gang of Four", "CS", 2)) loki = Member("Rakshak S", "rakshak@rvce.edu", MembershipType.PREMIUM) lib.register(loki) r = lib.borrow(loki.member_id, "978-0-13-110362-7") lib.return_book(loki.member_id, "978-0-13-110362-7") results = lib.search("design", by="title") print(f"Search 'design': {[b.title for b in results]}")
    High-Level Design — HLD

    HLD: Design Google Maps / Location Service

    Location-aware systems (navigation, ride-hailing, food delivery, geo-fencing) need geospatial indexing, route calculation, and real-time tracking. This teaches the full architecture.

    Scale Estimation

    Estimation Users: 1B monthly active, 100M DAU Location updates: 100M x 1 update/5sec = 20M location writes/sec Map tile requests: ~58K req/sec Route requests: ~3,500 routes/sec Storage: road network graph = TB range; tile storage = PB for all zoom levels

    Geospatial Indexing — The Core Problem

    🌍 Finding "restaurants within 2km of me" without a spatial index

    You can't do WHERE distance(lat, lng, my_lat, my_lng) < 2km on every query — that's a full table scan. On 10M restaurants: seconds per query. You need a spatial index. Two main approaches:

    Geohash — Encode location as a string prefix

    📦 Geohash divides Earth into a grid, encodes each cell as a string

    Geohash "tdr1wz" = a ~100m x 100m cell in Bengaluru. Longer string = smaller, more precise cell. "tdr1" = ~40km cell. "tdr1wzk" = ~5m cell.

    Key property: nearby locations share a common prefix. "tdr1wz" and "tdr1wy" are adjacent cells. Find all locations within ~1km = search for all geohashes starting with "tdr1w" (5-char prefix = ~5km cell).

    Python — Geohash proximity search import geohash2 lat, lng = 12.9716, 77.5946 # Bengaluru gh = geohash2.encode(lat, lng, precision=6) # "tdnn59" neighbors = geohash2.neighbors(gh) # 8 surrounding cells search_cells = [gh] + neighbors # 9 cells total to search # Query: WHERE LEFT(geohash, 5) IN ('tdnn5', 'tdnn4', ...) # With index on geohash column: fast B-tree prefix lookup

    PostGIS — Full geospatial SQL

    SQL — PostGIS spatial queries CREATE EXTENSION postgis; CREATE TABLE restaurants ( id BIGSERIAL PRIMARY KEY, name TEXT, location GEOGRAPHY(Point, 4326) ); CREATE INDEX idx_location ON restaurants USING GIST(location); -- Find restaurants within 2km using spatial index SELECT name, ST_Distance(location, ST_GeogFromText('POINT(77.5946 12.9716)')) AS dist_m FROM restaurants WHERE ST_DWithin(location, ST_GeogFromText('POINT(77.5946 12.9716)'), 2000) ORDER BY dist_m LIMIT 20;

    Redis GEO — Real-time driver locations

    Python — Redis GEO for live tracking import redis r = redis.Redis(decode_responses=True) # Store driver location (lng, lat order for Redis GEO) r.geoadd("drivers", 77.5946, 12.9716, "driver:001") # Find drivers within 5km of user nearby = r.georadius("drivers", 77.5946, 12.9716, 5, "km", withcoord=True, withdist=True, sort="ASC") # Returns: [('driver:001', 0.0, (77.5946, 12.9716)), ...] # Redis GEORADIUS uses geohash internally = fast, no full scan

    Full Architecture

    Google Maps Architecture
    graph TB Client["Mobile Client"] --> AG["API Gateway + LB"] AG --> LS["Location Service\n(writes driver positions)"] & RS["Route Service\n(Dijkstra/A*)"] & TS["Tile Service\n(serve map tiles)"] LS --> RC["Redis GEO\n(real-time driver locations)\nGEOADD + GEORADIUS"] LS --> DB["PostgreSQL + PostGIS\n(historical locations, POIs, geofences)"] RS --> GS["Road Graph Store\n(custom graph engine)"] RS --> Cache["Route Cache (Redis)\n(common routes pre-computed)"] TS --> CDN["CDN (CloudFront)\n99%+ tile cache hit\nPB of pre-rendered tiles at all zoom levels"]
    High-Level Design — HLD

    HLD: Design Swiggy / Zomato — Food Delivery App

    Food delivery touches every system design concept: real-time location, matching algorithms, payments, notifications, and complex state machines. Here's the complete design.

    Order State Machine

    Order Lifecycle
    stateDiagram-v2 [*] --> PLACED : Customer places order PLACED --> CONFIRMED : Restaurant accepts PLACED --> CANCELLED : Restaurant rejects or timeout CONFIRMED --> PREPARING : Kitchen starts cooking PREPARING --> READY : Food ready for pickup READY --> PICKED_UP : Delivery partner collects PICKED_UP --> DELIVERED : Delivered to customer CONFIRMED --> CANCELLED : Customer cancels before prep starts

    Architecture

    Swiggy-like Architecture
    graph TB Apps["Customer / Restaurant / Delivery Apps"] --> AG["API Gateway + Auth"] AG --> OS["Order Service\n(state machine)"] & LS["Location Service\n(partner tracking)"] & PS["Payment Service"] & NS["Notification Service"] OS -->|"order events"| KF["Kafka\n(order-events topic)"] KF --> Match["Matching Service\n(find nearby partner)"] & Notif["Push Notifications"] & Analytics["ClickHouse\n(real-time analytics)"] LS -->|"every 5 seconds"| Redis["Redis GEO\n(live partner positions)"] Match --> Redis OS --> PG["PostgreSQL\n(orders, users, menus)"] PS --> GW["Razorpay / Stripe"]

    Partner Matching — The Key Algorithm

    Python — Delivery partner matching with Redis + atomic lock import redis, uuid r = redis.Redis(decode_responses=True) def update_location(partner_id: str, lat: float, lng: float): r.geoadd("available_partners", lng, lat, partner_id) r.setex(f"partner:status:{partner_id}", 30, "available") def assign_partner(order_id: str, restaurant_lat: float, restaurant_lng: float) -> Optional[str]: nearby = r.georadius("available_partners", restaurant_lng, restaurant_lat, 5, "km", withdist=True, sort="ASC") for partner_id, distance in nearby: if r.get(f"partner:status:{partner_id}") != "available": continue # Atomically claim the partner (SETNX = set if not exists) # Prevents two orders from claiming the same partner simultaneously claimed = r.set(f"partner:assigned:{partner_id}", order_id, nx=True, ex=600) if claimed: r.delete(f"partner:status:{partner_id}") return partner_id return None # No partner available within 5km

    Key Design Decisions

    DecisionChoiceWhy
    Real-time location storageRedis GEOMillisecond radius search. 20M location updates/sec would kill any DB. Redis GEO uses geohash internally — fast and lightweight.
    Order state persistencePostgreSQLACID required — order state must be exactly right. Can't lose or double-count orders. PostgreSQL handles this safely.
    Partner assignmentRedis SETNXAtomic compare-and-set prevents two orders claiming same partner — distributed lock without Zookeeper complexity.
    Restaurant notificationWebSocket + push fallbackRestaurant apps need instant order alerts. WebSocket for when app is open, FCM push for when app is closed.
    Analytics (ETA accuracy, order trends)Kafka → ClickHouseStream events at millions/sec. ClickHouse for sub-second analytical queries on billions of rows.
    Low-Level Design

    Design Patterns — Every Pattern You Must Know

    Design patterns are reusable solutions to recurring OOP problems. You'll be asked to apply them in every LLD interview. This covers the 12 most important patterns with Python implementations and real system examples.

    The Three Pattern Categories

    🏗️ Creational → Structural → Behavioural

    Creational: How objects are created. Structural: How objects are composed. Behavioural: How objects communicate. In LLD interviews: the interviewer won't always say "use the Observer pattern" — but when you apply it correctly and name it, that's what separates good from great answers.

    Singleton — Exactly One Instance

    💡 Real use: DB connection pool, logger, config manager, thread pool

    The whole point: one and only one instance of a class exists in the entire program. Every part of the code that asks for this object gets the SAME object, not a new copy.

    Python — Thread-safe Singleton import threading class DatabasePool: _instance = None _lock = threading.Lock() def __new__(cls): if cls._instance is None: with cls._lock: # acquire lock if cls._instance is None: # double-check after lock cls._instance = super().__new__(cls) cls._instance.connections = [] cls._instance._init_pool() return cls._instance def _init_pool(self): for _ in range(10): self.connections.append(create_db_connection()) # Every call returns the SAME pool object pool1 = DatabasePool() pool2 = DatabasePool() assert pool1 is pool2 # True — same object # Python's Borg pattern (shared state, different objects — often better): class Config: _shared_state = {} def __init__(self): self.__dict__ = Config._shared_state # When to use: DB pool, logging, cache client, configuration, thread pool # When NOT to use: when you might need multiple instances later (makes testing hard)

    Factory — Delegate Object Creation

    🏭 Real use: notification sender, payment processor, storage backends

    Instead of the caller knowing which concrete class to instantiate, a factory makes the decision. The caller just says what it wants (type/parameters), and the factory returns the right object. Decouples the creator from the created.

    Python — Factory Method + Abstract Factory from abc import ABC, abstractmethod # Factory Method Pattern class Notification(ABC): @abstractmethod def send(self, to: str, msg: str): ... class EmailNotification(Notification): def send(self, to, msg): print(f"Email to {to}: {msg}") class SMSNotification(Notification): def send(self, to, msg): print(f"SMS to {to}: {msg}") class PushNotification(Notification): def send(self, to, msg): print(f"Push to {to}: {msg}") class NotificationFactory: _registry = { "email": EmailNotification, "sms": SMSNotification, "push": PushNotification, } @classmethod def create(cls, ntype: str) -> Notification: cls_ref = cls._registry.get(ntype) if not cls_ref: raise ValueError(f"Unknown notification type: {ntype}") return cls_ref() @classmethod def register(cls, ntype: str, cls_ref): # Open/Closed: add new types without modifying factory cls._registry[ntype] = cls_ref # Caller doesn't know or care about EmailNotification, SMSNotification etc. notif = NotificationFactory.create("email") notif.send("user@example.com", "Your order shipped") # Add WhatsApp support without changing factory code: class WhatsAppNotification(Notification): def send(self, to, msg): print(f"WhatsApp to {to}: {msg}") NotificationFactory.register("whatsapp", WhatsAppNotification)

    Observer — Publish/Subscribe

    📰 Real use: event systems, UI frameworks, Kafka conceptually, notification triggers

    One subject (publisher). Many observers (subscribers). When the subject changes state, it automatically notifies all observers. No tight coupling — the subject doesn't know who's listening.

    Python — Observer Pattern (event-driven) from abc import ABC, abstractmethod from typing import Callable class OrderService: """Publisher — emits events when order state changes""" def __init__(self): self._handlers: dict[str, list[Callable]] = {} def subscribe(self, event: str, handler: Callable): self._handlers.setdefault(event, []).append(handler) def emit(self, event: str, **data): for h in self._handlers.get(event, []): h(**data) def place_order(self, order_id: str, user_id: str, total: float): # Business logic... db.save_order(order_id, user_id, total) # Notify ALL subscribers — service doesn't need to know who they are self.emit("order.placed", order_id=order_id, user_id=user_id, total=total) # Different services subscribe independently order_svc = OrderService() # Email service subscribes order_svc.subscribe("order.placed", lambda **e: email.send(e["user_id"], "Order confirmed!")) # Analytics subscribes order_svc.subscribe("order.placed", lambda **e: analytics.track("order", e["total"])) # Inventory subscribes order_svc.subscribe("order.placed", lambda **e: inventory.reserve(e["order_id"])) # Place order — all 3 handlers fire automatically order_svc.place_order("ord-123", "user-456", 99.99) # This is EXACTLY how event-driven microservices work — Kafka is this pattern at scale

    Strategy — Swap Algorithms at Runtime

    🔀 Real use: sorting, compression, payment methods, routing algorithms

    Define a family of algorithms, encapsulate each one, make them interchangeable. The caller doesn't change — just swap the strategy object. Used heavily in LLD: parking lot (vehicle-spot matching), file compression, payment processing.

    Python — Strategy Pattern from abc import ABC, abstractmethod class SortStrategy(ABC): @abstractmethod def sort(self, data: list) -> list: ... class QuickSort(SortStrategy): def sort(self, data): return sorted(data) # simplified class MergeSort(SortStrategy): def sort(self, data): return sorted(data, key=lambda x: x) class DataProcessor: def __init__(self, strategy: SortStrategy): self.strategy = strategy def set_strategy(self, strategy: SortStrategy): self.strategy = strategy # swap at runtime def process(self, data: list) -> list: return self.strategy.sort(data) processor = DataProcessor(QuickSort()) processor.process([3, 1, 4, 1, 5]) processor.set_strategy(MergeSort()) # swap — no other code changes processor.process([3, 1, 4, 1, 5]) # Payment strategy — real world example: class PaymentStrategy(ABC): @abstractmethod def pay(self, amount: float): ... class CreditCard(PaymentStrategy): def pay(self, amt): print(f"Paying Rs.{amt} via Credit Card") class UPI(PaymentStrategy): def pay(self, amt): print(f"Paying Rs.{amt} via UPI") class Checkout: def __init__(self, payment: PaymentStrategy): self.payment = payment def complete(self, amount): self.payment.pay(amount) Checkout(UPI()).complete(999) # "Paying Rs.999 via UPI" Checkout(CreditCard()).complete(999) # "Paying Rs.999 via Credit Card"

    Decorator — Add Behaviour Without Subclassing

    🎂 Real use: logging, auth middleware, caching, retry logic, compression

    Wrap an object to add behaviour without changing its class or subclassing it. Stack multiple decorators. Python's @decorator syntax is literally this pattern.

    Python — Decorator Pattern (function + class version) from abc import ABC, abstractmethod import functools, time # Class-based Decorator (beverage pricing example) class Beverage(ABC): @abstractmethod def cost(self) -> float: ... @abstractmethod def description(self) -> str: ... class Coffee(Beverage): def cost(self): return 50 def description(self): return "Coffee" class MilkDecorator(Beverage): def __init__(self, beverage: Beverage): self._b = beverage def cost(self): return self._b.cost() + 15 def description(self): return self._b.description() + ", Milk" class SugarDecorator(Beverage): def __init__(self, beverage: Beverage): self._b = beverage def cost(self): return self._b.cost() + 5 def description(self): return self._b.description() + ", Sugar" order = SugarDecorator(MilkDecorator(Coffee())) print(order.description()) # Coffee, Milk, Sugar print(order.cost()) # 70 # Function-based decorator (used in production code daily) def retry(times=3, delay=1): def decorator(func): @functools.wraps(func) def wrapper(*args, **kwargs): for attempt in range(times): try: return func(*args, **kwargs) except Exception as e: if attempt == times - 1: raise time.sleep(delay * (2 ** attempt)) return wrapper return decorator @retry(times=3, delay=1) def call_payment_api(amount): # Automatically retried up to 3 times on failure return requests.post("/pay", json={"amount": amount})

    Command — Encapsulate a Request as an Object

    📋 Real use: undo/redo, task queues, macro recording, transaction rollback

    Encapsulate a request (method call) as an object. Benefits: queue commands, log them, support undo/redo, execute later. A Celery task is essentially the Command pattern — you create a task object (command) and execute it later on a worker.

    Python — Command Pattern with Undo from abc import ABC, abstractmethod class Command(ABC): @abstractmethod def execute(self): ... @abstractmethod def undo(self): ... class TextEditor: def __init__(self): self.text = "" def insert(self, s): self.text += s def delete(self, n): self.text = self.text[:-n] class InsertCommand(Command): def __init__(self, editor: TextEditor, text: str): self.editor, self.text = editor, text def execute(self): self.editor.insert(self.text) def undo(self): self.editor.delete(len(self.text)) class CommandHistory: def __init__(self): self.history: list[Command] = [] def execute(self, cmd: Command): cmd.execute() self.history.append(cmd) def undo(self): if self.history: self.history.pop().undo() editor = TextEditor() hist = CommandHistory() hist.execute(InsertCommand(editor, "Hello")) hist.execute(InsertCommand(editor, " World")) print(editor.text) # "Hello World" hist.undo() print(editor.text) # "Hello"

    All 12 Patterns — Quick Reference

    PatternCategoryProblem it SolvesLLD Interview Use
    SingletonCreationalOne instance needed globallyDB pool, logger, config
    Factory MethodCreationalDelegate object creation to subclassesNotification types, vehicle types
    Abstract FactoryCreationalCreate families of related objectsUI toolkit (Windows/Mac widgets)
    BuilderCreationalConstruct complex objects step-by-stepQuery builder, pizza order builder
    PrototypeCreationalClone existing objectsConfig templates, game entities
    AdapterStructuralMake incompatible interfaces work togetherLegacy API integration, payment gateways
    DecoratorStructuralAdd behaviour without changing the classMiddleware, retry, logging, caching
    CompositeStructuralTreat individual and groups uniformlyFile system (files + folders), menus
    FacadeStructuralSimplify complex subsystem with simple interfaceAPI gateway, compiler front-end
    ObserverBehaviouralNotify many objects when one changesEvent systems, Kafka pattern, UI events
    StrategyBehaviouralSwap algorithms/behaviours at runtimeSorting, payment, routing, compression
    CommandBehaviouralEncapsulate a request as an objectUndo/redo, task queues, transactions
    High-Level Design — HLD

    HLD: Design a Payment System (Stripe/Razorpay)

    Payment systems require perfect reliability, zero data loss, idempotency, and compliance. Every design decision here is driven by "what happens if this fails?" This is one of the hardest system design problems because money cannot be wrong.

    The Core Problem: Money Cannot Be Lost or Doubled

    💰 The Two Failure Modes That Cannot Happen

    Under-charging: User pays Rs.1000, system processes Rs.100. Company loses money.
    Double-charging: Network timeout. User's browser retries. Card charged twice. User furious.

    Every engineering decision in payment systems is about preventing these two outcomes. Idempotency is the key weapon.

    Idempotency — The Most Important Concept

    Idempotency: Calling the same operation multiple times produces the same result as calling it once. In payments: retrying a failed payment request must NOT create a second charge.
    Python — Idempotent payment endpoint import hashlib, uuid from datetime import datetime # Client generates idempotency key ONCE, sends with every retry # Key = hash(user_id + order_id + amount) — deterministic per transaction def generate_idempotency_key(user_id: str, order_id: str, amount: int) -> str: payload = f"{user_id}:{order_id}:{amount}" return hashlib.sha256(payload.encode()).hexdigest() def process_payment(idempotency_key: str, amount: int, card_token: str) -> dict: # Check if we've already processed this exact request existing = db.query_one( "SELECT result FROM payment_requests WHERE idempotency_key=%s", idempotency_key ) if existing: # Already processed — return cached result (no second charge!) return existing["result"] # First time seeing this key — process the payment # Store the key FIRST, before charging (prevents duplicate on race condition) db.execute( "INSERT INTO payment_requests (idempotency_key, status, created_at) VALUES (%s, 'processing', NOW())", idempotency_key ) try: # Charge the card via payment gateway (Stripe, Razorpay) charge_result = payment_gateway.charge(card_token, amount) result = {"status": "success", "charge_id": charge_result.id, "amount": amount} db.execute( "UPDATE payment_requests SET status='success', result=%s WHERE idempotency_key=%s", result, idempotency_key ) return result except PaymentError as e: db.execute( "UPDATE payment_requests SET status='failed', result=%s WHERE idempotency_key=%s", {"error": str(e)}, idempotency_key ) raise

    Architecture

    Payment System Architecture
    graph TB Client["Client App"] -->|"POST /payments\n+ Idempotency-Key header"| AG["API Gateway\n(rate limiting, auth)"] AG --> PS["Payment Service"] PS -->|"check idempotency key"| DB[("PostgreSQL\npayment_requests\nidempotency_keys")] PS -->|"charge card"| PGW["Payment Gateway\n(Stripe / Razorpay)"] PGW -->|"webhook: charge.succeeded"| WH["Webhook Handler"] WH --> KF["Kafka\n(payment-events)"] KF --> OS["Order Service\n(confirm order)"] & NS["Notification\n(send receipt)"] & AN["Analytics\n(revenue tracking)"] PS --> WL["Wallet Service\n(refunds, credits)"] PS --> FR["Fraud Detection\n(ML scoring, velocity checks)"] style DB fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#d97757 style PGW fill:#fdfaf5,stroke:#6a9bcc

    Handling the Webhook Reliability Problem

    The Webhook Problem: Stripe/Razorpay sends a webhook "payment succeeded" to your server. Your server crashes before processing it. Stripe retries. Now you receive the webhook twice. Do you fulfill the order twice?
    Python — Idempotent webhook handler import hmac, hashlib def handle_stripe_webhook(payload: bytes, signature: str) -> dict: # 1. Verify the webhook is actually from Stripe (not spoofed) expected = hmac.new(STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET.encode(), payload, hashlib.sha256).hexdigest() if not hmac.compare_digest(expected, signature): raise ValueError("Invalid webhook signature") event = json.loads(payload) event_id = event["id"] # e.g. "evt_1abc2def" # 2. Idempotency: has this event been processed before? if redis.get(f"webhook_processed:{event_id}"): return {"status": "already_processed"} # 3. Process the event if event["type"] == "payment_intent.succeeded": order_id = event["data"]["object"]["metadata"]["order_id"] db.execute("UPDATE orders SET status='paid' WHERE id=%s", order_id) kafka.produce("payment-events", {"type": "payment.completed", "order_id": order_id}) # 4. Mark as processed (with 24hr TTL — Stripe retries within 24hr) redis.setex(f"webhook_processed:{event_id}", 86400, "1") return {"status": "processed"}

    Payment System Schema

    SQL — Payment tables with audit trail CREATE TABLE payments ( id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(), order_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id), user_id BIGINT NOT NULL, amount_paise BIGINT NOT NULL, -- Store in smallest unit (paise, cents). NEVER float. currency CHAR(3) DEFAULT 'INR', status VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL, -- pending, processing, succeeded, failed, refunded gateway VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL, -- razorpay, stripe, paytm gateway_txn_id VARCHAR(100) UNIQUE, -- gateway's charge ID idempotency_key VARCHAR(64) UNIQUE NOT NULL, failure_reason TEXT, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW() ); -- CRITICAL: Store money as integers (paise/cents), NEVER as FLOAT -- FLOAT arithmetic: 0.1 + 0.2 = 0.30000000000000004 in floating point -- Money in paise: 100 + 200 = 300 paise = Rs.3.00 (exact) -- Audit log: every state change is recorded (legal requirement) CREATE TABLE payment_events ( id BIGSERIAL PRIMARY KEY, payment_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES payments(id), event_type VARCHAR(50), -- created, charged, refunded, disputed payload JSONB, -- full gateway response stored for debugging created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW() );

    Key Interview Points for Payment Systems

    TopicAnswer
    Why store money as integer (paise/cents)?Floating point cannot exactly represent 0.1. Arithmetic errors accumulate. At Swiggy scale: even Rs.0.01 error × 10M orders/day = Rs.100,000 discrepancy. Use integer paise and divide by 100 only for display.
    How to prevent double-charge?Client generates idempotency key (hash of user+order+amount). Server checks key before charging. Returns cached result for duplicate keys. Stripe does exactly this.
    What if payment service crashes mid-charge?Webhook from gateway confirms success/failure. Process webhook idempotently. Use outbox pattern to guarantee at-least-once webhook processing.
    How to handle refunds?Refunds are separate transactions referencing original payment_id. Webhook confirms refund completion. Update order status to refunded. Never modify original payment row.
    How to detect fraud?Rule engine (velocity checks: 5 failed attempts in 1 hour → block). ML model scoring (unusual location, amount, time). Check against deny list. Decision made pre-charge in <100ms.
    High-Level Design — HLD

    HLD: Design Google Drive / Dropbox

    File storage and sync is deceptively complex: chunked uploads, deduplication, conflict resolution, delta sync, and versioning. This teaches the complete design.

    Estimation

    Estimation — Dropbox at Scale Users: 500M registered, 100M DAU Storage per user: avg 5GB used = 500M x 5GB = 2.5 PB total File uploads: 100M users x 1 upload/day = ~1,160 uploads/sec File reads: 10:1 read:write = 11,600 reads/sec Avg file size: 1MB (mix of docs and photos) Upload bandwidth: 1,160 x 1MB = 1.16 GB/sec

    The Core Problems

  • Problem 1: Large file uploads

    A 4GB video cannot be uploaded as a single HTTP request — it times out, fails on network blips, wastes bandwidth if retried from scratch. Solution: chunk the file, upload each chunk independently, reassemble on the server.

  • Problem 2: Deduplication

    If 10,000 users upload the same 1GB video (say, a popular movie), storing 10,000 copies = 10TB wasted. Dropbox stores it once. If the content hash matches: store a reference, not another copy. This saves enormous storage.

  • Problem 3: Delta sync (only sync what changed)

    You edit one sentence in a 100MB Word doc. Naive sync: re-upload 100MB. Smart sync: compare checksums of each chunk, only upload the 1 chunk that changed. Dropbox's defining feature.

  • Problem 4: Conflict resolution

    You edit a file offline on laptop. Colleague edits the same file on desktop. Both sync when they reconnect. Solution: keep both versions as "conflicted copy", let user merge.

    • Chunking and Deduplication — The Core Algorithm

    Python — Client-side chunking with deduplication import hashlib, os CHUNK_SIZE = 4 * 1024 * 1024 # 4MB per chunk def chunk_file(filepath: str) -> list[dict]: """Split file into 4MB chunks, compute SHA-256 of each""" chunks = [] with open(filepath, 'rb') as f: index = 0 while True: data = f.read(CHUNK_SIZE) if not data: break chunk_hash = hashlib.sha256(data).hexdigest() chunks.append({'index': index, 'hash': chunk_hash, 'data': data, 'size': len(data)}) index += 1 return chunks def smart_upload(filepath: str, file_id: str): chunks = chunk_file(filepath) chunk_hashes = [c['hash'] for c in chunks] file_hash = hashlib.sha256(''.join(chunk_hashes).encode()).hexdigest() # Step 1: Ask server which chunks it already has (dedup check) response = api.post('/upload/init', json={ 'file_id': file_id, 'file_hash': file_hash, 'chunk_hashes': chunk_hashes }) if response['file_exists']: print("File already exists — no upload needed (dedup)!") return missing_chunks = set(response['missing_chunk_hashes']) # Step 2: Only upload chunks the server doesn't already have for chunk in chunks: if chunk['hash'] in missing_chunks: api.put(f'/upload/chunk/{chunk["hash"]}', data=chunk['data']) print(f"Uploaded chunk {chunk['index']} ({chunk['size']//1024}KB)") else: print(f"Chunk {chunk['index']} already on server — skipped!") # Step 3: Tell server to assemble the file from chunks api.post('/upload/complete', json={'file_id': file_id, 'chunk_hashes': chunk_hashes})

    Architecture

    Google Drive Architecture
    graph TB Client["Desktop / Mobile Client\n(chunking, delta sync)"] --> AG["API Gateway"] AG --> MS["Metadata Service\n(file hierarchy, versions, sharing)"] & US["Upload Service\n(chunk handling, dedup)"] & SS["Sync Service\n(change notification)"] MS --> MDB[("PostgreSQL\nfiles, folders, permissions, versions")] US -->|"chunk hash exists?"| CS["Chunk Store Index\n(Redis: hash→S3 key)"] US -->|"store new chunks"| S3["S3\n(content-addressed storage)\nchunks stored by SHA-256 hash"] SS -->|"file changed event"| KF["Kafka"] KF --> NS["Notification Service\n(WebSocket push to clients)"] Client -->|"long-poll or WebSocket\nfor change notifications"| SS

    Database Schema

    SQL — Google Drive schema CREATE TABLE files ( id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(), owner_id BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id), name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL, parent_id UUID REFERENCES files(id), -- NULL = root folder is_folder BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE, size_bytes BIGINT, file_hash VARCHAR(64), -- SHA-256 of the full file mime_type VARCHAR(100), is_deleted BOOLEAN DEFAULT FALSE, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW() ); CREATE TABLE file_versions ( id UUID PRIMARY KEY DEFAULT gen_random_uuid(), file_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES files(id), version INT NOT NULL, chunk_hashes TEXT[], -- ordered array of chunk SHA-256 hashes size_bytes BIGINT, created_by BIGINT, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), UNIQUE(file_id, version) ); CREATE TABLE chunks ( hash VARCHAR(64) PRIMARY KEY, -- SHA-256 of chunk content s3_key VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL, -- where in S3 this chunk is stored size_bytes INT NOT NULL, ref_count INT DEFAULT 1 -- how many files reference this chunk -- When ref_count reaches 0: can delete from S3 (garbage collect) ); -- Sharing table (who can access what) CREATE TABLE file_shares ( file_id UUID NOT NULL REFERENCES files(id), shared_with BIGINT NOT NULL REFERENCES users(id), permission VARCHAR(10), -- 'view', 'edit', 'owner' created_at TIMESTAMPTZ DEFAULT NOW(), PRIMARY KEY(file_id, shared_with) );
    Low-Level Design — LLD

    LLD: Design a Vending Machine

    The vending machine is a classic state machine problem. It tests your ability to model state transitions, handle edge cases (insufficient funds, out of stock), and use the State pattern.

    States and Transitions

    Vending Machine State Machine
    stateDiagram-v2 [*] --> IDLE : Machine starts IDLE --> HAS_MONEY : insert_coin(amount) HAS_MONEY --> HAS_MONEY : insert_coin(more money) HAS_MONEY --> IDLE : cancel() — return coins HAS_MONEY --> DISPENSING : select_product(sufficient funds) HAS_MONEY --> HAS_MONEY : select_product(insufficient funds) DISPENSING --> IDLE : dispense complete, return change IDLE --> OUT_OF_SERVICE : maintenance_mode() OUT_OF_SERVICE --> IDLE : restock_complete()
    Python — Vending Machine with State Pattern from abc import ABC, abstractmethod from dataclasses import dataclass from typing import Optional @dataclass class Product: code: str name: str price: int # in paise quantity: int class VendingState(ABC): def __init__(self, machine: 'VendingMachine'): self.machine = machine @abstractmethod def insert_coin(self, amount: int): ... @abstractmethod def select_product(self, code: str): ... @abstractmethod def cancel(self): ... class IdleState(VendingState): def insert_coin(self, amount: int): self.machine.balance += amount print(f"Inserted Rs.{amount//100}. Balance: Rs.{self.machine.balance//100}") self.machine.set_state(self.machine.has_money_state) def select_product(self, code): print("Please insert coins first") def cancel(self): print("No transaction in progress") class HasMoneyState(VendingState): def insert_coin(self, amount: int): self.machine.balance += amount print(f"Total balance: Rs.{self.machine.balance//100}") def select_product(self, code: str): product = self.machine.inventory.get(code) if not product: print(f"Product {code} not found"); return if product.quantity == 0: print(f"'{product.name}' out of stock"); return if self.machine.balance < product.price: print(f"Insufficient: need Rs.{product.price//100}, have Rs.{self.machine.balance//100}") return # Enough money — dispense self.machine.set_state(self.machine.dispensing_state) self.machine.dispense(product) def cancel(self): print(f"Returning Rs.{self.machine.balance//100}") self.machine.balance = 0 self.machine.set_state(self.machine.idle_state) class DispensingState(VendingState): def insert_coin(self, amount): print("Please wait, dispensing...") def select_product(self, code): print("Please wait, dispensing...") def cancel(self): print("Cannot cancel — dispensing in progress") class VendingMachine: def __init__(self): self.idle_state = IdleState(self) self.has_money_state = HasMoneyState(self) self.dispensing_state = DispensingState(self) self.current_state = self.idle_state self.balance = 0 self.inventory: dict[str, Product] = {} def set_state(self, state): self.current_state = state def insert_coin(self, amount): self.current_state.insert_coin(amount) def select_product(self, code): self.current_state.select_product(code) def cancel(self): self.current_state.cancel() def add_product(self, p: Product): self.inventory[p.code] = p def dispense(self, product: Product): change = self.balance - product.price product.quantity -= 1 self.balance = 0 print(f"Dispensing '{product.name}'!") if change > 0: print(f"Change: Rs.{change//100}") self.set_state(self.idle_state) # Demo vm = VendingMachine() vm.add_product(Product("A1", "Chips", 3000, 5)) # Rs.30 vm.insert_coin(2000) vm.insert_coin(2000) vm.select_product("A1") # Dispenses chips, returns Rs.10 change
    Low-Level Design — Fundamentals

    SOLID Principles — With Real Examples

    SOLID principles are the foundation of good OOP design. They're not abstract rules — each one solves a specific real-world problem. Every LLD interviewer expects you to apply these instinctively.

    S — Single Responsibility Principle

    One class = one reason to change
    Python — SRP: Bad vs Good # ❌ BAD: UserService does too many things class UserService: def create_user(self, name, email): ... # user logic def send_welcome_email(self, email): ... # email logic def log_user_creation(self, user_id): ... # logging logic def save_to_db(self, user): ... # DB logic # Reason to change: user model changes, email provider changes, # log format changes, DB schema changes. 4 reasons = 4 violations of SRP. # ✅ GOOD: Separate responsibilities class UserRepository: # only DB operations def save(self, user): db.execute("INSERT INTO users ...", user) class EmailService: # only email operations def send_welcome(self, email): mailer.send(email, "Welcome!") class AuditLogger: # only logging def log(self, event): logger.info(event) class UserService: # only orchestration def __init__(self, repo, email_svc, logger): self.repo, self.email, self.logger = repo, email_svc, logger def create_user(self, name, email): user = User(name=name, email=email) self.repo.save(user) self.email.send_welcome(email) self.logger.log(f"User created: {email}")

    O — Open/Closed Principle

    Open for extension, closed for modification

    Adding new behaviour should not require changing existing code. Use abstraction (interfaces/abstract classes) so you can add new implementations without touching the original.

    Python — OCP with plugins/extensions from abc import ABC, abstractmethod class DiscountStrategy(ABC): @abstractmethod def apply(self, price: float) -> float: ... class NoDiscount(DiscountStrategy): def apply(self, price): return price class PercentDiscount(DiscountStrategy): def __init__(self, pct): self.pct = pct def apply(self, price): return price * (1 - self.pct / 100) class OrderProcessor: def __init__(self, discount: DiscountStrategy): self.discount = discount def total(self, price): return self.discount.apply(price) # Add new "Buy 2 Get 1 Free" discount → NEW class, no existing code changes class BuyTwoGetOneFree(DiscountStrategy): def apply(self, price): return price * 2 / 3 OrderProcessor(BuyTwoGetOneFree()).total(300) # 200

    L — Liskov Substitution Principle

    A subclass must be usable wherever the parent is used, without breaking the program
    Python — LSP violation and fix # ❌ CLASSIC VIOLATION: Square extends Rectangle class Rectangle: def set_width(self, w): self.width = w def set_height(self, h): self.height = h def area(self): return self.width * self.height class Square(Rectangle): def set_width(self, w): self.width = self.height = w # must keep sides equal! def set_height(self, h): self.width = self.height = h def test_resize(r: Rectangle): r.set_width(5); r.set_height(10) assert r.area() == 50 # Fails for Square! Square forces width=height=10, area=100 # ✅ FIX: Don't inherit, use composition or separate hierarchy class Shape(ABC): @abstractmethod def area(self) -> float: ... class Rectangle(Shape): def __init__(self, w, h): self.w, self.h = w, h def area(self): return self.w * self.h class Square(Shape): def __init__(self, s): self.s = s def area(self): return self.s ** 2

    I — Interface Segregation Principle

    Python — ISP: Many small interfaces > one fat interface # ❌ BAD: Fat interface forces empty implementations class AllInOnePrinter(ABC): @abstractmethod def print(self): ... @abstractmethod def scan(self): ... @abstractmethod def fax(self): ... # most printers can't fax! class SimplePrinter(AllInOnePrinter): def print(self): print("printing") def scan(self): raise NotImplementedError # forced to implement useless method def fax(self): raise NotImplementedError # forced to implement useless method # ✅ GOOD: Separate interfaces class Printable(ABC): @abstractmethod def print(self): ... class Scannable(ABC): @abstractmethod def scan(self): ... class SimplePrinter(Printable): # only what it can do def print(self): print("printing") class MultiFunctionPrinter(Printable, Scannable): def print(self): print("printing") def scan(self): print("scanning")

    D — Dependency Inversion Principle

    Python — DIP with dependency injection # ❌ BAD: High-level depends on low-level class OrderService: def __init__(self): self.db = MySQLDatabase() # tightly coupled — can't swap to Postgres or mock # ✅ GOOD: Both depend on abstraction class Database(ABC): @abstractmethod def save(self, data): ... class PostgresDB(Database): def save(self, data): print(f"Postgres: {data}") class InMemoryDB(Database): # for testing def save(self, data): self.store = data class OrderService: def __init__(self, db: Database): # inject the dependency self.db = db # Production: OrderService(PostgresDB()) # Tests: OrderService(InMemoryDB()) # no real DB needed

    SOLID in a Single Interview Answer

    When interviewer asks "How would you make this code more maintainable?"

    "I'd apply SOLID principles. First, Single Responsibility — split this class into separate classes for each concern so each has only one reason to change. Then Open/Closed — extract the varying behaviour (like payment method, notification type) into an interface so we can add new implementations without touching existing code. For dependencies, use Dependency Injection so this class accepts its collaborators rather than creating them — makes testing trivial. And I'd make sure any interface has only the methods each implementer actually needs — Interface Segregation."

    Databases — Practice

    SQL Joins, Subqueries and Complex Queries — Complete Guide

    SQL mastery is tested in every backend, data engineering, and SDE interview. This page covers every join type, subquery pattern, window functions, and the most asked interview queries — all with worked examples.

    The Sample Schema (used throughout this page)

    SQL — Sample Schema: E-commerce CREATE TABLE users (id, name, email, created_at, country); CREATE TABLE orders (id, user_id, total, status, created_at); CREATE TABLE products (id, name, price, category_id, stock); CREATE TABLE order_items (order_id, product_id, quantity, unit_price); CREATE TABLE categories (id, name, parent_id); -- hierarchical

    All Join Types — Visual and Code

    SQL — All joins explained -- INNER JOIN: only rows where BOTH tables match SELECT u.name, o.total FROM users u INNER JOIN orders o ON u.id = o.user_id; -- Result: only users who HAVE orders. Users with no orders excluded. -- LEFT JOIN: ALL rows from left table, matched rows from right (NULL if no match) SELECT u.name, o.total FROM users u LEFT JOIN orders o ON u.id = o.user_id; -- Result: ALL users. users with no orders show NULL for o.total. -- Use case: "Show all users and their order count (including 0 orders)" -- Find users with NO orders (anti-join pattern) SELECT u.name FROM users u LEFT JOIN orders o ON u.id = o.user_id WHERE o.id IS NULL; -- NULL in right table = no match = user has no orders -- SELF JOIN: join a table with itself (hierarchies, org charts) -- "Show category and its parent category name" SELECT c.name AS category, p.name AS parent FROM categories c LEFT JOIN categories p ON c.parent_id = p.id; -- CROSS JOIN: every combination (cartesian product) -- "All possible product-user combinations for a recommendation table" SELECT u.id, p.id FROM users u CROSS JOIN products p; -- Warning: 1000 users x 10000 products = 10M rows. Use carefully.

    Window Functions — The Power Tool

    Window functions are the most tested SQL concept in senior interviews. They compute values across a set of rows (a "window") related to the current row — without collapsing them into a single group like GROUP BY does.
    SQL — Window functions with OVER() -- ROW_NUMBER: rank within a partition -- "Find the most recent order per user" SELECT * FROM ( SELECT u.name, o.*, ROW_NUMBER() OVER(PARTITION BY o.user_id ORDER BY o.created_at DESC) AS rn FROM orders o JOIN users u ON u.id = o.user_id ) ranked WHERE rn = 1; -- RANK vs DENSE_RANK vs ROW_NUMBER -- Scores: 100, 100, 90, 80, 80 -- ROW_NUMBER: 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 (unique, arbitrary tiebreak) -- RANK: 1, 1, 3, 4, 4 (ties get same rank, next rank skips) -- DENSE_RANK: 1, 1, 2, 3, 3 (ties get same rank, no gap) -- LAG/LEAD: access previous/next row's value -- "Month-over-month revenue change" SELECT DATE_TRUNC('month', created_at) AS month, SUM(total) AS revenue, LAG(SUM(total)) OVER(ORDER BY DATE_TRUNC('month', created_at)) AS prev_month, SUM(total) - LAG(SUM(total)) OVER(ORDER BY DATE_TRUNC('month', created_at)) AS change FROM orders GROUP BY month ORDER BY month; -- Running total SELECT created_at, total, SUM(total) OVER(ORDER BY created_at) AS running_total FROM orders; -- NTILE: divide into N equal buckets (quartiles, percentiles) SELECT name, total, NTILE(4) OVER(ORDER BY total) AS quartile FROM orders; -- 1=bottom 25%, 4=top 25%

    Most Asked Interview Queries

    SQL — Top interview questions solved -- ═══ 1. Find the Nth highest salary ═══ -- Method 1: DENSE_RANK (handles ties correctly) SELECT salary FROM ( SELECT salary, DENSE_RANK() OVER(ORDER BY salary DESC) AS rnk FROM employees ) t WHERE rnk = 3; -- 3rd highest -- ═══ 2. Find duplicate rows ═══ SELECT email, COUNT(*) AS cnt FROM users GROUP BY email HAVING COUNT(*) > 1; -- Delete duplicates, keep one DELETE FROM users WHERE id NOT IN ( SELECT MIN(id) FROM users GROUP BY email ); -- ═══ 3. Users who bought EVERY product ═══ SELECT user_id FROM order_items oi JOIN orders o ON o.id = oi.order_id GROUP BY user_id HAVING COUNT(DISTINCT oi.product_id) = (SELECT COUNT(*) FROM products); -- ═══ 4. Consecutive login days ═══ -- "Find users with at least 3 consecutive login days" WITH dated AS ( SELECT user_id, login_date, login_date - ROW_NUMBER() OVER(PARTITION BY user_id ORDER BY login_date)::INT AS grp FROM logins ) SELECT user_id, MIN(login_date), MAX(login_date), COUNT(*) AS streak_days FROM dated GROUP BY user_id, grp HAVING COUNT(*) >= 3; -- ═══ 5. Top-N products per category ═══ SELECT * FROM ( SELECT p.name, p.price, c.name AS category, RANK() OVER(PARTITION BY p.category_id ORDER BY p.price DESC) AS price_rank FROM products p JOIN categories c ON c.id = p.category_id ) ranked WHERE price_rank <= 3; -- top 3 most expensive per category -- ═══ 6. Pivot: count orders by status per month ═══ SELECT DATE_TRUNC('month', created_at) AS month, COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'pending') AS pending, COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'delivered') AS delivered, COUNT(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'cancelled') AS cancelled FROM orders GROUP BY month ORDER BY month; -- ═══ 7. Recursive CTE: org hierarchy ═══ WITH RECURSIVE org_tree AS ( -- Base case: top-level managers (no manager) SELECT id, name, manager_id, 0 AS level FROM employees WHERE manager_id IS NULL UNION ALL -- Recursive case: find all reports SELECT e.id, e.name, e.manager_id, t.level + 1 FROM employees e JOIN org_tree t ON e.manager_id = t.id ) SELECT REPEAT(' ', level) || name AS org_chart, level FROM org_tree ORDER BY level, name;
    DevOps & Production

    CI/CD, Deployments and Production Engineering

    Understanding how code gets from your laptop to production is essential for senior engineers. This covers CI/CD pipelines, deployment strategies (blue-green, canary, rolling), and feature flags.

    The Full CI/CD Pipeline

    CI/CD Pipeline — Code to Production
    graph LR Dev["Developer\npushes code"] --> CI["CI Pipeline\n(GitHub Actions / Jenkins)"] CI --> B["Build\n(compile, bundle)"] B --> T["Test\n(unit + integration)"] T --> SA["Static Analysis\n(lint, SAST, coverage)"] SA --> D["Build Docker Image\npush to ECR/GCR"] D --> STG["Deploy to Staging\n(auto)"] STG --> E2E["E2E Tests\n(Selenium/Playwright)"] E2E --> GATE{Manual\nApproval?} GATE -->|"approved"| PROD["Deploy to Production\n(canary/blue-green)"] GATE -->|"rejected"| ROLLBACK["Rollback"] PROD --> MON["Monitor\n(error rate, latency)"] MON -->|"anomaly detected"| ROLLBACK

    Deployment Strategies

    Rolling Deployment — Replace Instances One at a Time

    🔄 Replace pods one at a time, always keeping some v1 alive

    You have 10 pods running v1. Replace pod 1 with v2. Wait until healthy. Replace pod 2. Continue until all 10 are v2. At any point, some pods run v1 and some v2.

    YAML — Kubernetes Rolling Deployment apiVersion: apps/v1 kind: Deployment spec: replicas: 10 strategy: type: RollingUpdate rollingUpdate: maxSurge: 2 # allow 2 extra pods during rollout maxUnavailable: 1 # max 1 pod unavailable at any time # Kubernetes handles the rolling logic automatically # Rollback: kubectl rollout undo deployment/my-app
    Best for: Stateless services. Low risk. Zero downtime. No extra infrastructure.
    Problem: During rollout, two versions run simultaneously. If v2 has a breaking API change, some requests go to v1 (old), some to v2 (new). Must be backward-compatible.

    Blue-Green Deployment — Full Switch

    🔵🟢 Two identical environments — only one is live at a time

    Blue = current production (v1). Green = new version (v2). Deploy v2 to Green completely. Run tests. Switch load balancer from Blue to Green in one atomic operation. Blue stays idle (instant rollback if needed).

    Blue-Green with AWS ALB Target Groups # v1 running on target-group-blue # Deploy v2 to target-group-green (zero traffic) # Run smoke tests against green # Switch: route 100% traffic to green aws elbv2 modify-listener \ --listener-arn arn:aws:elasticloadbalancing:... \ --default-actions Type=forward,TargetGroupArn=arn:...:target-group-green # If something wrong: switch back instantly aws elbv2 modify-listener \ --default-actions Type=forward,TargetGroupArn=arn:...:target-group-blue
    Best for: Critical services where rollback must be instant. DB migrations.
    Cost: 2× infrastructure during deployment. Switch is instant but brief downtime possible if connection draining not configured.

    Canary Deployment — Gradual Traffic Shift

    🐦 Like coal miners' canary — send a small fraction first to detect problems

    Send 1% of traffic to v2. Monitor error rates and latency. If healthy after 30 minutes: 10%. Then 25%, 50%, 100%. If anything spikes: rollback to 0% instantly.

    Nginx — Canary traffic splitting upstream backend { server v1-service:8080 weight=99; # 99% to stable v1 server v2-service:8080 weight=1; # 1% to canary v2 } # After successful monitoring, gradually adjust weights: # weight=90, weight=10 # weight=50, weight=50 # weight=0, weight=100 (rollout complete) # Kubernetes with Argo Rollouts: # steps: # - setWeight: 5 # 5% # - pause: {duration: 30m} # - setWeight: 25 # - pause: {duration: 1h} # - setWeight: 100
    Best for: High-risk changes. User-facing features. Gradual confidence building. Netflix, Google, and Uber all use canary deployments for major changes.

    Feature Flags — Decouple Deploy from Release

    🚩 Deploy to 100% of servers, enable for 1% of users — without redeploying

    Feature flags let you merge code to main and deploy it, but control who sees it via a flag in your database/config. Test with 1% of users → 10% → 100% → remove flag. Or kill it instantly without a deploy if something's wrong.

    Python — Feature Flag Implementation import hashlib class FeatureFlagService: def __init__(self): # In production: store in Redis/database, update without deploy self.flags = { "new_checkout_ui": {"enabled": True, "rollout_pct": 10}, # 10% of users "dark_mode": {"enabled": True, "rollout_pct": 100}, # everyone "ai_recommendations":{"enabled": False, "rollout_pct": 0}, # off } def is_enabled(self, flag_name: str, user_id: str) -> bool: flag = self.flags.get(flag_name) if not flag or not flag["enabled"]: return False # Consistent hashing: same user always gets same result # (user doesn't see feature appear/disappear on page refresh) h = int(hashlib.md5(f"{flag_name}:{user_id}".encode()).hexdigest(), 16) bucket = h % 100 # 0-99 return bucket < flag["rollout_pct"] ff = FeatureFlagService() # Usage in code: if ff.is_enabled("new_checkout_ui", user_id="user-123"): return render_new_checkout() else: return render_old_checkout() # Production tools: LaunchDarkly, Unleash, Flipper, AWS AppConfig
